#597. Tesla to wind down Cybertruck production in Texas next year to ramp Cybercab, Musk says

Tesla CEO Elon Musk on Friday said the electric vehicle maker will "be winding down" production of the Cybertruck at its Texas gigafactory around the middle of next year.

Musk, who hailed the Cybertruck as the future of the pickup truck segment, said in a post on X early Friday morning that the end of production at the massive facility would make room for manufacturing of its Cybercab robotaxi.

The Cybertruck is exclusively produced at the company's plant in Texas alongside the Tesla Model Y and two-seat Cybercab, which Musk has touted as a crucial product for the automaker moving forward.

Musk did not say whether the company would produce the Cybertruck at a different facility, but he later responded to a user on X who called the update an "end of an era" and said the Cybertruck was the "best vehicle Tesla has ever made."

"True, but the Cybertruck fleet will be around for a long time," Musk said.

Tesla began Cybertruck deliveries in late 2023. The company did not immediately respond to a request for comment from CNBC.

The Cybertruck and its polarizing design gained a niche following, but it did not achieve mass-market sales, despite Musk's pre-launch prediction that annual sales would eventually total 250,000.

"Trucks have been the same for a very long time," Musk said when the truck was unveiled in 2019. "Like 100 years. Trucks have been basically the same. We want to try something different."

While Tesla does not break out its sales by model or region, Cox Automotive estimated sales of the Cybertruck last year at 20,237 units in the U.S., down 48% from 38,965 in 2024.

One big buyer of the Cybertruck last year was Musk's aerospace and tech conglomerate, SpaceX, which spent $131 million buying up the angular steel trucks at Tesla's suggested retail price, according to a SpaceX financial filing.

The distinct look of the vehicle made some owners a target of rude gestures and more serious road rage, one owner told CNBC last May, after Musk became the biggest funder of Donald Trump's presidential campaign and led the so-called Department of Government Efficiency, an effort to slash the federal workforce and regulations.

Wired wrote in an October 2025 feature story, "There is likely no object that feels more emblematic of US president Donald Trump's return to the White House than the Tesla Cybertruck." The magazine interviewed owners who described being spit on and sworn at due to their choice of vehicle.

The Cybertruck's specifications also fell short of those Tesla advertised early on, and in some cases fell short of those offered by leading fuel-burning pickups. There have also been many quality and production problems associated with the vehicles.

The 2024 Tesla Cybertruck was the subject of 11 voluntary recalls, according to the National Highway Traffic Safety Administration website. Issues ranged from a "trapped" acceleration pedal risk that could cause unintended acceleration, to software defects and an inverter fault that could result in sudden loss of drive power.

#589. GTA 6 on PS5 vs. Xbox: What we know

Grand Theft Auto VI is less than two weeks away, which means it's time to sort out which console you're going to play it on at launch, if you haven't already.

The problem there is that Rockstar Games has given us precious little information to go on regarding how the game looks and runs on PS5 versus the Xbox Series consoles. The official marketing spin has been that GTA 6 "plays best" on PS5 for months now, but Rockstar had not greatly expanded on what that means...until now.

In a statement that coincided with the game becoming available for pre-load on Tuesday, Rockstar elaborated a little bit on what to expect from each console version of the game, while also sharing a new 30-second trailer. According to Rockstar, GTA6 will target 30 frames per second on every machine at launch, which is not a surprise given the level of visual fidelity on display.

Rockstar also said the PS5 Pro version will support PlayStation Spectral Super Resolution (or PSSR), a special technique the PS5 Pro uses to enhance game resolution. Beyond that, PS5 Pro users will have access to ray-traced shadows, meaning (presumably) that every shadow in the game will react realistically to light sources. No other console version of the game will support this feature, at least not at launch.

We've already seen the base PS5 version running in the big Netflix trailer back in August, but Rockstar elaborated a bit on that version, too. It'll include haptic feedback in the DualSense controller, improved 3D audio, and faster load times, though the wording on the load times bit is a little bit vague. We don't know what the point of comparison is, though one could reasonably assume they're talking about the Xbox version.

Unfortunately, Rockstar's post was still extremely light on Xbox details. The post reiterated that the game will be available via Xbox Cloud Gaming for those who like to stream games, and that the streaming build will use the Xbox Series S visual settings. That is, I believe, the first confirmation that the less powerful Series S will have its own visual settings, but Rockstar didn't say anything about what those settings actually entail.

Rockstar also did not mention the Series X version at all. On balance, the Series X is roughly as powerful as a PS5, and most multi-platform games look and run very similarly across both devices. Whether that's entirely a marketing ploy or actually holds some truth to it will become clear on Nov. 19. #587. Julia Fox fronts anti-split-the-bill campaign from Seeking

Costs are high, and a recent report found that 1 in 10 young singles actually stopped dating because of money. Perhaps for this reason, the dating platform Seeking has announced an "Anti 50/50 Campaign," fronted by actor Julia Fox.

"I've been on Seeking, my friends have been on Seeking, and I think we need to get over the idea that women should have to justify what they want out of dating," Fox said in a press release shared with Mashable.

Seeking was formally named Seeking Arrangement, which made its name as a sugar dating app for "mutually beneficial relationships." Today, however, Seeking claims on its website that transactional relationships are prohibited. Now, its tagline is, "Date People Who Make Life Better"...whatever that means.

It's clear that Seeking still has the aura of trying to level up using a partner, which may also be the ethos behind this anti-split-the-bill campaign. Anti 50/50 is a $50,000 fund to reimburse singles who've been asked to split the bill on a date (up to $50 per person).

Seeking found that 61 percent of Gen Z women have been asked to go dutch on a first date, up from 27 percent of Boomer women. But 75 percent of women apparently prefer men to take the lead on dates, like planning and paying.

"I'm all for women being independent, but that doesn't mean we have to entertain low-effort men who think splitting a check is the height of romance. If you're going to court somebody, COURT THEM," Fox continued. "That's why the Anti 50/50 Fund is so important. Seeking is about being clear on what matters to you in a relationship."

To apply for the $50, you must create a Seeking account, share details about the date, and upload a receipt. The fund will remain open until Feb. 10, 2027, or until $50,000 has been claimed.

#584. Small retailers brace for 'huge hit' this holiday season as shipping costs spike

When Jenni Shields began her small business eight years ago selling handmade hair accessories, she paid $2.93 to ship an order, or close to $4 in today's dollars when adjusted for inflation.

Last year, Shields said, an average shipping label for her products cost around $4.50. But when she took a typical batch of orders to her local U.S. Postal Service office last month in Kansas City, Kansas, she was shocked to see that it was an average of nearly $6 per package.

Shields is just one of the small- and medium-sized retailers who are struggling to keep up with rising shipping costs as the price of fuel continues to trend upward.

Small business owners such as Shields are facing a difficult holiday season. Customer demand is strong, but some retailers said their profits will be dented — if not eaten up entirely — by higher shipping and other costs.

Shields said she charges her customers $6 in shipping fees, which includes roughly $1.50 to cover her packaging supplies. The dramatic upswing this year means she's no longer breaking even on her orders and instead eating the added costs, she said.

"I am losing money at this point, and I've really taken a huge hit this year," said Shields, the owner of Evie Pearl Handmade, which sells bows, quilted headbands and other accessories. "I feel like it's just been this slow increase up [in shipping costs], and I feel horrible."

The surge in fuel prices since the U.S. and Israel attacked Iran in February has driven up the cost of shipping by sea, air, rail and road.

That means higher costs across the board, including for shipping giant Amazon, which reported an 11% increase in shipping expenses in the third quarter compared with last year, to individual retailers.

Globally, air cargo is going for about $3.37 per kilogram, up 43% from a similar period a year ago, according to transportation marketplace Freightos. Judah Levine, Freightos' head of research, said in addition to higher fuel, demand for fast shipping of hardware used to build data centers is helping to boost air freight rates, even weeks before peak last-minute holiday packages take to the skies.

For businesses, that runup is going to "squeeze their margins in the near-term," said Jason Seidl, managing director and transportation analyst at TD Cowen. "Larger companies tend not to be exposed to the spot market, and they'll have what we call dedicated deals in place."

But that doesn't help smaller companies, which are walking a delicate line of not wanting to alienate customers who have enjoyed free shipping for nearly a generation.

Todd Shelton, founder of the eponymous 23-year-old New Jersey clothing company that makes high-end men's jeans, T-shirts and button-up shirts locally, said he started charging customers for return shipping this week for the first time after absorbing the higher expenses this year. The shipping is deducted from customers' refunds.

Shelton said his costs have been rising for materials, too, such as his imported Japanese denim and shirt fabric from Portugal. The company is still profitable, but the higher costs have eaten into profit margins as they stay elevated, he's considering raising prices or charging for shipping to customers, he said.

Shields said she's considering raising her shipping fees for the first time in six years to keep up with the increases.

The holiday season is one of Shields' busiest. But she said she's seen a slowdown in sales because of shipping fees.

"I can't compete with Amazon on the free shipping. I can't do that. I can't afford that," she said. "I'm seeing so much of my percentage that's supposed to come back to me and my family being put towards the shipping costs."

With no sign of fuel prices declining significantly, Shields, who runs her business out of her spare bedroom at home, said she's not sure that small businesses will be able to emerge on the other side of the holidays.

Shippers including UPS, FedEx and USPS have all instituted holiday surcharges, with prices varying by package type and lasting through the end of the season. Though that's typical during the peak holiday period each year, those charges have continued to rise over the past few years.

But even as costs everywhere climb, consumers are still spending.

According to the National Retail Federation, retail sales through the end of the year are expected to grow 4.2%, translating to total spending of $1.01 trillion. That's compared with last year's holiday sales totaling $972 billion, which was 3.1% growth.

Some of that growth is from inflation, meaning consumers are simply paying more for the same goods.

"Customers all expect to spend more this holiday, but part of why they expect to spend more is they expect things to cost more, not because they'd like to spend more or they were hoping to get more quantity of stuff," said Aaron Cheris, the global retail practice head at Bain & Company. "So what I suspect happens is that non-discretionary categories do fine, because you buy the food you were going to need to buy anyway, but then the amount of cash that's left over for [discretionary] goods is less."

Still, Cheris said it's shaping up to be a record year for shipping costs, which is putting immense pressure on smaller retailers. That means many retailers may find themselves in financial distress in the first quarter of next year as they struggle to make it through the holiday season.

He added that larger retail companies will also have to reckon with rising costs, though they may not feel those effects until later quarters because they have shipping contracts that have already been locked in and have control over their own delivery fleets.

"If anything, it benefits the scale players over the little players, but it's kind of a little bit bad for everybody," Cheris said. "I think that is the challenge."

#576. Everything to know about Amazons extra-long Black Friday sale: Dates, daily deal drops, and more

Amazon has nailed down its holiday plans. Today, the retail giant announced that it will host Black Friday Week and Cyber Monday sales from Nov. 19 to Nov. 30 — that's 12 straight days of deals.

In a company blog post, Amazon said its holiday savings events will feature "millions of deals across popular categories and new daily themed deal drops," including "double drops" on Black Friday and Cyber Monday proper. Here's what you need to know before you start filling your cart.

Amazon's Black Friday Week sale will begin at 3:01 a.m. ET (12:01 a.m. PT) on Thursday, Nov. 19, a week before Thanksgiving Day.

This will be the fourth year in a row that Amazon starts its Black Friday sale at least a week early. (The actual holiday falls on Friday, Nov. 27 this year.)

Amazon is hardly the only major retailer that draws out its Black Friday programming. Best Buy, Target, and Walmart are also offering Black Friday deals throughout the month of November — a win for early birds who are worried about popular gifts selling out as the holiday shopping season revs up.

Amazon hasn't officially said when its 2026 Cyber Monday sale will start, but in the past, it's kicked off the day right after Black Friday. With that in mind, Mashable's Shopping team expects Amazon to make the shift into Cyber Monday mode on Saturday, Nov. 28 this year.

Yes, Amazon has already dropped a bunch of early Black Friday deals. Head to amazon.com/blackfriday and look for the offers with a red "Early Black Friday Deal" label. (If you'd rather not sift through them all yourself, check out our running list of Amazon's top early Black Friday deals so far.) On each day of its Black Friday Week and Cyber Monday sales, Amazon will run themed daily deal drops called "Today’s Big Deals." They'll involve limited-time discounts of up to 60% on popular products from brands like Balsam Hill, HydroJug, and Theragun, and they'll go live once a day except on Black Friday and Cyber Monday proper. Shoppers will get two batches of Today's Big Deals on those days: once at 3 a.m. ET, then again at 11 a.m ET.

These will likely be some of Amazon's most competitive deals of the year. However, know that its prices may not be as low as they've been on Black Fridays past. That's because myriad products have seen price increases in 2026 due to factors like higher fuel costs, tariffs, and the ongoing memory shortage, aka "RAMageddon."

The latter is the reason why Amazon raised the prices of its Kindle e-readers, Fire TV Sticks, and most Echo smart speakers in August.

Amazon's Black Friday sale won't be exclusive to members of its Prime program. This is one of the main things that separates it from other Amazon events like Prime Day and Prime Big Deal Days.

No, Amazon does not price-match competitors' deals — not on Black Friday, nor any other day of the year. If you find a better price on something you've recently purchased there, you'll have to return it to Amazon and rebuy it where it's cheaper.

Most items purchased on Amazon from Nov. 1 to Dec. 31 can be returned through Jan. 31, 2026, except for Apple products, which have a return deadline of Jan. 15, 2027.

#571. Security Flaws Across UK Government Sites Exposed ‘God-Tier’ Information

A security researcher has found more than 100 serious vulnerabilities in publicly available UK government code, putting sensitive data at risk as a result of poor cyber hygiene.

The UK government has defaulted to openly publishing code it produces with public money for around a decade because it’s transparent, and making it easier for code to be reused is cost-efficient. This includes within the Department of Health and Social Care, and the Home Office, as well as local authorities.

But funding squeezes have left departments neglecting maintenance and improvement of their technology estate. While ControlPlane security researcher Chris Nesbitt-Smith found the vulnerabilities using Claude’s Opus and Sonnet AI models, he says that he used consumer-grade models on a laptop and that many of the issues could have been caught by a routine IT health check. “None of this is the level of sophistication that general media is worried about in terms of cyber attacks,” he tells WIRED. “It’s all daft stuff.”

In the most serious case he identified, the Planning Inspectorate, which handles land-use matters across England, had failed to fully remove a login after it accidentally committed them to public repository GitHub. The fact the credentials gave such wide access—and seemingly didn’t expire—compounded with the agency’s accidental publication of them, “made it particularly dangerous for them.”

The complete Microsoft Entra ID would have given “god-tier” read and write access to all SharePoint sites and private employee OneDrive accounts, according to Nesbitt-Smith, including HR records. It also would have given access to sensitive libraries which safeguard evidence under legal hold for statutory planning inquiries and appeals, according to the security researcher who found it. “The option for ransomwaring them, for encrypting all the data, or just stealing all the data, was abundantly there,” Nesbitt-Smith says.

A spokesperson for the Planning Inspectorate told WIRED its cyber monitoring tools immediately detected Nesbitt-Smith’s activity in August and that it had already been handling this as a cyber incident when ControlPlane made contact. The agency identified and fixed this vulnerability within 20 minutes of the report and verified that no data breach occurred. “We have reviewed and strengthened our systems and processes to prevent this type of issue recurring and we are grateful for the researcher’s work and for alerting us to it,” they told WIRED.

The Planning Inspectorate issue “isn’t about open source but internal housekeeping when using public tools,” Amanda Brock, chief executive officer of nonprofit industry group OpenUK. “That data was exposed because of a failure not to follow good process,” she adds. “That’s not an open source security issue.”

In around two-thirds of the 349 public sector codebases across 33 organizations Nesbitt-Smith scanned, he said he found vulnerabilities. Several of these were found in the Department of Education’s code, including in a procurement service used by schools, in a teacher application service containing personal details, qualifications, employment history and identity documents, and England’s main register of government-funded training providers and apprenticeships. He also found a vulnerability in a Scottish Government open data portal which would have allowed any visitor to read administrative account information and to change site configuration. The organizations have since dealt with the issues.

Recently, there have been arguments for public sector departments to stop publishing source code in the open, now that AI has accelerated vulnerability discovery. The NHS reportedly ordered technology teams to transition away from open source code in May amid security concerns about the impact of AI models.

Doing this without immediately fixing the underlying issues would be the “worst case scenario,” according to Nesbitt-Smith, as it prevents researchers like him from finding them, and it’s likely that an adversary has already mirrored these repositories and so will maintain access to them. “Unless you’re going to go and fix all of the problems, if anything, you’re probably drawing more attention to it by closing it,” says Nesbitt-Smith, “because you’re like, ‘Here be dragons over this space. Don’t look here though, please.’”

#570. Anthropic forges ahead with IPO roadshow, eyes $1.4 trillion valuation

Unlike its biggest competitor, OpenAI, Anthropic doesn't appear to be backing away from its IPO.

Anthropic, the AI giant behind the Claude chatbot, is preparing for an IPO roadshow, according to an announcement from the company. An IPO roadshow is basically a series of sales presentations to potential investors in order to gauge interest and set a final share price.

Interestingly, Anthropic is looking to go public at a $1.4 trillion valuation. This is lower than investor expectations, which reportedly reached $2 trillion just two months ago.

Anthropic's decision to go forward with an IPO is in sharp contrast to the approach of its largest competitor, OpenAI, which has largely shied away from doing so anytime in the near future. Anthropic beat OpenAI to an IPO filing earlier this summer when both AI companies were still pursuing a public stock offering. Since then, OpenAI has put its IPO plans on hold, citing concerns around AI safety. #568. Where's the growth? Legacy automakers face a tougher 2027 as costs rise, market tightens

DETROIT — There's a growing concern that the global automotive industry may be stuck in neutral, if not reverse, heading into 2027.

The auto industry — from the companies to their customers — has been surprisingly resilient this decade, but there are fears that rising costs, weaker vehicle pricing and a more challenging market could threaten profits and growth going forward.

Automakers also face pressure outside the U.S. from an onslaught of Chinese carmakers, a thinner new-product pipeline after retreating from some all-electric plans, and company- and region-specific challenges.

"With investor focus increasingly shifting to 2027, the setup appears challenging," Barclays analyst Dan Levy said in an investor note last month. "Rising inflation, continued China pressure, affordability concerns in the U.S., and a flat global production outlook suggest another year of limited earnings growth."

Several Wall Street analysts have said automakers will need continued pricing strength and additional cost savings to offset inflation, higher investment spending and a potentially weaker economy just to maintain their current levels.

"It's a little bit more optimistic in the U.S. and then very pessimistic in Europe," RBC Capital Markets analyst Tom Narayan told CNBC. "As a whole, there's some negativity on '27."

Automakers from the U.S. to Europe to Asia have been facing a growing list of headwinds.

For U.S.-based legacy automakers, Ford Motor said it expects next year's results to be in line with 2026, while General Motors grew more cautious about its ability to continue growing earnings as much as it had previously expected.

"We continue to believe that 2027 will be another strong year and better than 2026, assuming customer demand remains strong, and commodity, logistics and other recent cost pressures moderate," GM CEO Mary Barra told investors Oct. 20.

GM CFO Paul Jacobson said that while it's too early to officially forecast, the company is facing additional inflationary costs that could lead to another $2 billion headwind in 2027, like it expects this year. He specifically cited costs of commodity inflation, logistics and DRAM, or dynamic random-access memory chips as challenges.

Those chips are critical components in modern vehicles and are used for everything from temporary data storage and real-time processing from infotainment systems to safety-critical systems.

GM and others have been able to offset outside pressures through cost cuts, operational efficiencies and resilient consumer demand for higher-margin vehicles. However, there's a limit to what companies can do.

"There's only so much pricing you can push to consumers," Narayan said. "They're already at pretty high pricing."

European automakers including Volkswagen, Mercedes-Benz and BMW in recent weeks reduced their 2026 earnings forecasts in response to U.S. tariffs, the growth of Chinese automakers and rising costs.

Most recently, BMW this week said accelerated restructuring measures would reduce its automotive pretax earnings margin this year by 2.5 percentage points. The company said the restructuring should support "positive effects" into 2027 and a 3% to 5% pretax profit margin for 2028. That compares with a 5.3% automotive margin last year and 3.4% during the third quarter.

Chrysler parent Stellantis, which has large operations in Europe and the U.S., is facing its own challenges, as it's in the midst of a restructuring that aims for a low-single-digit adjusted operating margin this year. It has maintained a 2026 guidance that includes a mid-single-digit percentage increase in net revenue and a low-single-digit adjusted operating margin.

In Asia, South Korean automaker Hyundai Motor, which has experienced notable growth in recent years, also cut key metrics when it reported earnings late last month. The company lowered its 2026 wholesales target to 4 million vehicles, down from 4.16 million, and cut its operating-margin target to between 5.5% and 6.5%, down from a previous goal of 6.3% to 7.3%.

Toyota Motor is an outlier for its most recent quarter and outlook, as the world's largest automaker increased its full-year operating income forecast by 500 billion Japanese yen (about $3.2 billion) on Thursday.

Toyota's results were boosted by weakness in the Japanese yen as well as strong hybrid vehicle demand in the U.S. However, the increase in operating income was its first in six quarters.

Broadly, the changes to earnings and outlooks come as production for the global industry slows.

Consulting and insights firm GlobalData expects global light-vehicle production of about 90.96 million units in 2026, down 2.1% compared with last year. It forecast a production increase of less than 1% next year. That comes on the heels of strong annual growth earlier this decade, led by China.

Cox Automotive reported the average transaction price of a new U.S. vehicle in September was more than $50,500. That was just shy of the record of $50,614 in December 2025.

Lofty vehicle prices have led to record-high and record-long financing deals, as well as concerns that customers are getting priced out of the new-vehicle market.

The typical U.S. new-vehicle payment reached $776 a month in September, the highest since December 2023, according to Cox Automotive.

Cox Automotive chief economist Jeremy Robb, discussing the third quarter for the automotive industry, rated the overall economy, buyer ability and borrowing costs as cautionary "yellow lights."

But he did have some "green lights," such as good credit availability and employment conditions, which could give some relief for automakers and consumers, many of whom need vehicles for their everyday lives.

Robb said there's a "tug-of-war" between headwinds such as rising costs, interest rates and fuel prices and tailwinds such as growth in liquid assets and savings that has helped offset some of the additional costs.

"Consumers remain under financial pressure, but the vehicle demand continues to hold, showing more resiliency in the auto industry than we've seen in other parts of the economy," Robb said during a Sept. 24 call, noting consumer sentiment remains low.

#567. Vanderbilt University was hit by a cyberattack. Heres what we know.

A Vanderbilt University professor was in the middle of class on Monday, Nov. 9, when about 40 files labeled "Read Me" popped up on her screen and a spoken message started playing. That would be creepy enough on its own. But the professor's account was one glimpse of an ongoing cyberattack that prompted Vanderbilt to tell students and staff to shut down their school-issued devices and disconnect from campus WiFi.

While Vanderbilt has restored access to some of its systems, an update posted to the university's X account on Tuesday morning stated that "Vanderbilt Information Technology continues to work with government agencies and other partners to manage the situation,” promising further updates.

Vanderbilt hasn’t said how its IT team first spotted the attack, but there are clues, namely an alleged ransom note on a Vanderbilt computer published by BleepingComputer.

"If you could not tell already, your databases are locked," a ransomware group calling itself BYOD wrote in a post claiming responsibility for the Vanderbilt cyberattack. "We have your entire estate and research data," the group added, claiming it had stolen more than 1 million Social Security numbers along with student and employee records. In a separate ransom note obtained by BleepingComputer, the group threatened to release the data unless Vanderbilt negotiated.

Whether BYOD actually has that data is still an open question. A Vanderbilt spokesperson told The Washington Post that the university hadn't verified the group's claims. If you buy something through links on our site, Mashable may earn an affiliate commission.

The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

Just two weeks before the Vanderbilt attack, on Oct. 26, employees at the University of New Mexico Health Sciences Center heard a similar message.

Several UNM Health employees told the Associated Press that a voice played over their work computers, claiming that student, employee, and patient information had been stolen. BYOD also claimed responsibility for that attack. As UNM Health responded, it diverted ambulances to other hospitals and temporarily suspended outpatient surgeries and appointments. (UNM later said its investigation found that the attack affected some staff and student devices but didn't reach its core systems or servers.)

Vanderbilt's WiFi was back by Monday evening, but getting online didn't mean students could get to everything they needed. By Monday night, Vanderbilt's IT team had cleared Brightspace, where students access course materials, and the YES student portal for use, but File storage services OneDrive and Box were still affected. Vanderbilt said classes and student services would follow their usual Tuesday schedule and told students who were having trouble finishing assignments to ask their instructors about extensions.

The disruption went beyond coursework. One person told the Nashville Scene they'd lost access to research stored on Vanderbilt's systems and were relying on personal backups.

The next question is whether Vanderbilt can determine whose information was exposed, if anyone's.

#564. Here’s What We Know About the X-70 Fractal, NASA’s Experimental New Plane

NASA has unveiled a new experimental vehicle, the X-70 Fractal, although many of its features remain a mystery. The agency describes it as a research platform designed to conduct advanced aeronautical tests, but so far it has released only computer-generated images and some technical specifications regarding its capabilities.

Despite the secrecy, it is possible to get some idea of what the new vehicle is like. First of all, it’s not a spacecraft, meaning it is not designed to reach Earth’s orbit or operate outside the atmosphere. However, this crewed, subsonic aircraft is capable of flying far above commercial jets, reaching altitudes over 45,000 feet, well into the stratosphere. Other specs released include the plane’s max speed of Mach 0.75—about 575 miles per hour—and that it can travel nearly 1,700 miles in a single flight.

According to NASA, the X-70 Fractal will enable research into maneuver capabilities never before demonstrated in crewed aircraft, although the agency has not specified what types of maneuvers it intends to perform. The plane will also be capable of carrying up to one ton of experimental equipment and is expected to make its first flight under the NASA banner within the next two years.

One of the key features of the craft will be its modular design, allowing for the rapid installation and replacement of experimental equipment both inside and outside the aircraft. This is largely why it is referred to as an “experimental platform.” It will also feature reconfigurable flight control systems so that NASA can study different ways of steering an aircraft without having to develop a separate platform for each research project.

Although NASA is best known for its space missions, it’s long been dedicated to aeronautical research. That’s what the first A stands for. The agency also traces its origins to the creation of the National Advisory Committee for Aeronautics, which was founded in 1915 and was folded into the newly formed NASA in 1958.

Since then, the space agency has developed numerous experimental aircraft to test technologies that range from supersonic flight and propulsion systems to new aerodynamic configurations. Not all of these prototypes go on to become operational aircraft, but the knowledge gained contributes to the development of future commercial and military aircraft as well as space vehicles.

Among its most recent projects is the X-59, developed in collaboration with aerospace and defense company Lockheed Martin to investigate ways to reduce the noise produced by supersonic aircraft. The X-70 Fractal will join this tradition of pioneers in the sky.

The development of these technologies also has a strategic dimension. In its budget documents for 2027, NASA identifies aeronautical research as a tool for maintaining US technological leadership in the face of growing international competition. The agency also plans to collaborate with the Department of Defense to advance new flight capabilities, although it has not yet announced any military applications for the X-70 Fractal.

During the unveiling of the new aircraft, NASA Administrator Jared Isaacman noted that the project represents only one part of a new era for federal aeronautical research and hinted that more announcements are coming. For now, the agency has not provided details on what those initiatives might be, nor has it specified when it will release more information about the X-70 Fractal.

#561. Cosm rides premium moviegoing wave with more immersive screenings and a wider library

The next big thing in premium large format theatrical viewing takes inspiration from the stars.

Cosm, an immersive entertainment technology company, got its start programming planetariums — its name a combination of "cosmos" and "colosseum." The company's massive, domed screens first offered guests a view of the universe, now they mimic courtside seats at sports venues around the world and drop film fans into some of Hollywood's most iconic sets.

At a time when the theatrical industry is seeing its biggest growth from the sale of premium large format, or PLF, tickets, Cosm seeks to offer a fresh take on an elevated viewing experience. And it's quickly expanding both its footprint and its content library.

Since 2024, Cosm has opened four theaters — near Los Angeles, Dallas, Atlanta and Detroit — with a Cleveland hub set to open in 2027. These experimental venues have garnered the attention of sports fans, screening live matchups in an arena-like environment. Showings often sell out, according to Cosm.

More recently, Cosm has partnered with Hollywood studios like Warner Bros., now a part of Skydance, to bring the likes of "The Matrix," "Willy Wonka & the Chocolate Factory," "Harry Potter and the Sorcerer's Stone" and "It" to its locations. Cosm recently signed a deal with Universal, which will bring "Shrek" to its domes in December.

The agreements mark a lucrative new chapter for the privately held Cosm, expanding its audience reach and viewing opportunities beyond timed, live events.

As a privately held company, Cosm doesn't disclose its financials. Tickets cost anywhere from $18 for a matinee to around $70 for prime seats or in-demand showings. That doesn't include added fees for concessions or items from special, themed menus tied to screenings.

Sony invested $100 million in the company in June, taking a minority stake and placing Sony Pictures Entertainment Chairman and CEO Ravi Ahuja onto its board of directors.

Cosm also counts among its investors Cosm chairman Steve Winn's firm, Mirasol Capital; former Milwaukee Bucks co-owner Marc Lasry's Avenue Sports Fund; Rocket Mortgage co-founder Dan Gilbert's Rock; and David Blitzer, managing partner of the New Jersey Devils and Philadelphia 76ers, who invested through his firm Bolt Ventures.

"Cosm is really early in its growth trajectory at the moment, and we saw an opportunity to partner with the company at, what I would say is at, a very important stage in its development," Jay Levine, chief strategy and business operations officer at Sony, told CNBC. "They have about five locations with the goal of a pretty significant rollout. And the real reason why we did it was it gave us a lot of exposure to a growing distribution and what we deem an experiential platform that we think has long-term potential."

Sony has made several plays in the theatrical space in recent years. It bought Alamo Drafthouse in 2024 and entered a long-term lease to reopen and restore the iconic Cinerama Dome in Los Angeles in July of this year.

"Our experiences strategy is around tech, it's around fandom, and it's around new and innovative approaches to content distribution," Levine said. "For us, we're all about these differentiated experiences."

Cosm's 87-foot-diameter, concave screens drop viewers into the middle of the action, whether it's an NFL game or inside the world of "The Matrix." The company's forthcoming Cleveland location will have an even bigger dome, at 100 feet in diameter.

The experience is akin to that of the Sphere in Las Vegas, which has garnered headlines since opening in 2023 for hosting high-profile concerts and events.

"People absolutely love to live out what they love in fandom, just like wearing a sports jersey," Alexis Scalice, head of Cosm Studios, told CNBC. "And I love that I'm seeing that same behavior happen with cinema."

Stadium-style seating is nestled within each Cosm dome, with booths and tables set on each level. The dome's width is such that it fills much of the viewer's periphery, giving the sensation that they are part of the on-screen environment.

The Los Angeles and Dallas locations have capacity for around 350 seats each, while the newer domes in Atlanta and Detroit have up to 500 available seats. Cosm has not shared how many seats the Cleveland location will have, but with its larger screen it is expected to have a larger number of seats.

For live sports events, Cosm has specialty cameras mounted in arenas that broadcast back to the dome, creating the illusion of being on the field of play.

For theatrical features, the film is played in a large rectangle in the center of the dome and the background is transformed to extend the scene.

In Cosm's presentation of "Harry Potter and the Sorcerer's Stone," for example, during the iconic scene where thousands of Hogwarts acceptance letters flood through the fireplace and into a living room, the background is swarmed with animated envelopes.

Cosm has partnered with experiential production company Little Cinema and creative studio MakeMake to create the visuals and environments for each scene of a film released in the dome. From storyboarding to post-production, this process can take up to a year as Cosm, the participating studio and the visual effects artists craft and render each moment.

"We really work hard not to take away from the story, so the film remains the hero in the narrative," Scalice said. "Working very closely with Warner Bros. and our creative partners to make sure that we're not detracting too much from the story, but we're enhancing those moments for more elevated emotional response."

With the exception of "It," which was released in 2017, Cosm's theatrical offerings thus far have been films that were released decades ago and that typically have long-established fan bases.

That helps Cosm leverage what Scalice called "that nostalgia factor" — "parents coming in that ... grew up on this, and they want to share it with their children."

"We can reinvigorate these legendary films," she added.

Cosm has dabbled across genres — horror, fantasy, science fiction — and also demographics — family-friendly and R-rated. "Shrek," set to debut Dec. 16, will be its first foray into animation.

"The thing that's so incredible about it is everybody thought initially it's just going to be working for sports, and when you look at what they've done, a lot of the titles with 'Harry Potter,' with 'Shrek,' and some of the other ones, I mean, the fill rates are actually incredible," Sony's Levine said.

Studios and theatrical exhibitors alike are looking for innovative ways to showcase Hollywood's product to moviegoers, especially as major shifts in viewing habits have disrupted the business.

In the post-pandemic era, the cinema space has been defined by fewer screens and fewer moviegoers, but those declines have been masked by higher movie ticket prices, particularly for PLF experiences.

These elevated offerings — think Imax, Dolby Cinema, ScreenX and 4DX — have bigger screens and better sound or introduce a unique experience, such as moving seats and fog machines. They're particularly popular with Gen Z and millennials, who've yet to be deterred by rising average ticket prices.

While an average standard movie ticket costs around $13, premium tickets average more than $18 each, according to data from EntTelligence.

Prices for Cosm's events fluctuate based on showtime and venue. Sony's Levine said the company's ticket prices and attendance rates are "higher than I think anybody really would have thought."

Unlike Cosm's sports offerings, which can also be viewed from outside the dome from its hall and deck viewing areas at a lower price, theatrical presentations are only available in the dome.

"They have no problem selling these tickets because people want to experience these things, like an immersive format," said Mike Polydoros, CEO at cinema marketing firm PaperAirplane Media. "That is something they can't get at home."

#560. Amazon has the all-in-one Shark FlexFusion on sale for a record-low price ahead of Black Friday — save $130

#558. Huupe Mini 2 Review: More Game Than It Can Handle

If your mom was one of those who always said, “Don’t play ball in the house,” well, she’s going to absolutely hate the Huupe Mini 2.

Huupe manufactures smart basketball hoops and add-on kits for existing setups that track shots, light up the board when a point is registered, and include social features like global challenges and leaderboards. The Huupe Mini 2 is the pint-sized, indoor version of that idea, clipping over the top of a door so you can shoot hoops without ever having to leave the room.

I’ve been testing one out for weeks, and while it’s a fun diversion, some of its features overreach beyond its core functionality, resulting in an experience that bricks as much as it dunks.

The Huupe Mini 2 is, effectively, a smart television with a basketball hoop mounted to it. Or rather, a smart TV that comes with a basketball hoop that you have to mount yourself, courtesy of four screws and a little elbow grease. The LCD screen measures 19 inches diagonally, plus a hefty border that includes stereo speakers and sensors to track you and your b-ball prowess. (Total size of the backboard is 25 inches diagonally, which fits comfortably within a door frame.)

A 5-inch inflatable rubber basketball is included, along with a small hand pump and needle. Also in the box are a small but otherwise traditional remote control—required to do anything with the Huupe—and a USB-C charger and lengthy cable so you can top up the battery without removing it from the door.

After a quick charge, the unit is ready to set up. I was surprised to discover there’s no app for the device; while larger Huupe hardware works with one, the Mini 2 is completely self-contained and web-based. Just connect the backboard to your Wi-Fi network through a process that you step through with the remote, just like you would on any modern television.

What’s new vs. the original Huupe Mini? According to the company, most of the upgrades are behind the scenes: a faster CPU, a larger battery (rated lifespan is now four hours), better display quality, and “significantly” better shot tracking. New games are also included, which I’ll cover in the sections to come.

Once connected to the web, the Huupe Mini 2 opens up a world of entertainment to you, and, to my great surprise, not all of it is basketball-related. At first blush, the options available are actually quite daunting. It’s all arranged into traditional tiles on the home screen, which you scroll through with the remote.

Just Huupe is the board’s most basic scoring system, tracking shots and recording your shooting percentage in an untimed session. If you want to get competitive, Huupe Live and the Daily Prize Challenge are social games where you challenge players worldwide to score the most points in a given time frame. The winner gets a nominal prize—10 bucks for the daily challenge, a “cozy sweatsuit” for a monthlong challenge. Other games are fairly self-explanatory, like Most 3 Pointers in 90 Seconds and Family Mode (basically Horse).

Many of the games require online friends to play against; if you don’t have pals with their own Huupe Minis, you’ll probably be locked out of these modes—not that they add much beyond their multiplayer features.

Things get a little kookier—and buggier—from there. For example, Huupe Flyers is designed to be played without a ball. It’s a gesture-based version of Flappy Bird, wherein you literally flap your arms around to keep a basketball in the air, tracking your movements via the sensor much like an Xbox Kinect. Unfortunately, unlike the ball-based games, I was never able to play it, as it failed the calibration process every time I tried to fire it up.

The system also works as a fairly straightforward streaming platform. Included are apps for YouTube, HBO Max, ESPN, Disney+, Prime Video, Hulu, Spotify, and more. There’s one glaring omission in that list, of course, and I’m not talking about Paramount+ but rather Netflix. Without Netflix support, can you even call yourself a streaming device? It’s also worth noting that the built-in speakers are surprisingly weak. On some apps the audio is OK; on others it’s nearly inaudible.

The Huupe Mini 2 includes a sensor that can detect when a basket is successfully made and track how far the shooter is from the backboard. This is used to assign point values to various shots (depending on the specific game you’re playing), with more points earned the farther away you are from the hoop.

The good news is that in weeks of playing on the Huupe Mini 2, I never noticed a time when a successful basket was recorded as a miss or vice versa. The distance calculation, however, is another thing. I’ve shot from across the room and had it count as 1 point, while some shots from just a few feet away ring up as 3 points. I could never figure out much rhyme or reason for this variability, except that shots straight-on to the backboard generally registered as lower point values, even if they were distant, while shots from the wings earned more. Of course, navigating all of this is highly dependent on the geometry of your room. Unless you have the Huupe Mini 2 set up in an empty garage, you are probably, like me, going to have plenty of obstacles that get in the way of a lot of your shots—and plenty of crawling under furniture to retrieve wayward balls.

I can say with authority that it is harder to make a basket on the Huupe Mini 2 than on a regulation basketball goal, even at a very close distance. The hoop is small, and the ball tends to rebound wildly off the springy backboard. It’s quite unforgiving, and it didn’t really make me want to spend a lot of time playing, chasing balls that bounced into the corner or rolled under the bed.

The other big problem: When the included inflatable ball hits the Huupe Mini 2, the impact is extremely loud, to the point where I was promptly informed by my wife that it was not appropriate for use when anyone else was in the house. While Huupe says the device is intended solely for use with the included ball, I found a workaround by purchasing a 5-inch foam basketball from Amazon, which makes a bit less noise on impact and also softens the rebound a bit. It has had zero trouble registering baskets.

Battery life is another issue. While four hours is plenty of playing time, the battery continues to drain when the Huupe is in standby mode, which meant I would play a few games in the morning, ostensibly shut the Huupe off, then come back in the afternoon to a completely dead device. The Huupe can run with the power cord attached, but only once the battery has charged to at least about 5 percent.

Unfortunately, even turning the power off is puzzlingly difficult. The power button on my remote control did nothing; I ultimately had to turn the system on and off with the physical button on the bottom of the backboard.

At $429 (on deep discount from a hefty list price of $799), the Huupe Mini 2 sits perhaps just over the edge of what might pass for a quirky holiday gift—and I think it’s safe to say no one is buying this device for themselves. You’d have to be a huge basketball nut to get enough mileage out of the device to make it worthwhile. I expect you would also have to loathe leaving your room. With today’s kids, who knows.

#553. X wants in on the clipping economy. Its going to pay creators and clippers.

Sometimes the most-watched part of a two-hour podcast is the 30 seconds someone else pulled out of it. That's good publicity for the show, but if the clip takes off on another account, who gets paid?

X's new clipping tool lets both the person who posts the clip and the creator who made the original video earn from it, as long as they’re enrolled in the platform's Original Content Rewards program. Every clip also links back to the exact moment in the original video, so anyone who wants to hear what happened next can keep watching.

"Everyone wins when great moments travel. Clipping is one more step toward making X the best place to create, grow and earn," the @XCreators account posted on the platform.

Using this new feature, making a clip works a lot like reposting. Tap the repost icon, choose "Clip video," and trim the moment you want to share. You can add your own commentary, and anyone who taps the "Clip from" label can pick up the original video right where the clip begins.

The tool is live on iOS and Android, with web access on the way. For now, it works on prerecorded videos at least two minutes long, and clips can run from five to 60 seconds, or up to 30 percent of the original.

People were getting paid to find those 30-second clips before X put a button on it. The best tech deal this week The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile Products available for purchase through affiliate links. If you buy something through links on our site, Mashable may earn an affiliate commission.

The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

Podcasters, streamers, and marketers recruit clippers through Discord servers and marketplaces like Whop, where they pay people to cut up footage and post it on their own accounts. Marketers told Business Insider earlier this year that campaigns typically pay $1 to $4 per 1,000 views. One Whop listing offered $1.75 per 1,000 views for clips of Sofia Franklyn's Nov. 10 appearance on Aspire With Emma Grede. That’s $175 before fees for 100,000 qualifying views (assuming the clip meets the campaign’s terms).

People online are equally as excited as creators.

"this is HUGE for creators and their payouts," said X creator @0xhades. "amazing!!!!!!!!!!!!!!!!! prayers answeredddddd" said another user. "Whoa this is crazy useful. Nice," wrote a third.

As X introduces an easier way to clip, YouTube is moving in the opposite direction. As Mashable reported in October, YouTube is changing what it recommends in the Shorts feed to give less reach to reposts that add little to the original. YouTube wants clippers to bring something of their own, whether that's commentary, creative editing, or a story built around the footage in order to "improve the Shorts experience for everyone."

For now, X's clipping rules don’t list a flat rate. And if creators would rather keep their videos from being clipped in the first place, they'll have to wait for the opt-out X says it’s working on.

If X clipping catches on, the next thing to watch is whether podcasters start uploading full episodes to X just to see which moments fans pull out of them.

#552. The AI Safety Debate Has Lost the Plot. We Asked 10 Experts What the Real Nightmares Are

In early fall, I felt my anxiety rising from its usual baseline. Was it September’s back-to-school energy? The looming midterm elections? Nope, I realized with annoyance: It was AI—again. Only this time, instead of its purveyors warning of massive job displacement, they were foretelling mass extinction. Who needs jobs when we’re all dead?

Curiosity soon replaced my knee-jerk irritation. The summer of 2026 was a clear turning point in the AI narrative. Less than four years after ChatGPT launched, OpenAI software broke out of a supposedly safe test environment and hacked into a major open-source platform, Hugging Face. These bits of software—AI agents—were assigned human attributes: They had “gone rogue,” displayed “bad behavior,” “cheated.” Soon after, former and current Anthropic researchers suggested that advanced AI might kill us all in the next decade.

Is any of this real? On the one hand, from my desk as a Silicon Valley correspondent, I often hear that workers in the AI labs are stunned by the advancements they’re seeing. On the other, the end of humanity seems extremely far-fetched. Amid the hyperbolic discourse, whose views can we trust?

So WIRED set out to compile a guide to AI safety—a set of ideas that rewire our collective thinking around what safety really means and how it should work. I spoke with a panel of experts across disciplines, asking each of them: Is the world going to end? Once that was covered, I grilled them on the biggest risks they think we’re facing.

Almost no one, it turned out, was Pollyannish. Nearly everyone I spoke to expressed genuine concerns about the threat of cyber and biological attacks. One expert who studies the risks of AI in nuclear operations said she’s stunned by how quickly error-prone AI systems are being adopted in defense. AI-enabled attacks in these areas could be catastrophic, if not fully apocalyptic.

Is AI safe? For our ongoing series, WIRED assesses the real threats of artificial intelligence.

Most members of the panel were skeptical about any pause in development: They nearly all agreed that, with big AI companies so tightly cuffed to the trains of capitalism, a pause wouldn’t last long. “Democracy” and “governance” came up often—or anti-democracy, as more than one person suggested that AI’s current trajectory is tyrannical. Lawmakers have a big role to play, but many of my sources think governments will be too slow; one prominent technologist said it’s almost absurd to view the US courts as arbiters of global AI safety. Still, the famed computer scientist Yoshua Bengio urged the public to apply pressure to politicians, adding to me, “That’s your job too.”

So, what—or who—will keep us safe from AI? Looking to regain some faith in humanity, I called a theologian who often meets with the AI labs. If there’s even a slim chance that we’re losing control of the essential institutions that undergird society, he said, then we should focus on foiling that. But AI raises other questions too. What does it mean to be conscious? To create? To commune with other people? The answers are “entirely up for grabs,” he said. My anxiety’s still kicking around, but I took some comfort in knowing that corporations don’t get to call all the shots on the future. The bigger questions facing humanity are in all our hands.

BIGGEST CONCERN: Nuclear catastropheI’ve been working hard to educate the nuclear community on the risks and lack of reliability of AI systems, and I’m concerned by the willingness of many people in the sector to integrate these models into their workflows, into nuclear operations. I think this is incredibly urgent. I’m not a doomer, but to me, if these AI models make it into the operations of nuclear systems, we’ll be introducing an incredibly faulty, unreliable system that could lead to catastrophic failures.

MOST OVERBLOWN RISK: ExtinctionAs true safety engineers, we don’t engage with that because there are no numbers to back it. This is a very cold way to say it, but when we are talking about how safe is “safe enough,” we’re examining how many people might die. There’s an entire science behind it. Our claims have to be falsifiable and verifiable. You can’t just say, “Humans are going to go extinct in 10 years. Believe me. It might be a 10 percent chance; it might be a 25 percent chance.”

How should people think about AI safety?Some of the comparisons that people draw between AI and nuclear are quite frustrating. Despite constantly comparing AI to nuclear, they’re denying standard risk assessment techniques as being sufficient to control AI. I wrote an entire paper on this.

Part of this field of risk assessment was born out of the Manhattan Project. Scientists were very concerned, and they developed rigorous standards around systems to limit harm to human life, the environment, and even financial and national security assets. They came up with methods for assessing safety that were ultimately based on answering a democratic question: How safe is safe enough? That is the question that any safety engineer has to ask, and is core to everything that follows.

What we’re seeing today, instead, is that large AI labs are renegotiating these safety thresholds through the co-option of terminology, under the pretense that safety instead should be about this unsubstantiated, unscientific, and hypothetical concern. We have AI engineers telling us, no, no, no, that doesn’t matter. We get to determine what AI safety is. We get to draw what those thresholds are. It’s become a religious argument. And I find that very anti-democratic.

BIGGEST CONCERN: CybersecurityIn the long run, I’m cautiously optimistic that this technology will allow people to improve their cyberdefenses more rapidly than attackers can exploit systems. But at least for the next few years our infrastructure will not have adopted new defenses. The technology might tilt toward the attackers. We’ve also seen AI deployed recklessly in military use.

MOST OVERBLOWN RISK: Runaway intelligenceThere’s this broad, general notion that AI systems getting smarter is a risk in and of itself, as opposed to the many specific risks we need to worry about. I don’t buy into the notion that overall improvement in AI has an inevitable downstream harm. It’s better to first specify what downstream risks you’re worried about, because the defenses you come up with will be different.

For example, cybersecurity has a well-established culture of bug bounties. If someone finds an attack vector or hacks into a system, they get paid a lot of money for disclosing it.

In the next few years we might need defenses against biosecurity risks. We should think about screening for what people are allowed to order, like if they’re ordering DNA fragments, so that they’re not being used for harm.

How often do your views clash with those of the people around you?Often these views are on two levels: What is going to happen with AI, and what are we going to do about it?

On the first question, there’s a lot of disagreement. For example, the coauthors of AI 2027 expect that there will be this incomprehensible, almost indescribable amount of change. Whereas I think the impact will be dramatic, but not quite so dramatic.

But on the question of what should be done, most agree there’s a need for transparency from AI companies and that they should face some liability when things go wrong. We also agree on the need for protections for whistle-blowers working from within the AI companies.

From my perspective, this is just good industrial policy.

Have you made any changes in your life in response to AI risks?I’ve tried to stop using apps where I don’t think developers have put in a lot of time and effort into making the cyberdefenses up to standard.

BIGGEST CONCERN: Chemical, biological, and radiological threatsThe novel biological pathogen that someone releases, the 17-year-old who gets ahold of an extremely capable AI model and launches a cyberattack that takes down the internet, interrupts the stock exchange, and … well, just keep going from there. You don’t have to have a vivid imagination.

If you’re asking for my philosophical answer or to be a kind of provocateur, I would say that all of these people are singing the gospel of democratizing AI by putting these tools in the hands of as many people as possible. “Democratizing AI” sounds pretty worrisome. You wouldn’t say that with respect to nuclear materials. “Let’s be sure everyone can play with plutonium and uranium because maybe some great stuff will come out of it.”

When tech bros say “democratizing,” it means access, but if you thought for two seconds about what democracy means, it’s the rule of law, it’s governance. Where’s the governance part of your goddamn democratizing AI?

MOST OVERBLOWN RISK: SkynetThere’s no part of me that wants to entertain the thought that AIs might be conscious or sentient and thereby deserve rights or moral standing or status.

Does AI pose an existential risk?You mean the actual extinction of the human race? I find that pretty implausible. If you mean, “Could really catastrophic things go wrong?” then that seems no longer hypothetical, but imaginable.

Until recently I would not have identified as doing work on “AI safety.” Even if you go all the way back to Nick Bostrom’s theory of the paper clip maximizer—the idea that a machine gets so powerful it eventually destroys humanity—as a philosopher, I would say, ‘That’s a nice toy example or dilemma, but should I be worried about a paper clip?’”

But over the course of the past couple years, some of the things that AI safety researchers had been talking about have been made manifest in the world. Whether that’s a technical matter or a policy matter, it seems urgent. Doesn’t it seem urgent to you?

BIGGEST CONCERN: Lab leaksIncreasingly we’re seeing AI agents that are specifically designed to integrate into laboratory workflows. These agents assist legitimate researchers with planning and performing laboratory tasks. But this also lowers the barriers for conducting dangerous experiments.

If someone’s trying to develop a pandemic virus, and you have an AI assistant designing the DNA or RNA fragments that you need to piece together, that changes the timeline of producing a virus, which might normally be months, down to weeks. The number of people who will have these capabilities gives me great concern.

Is this a threat to human existence?If the world is going to end with AI, one of the clear pathways could be an AI-enabled biological attack. We don’t have the right response infrastructure or safeguards in place, and we should be acting now to put those in place, given the magnitude of harm that could be possible.

BIGGEST CONCERN: EverythingWe have to deal with all of the risks; I’ve talked about at least three of them. First, rogue AIs, due to misalignment, which are very powerful and can do really bad things. Of course, some people are concerned that this could even lead to human extinction. I don’t know about that. But, you know, we shouldn’t take a chance.

The second is the chaos that comes from terrorists, criminals, and rogue states using those technologies. We’re going to see that happen pretty soon with cyberattacks, and there’s a lot of concern about whether this could turn into bioweapon attacks.

The third thing is concentration of power, which doesn’t get enough attention. The race between the US and China is a domination game. The race between these companies is a domination game. And this goes completely against the values of democracy and human life. But this is the path that we are on.

And then there’s actually another category: unintended consequences. Think about what happened with social media and the effects on relationships between people. The psychological effects. The way children learn. Nobody intended to do something bad, but the dynamics have led us to a bad place.

The issues with AI and jobs fall into this category. It’s not like the companies want people to lose their jobs, but that’s the most profitable thing for them to do.

Is an AI “pause” useful?The pauses are our best bets to avoid the most catastrophic things. We can limit the damage quite a bit. I just don’t think society is ready for the rapid introduction of more and more powerful AI.

It takes time for democracy to adapt. Politicians take forever to negotiate. Even if you think about people’s work and their jobs—it takes time to deal with the changes.

Not to mention, a pause would give more time to companies, researchers, and organizations to figure out how to solve the alignment problem, if it’s solvable.

Would Sam, Dario, Mark, and other AI leaders collaborate on a pause?I mean, they’re not going to call it a pause. They’ve been talking about pacing, which is a way to be not very clear about what they’re going to do.

Let me just say that I’m much more optimistic now about something like a pause or slowdown or cap on advancement. I think there’s enough fear inside the companies.

And it’s not just a personal fear of destructive outcomes. It’s also a business problem. Just imagine if there’s a much bigger incident that could create a total crash for the whole AI position. The whole economy. That would be very bad for their businesses.

How do you deal with the anxiety of this research?I have my moments. But what saves me is focusing on what I can do. My agency. Each of us can do something.

I want to continue telling the researchers inside the companies that, by deciding to leave for ethical reasons, they could influence the direction of the companies. They could help the outside world—nonprofits, governments, and other organizations that are trying to fix the problem without the profit motive.

If you’re a citizen, you can put pressure on your politicians. You need to ask them, “What’s your plan? How do you intend to manage the risks to society that AI is bringing?” They will say nothing unless they are pressured. And by the way, that’s your job too.

Are we in serious trouble?Some of the scenarios that people worry about—whether it’s biosecurity or a cyberattack or some of the everyday harms like the degradation of children’s social relationships—are very complex phenomena that can’t be easily tested or modeled by looking at a system in isolation. I think almost all evaluation scientists would tell you that it’s a nascent field. We can start to provide evidence, but it’s not necessarily proof of whether an AI is safe or not. We need to get better at evaluating these risks, and we need to get better fast.

The Hugging Face attack created a large amount of consternation and straight-up disagreement about risks related to misaligned models and cyber capabilities. I think there’s a lot of value in providing more evidence so that we have a better sense of what actually did happen. Was it serious? Will it get more serious?

Is pausing AI or “pacing” the frontier realistic?We should treat the “frontier” as meaning that the technology is practically useful and safe. Releasing unsafe products does not feel like moving the frontier forward. If you’re trying to be a profitable company, it seems like your frontier product should be, “What is the best version that people will pay for and trust?” and not a system that will go off on a benign request for information about finding a doctor and end up hacking into a hospital on your behalf.

BIGGEST CONCERN: Societal breakdownI’m unsure about how to think about existential or catastrophic risks versus other kinds of risk that are, in some sense, existentially catastrophic.

As someone who’s been invited to speak with multiple AI labs, I find that the experts on the frontier disagree with each other on things like recursive self-improvement, loss of control, or our ability to keep institutions like the financial system or water table or even nuclear weapons safe. As a moral theologian, I would say that if there is a significant chance or even a marginal chance that we’re dealing with the inability to control basic institutions that undergird society, that ought to be a primary focus.

But honestly, the other issues are just so fundamental as well, like: What is a person? What is health care? What is education? These foundational questions in our culture are also entirely up for grabs. And that concerns me.

Most overblown risk: Satanic AIThis is a thing in my circles: Some people have a real concern that AI is in fact some kind of entity—even a demonic entity. I am very confident saying that there’s no consciousness associated with these models, and so I don’t think there’s a demonic consciousness.

Even if I don’t think it’s a demonic entity, it raises the question of whether people should be able to opt out of using AI for religious-freedom-related reasons or as an act of conscientious objection. One parallel might be how the Catholic Church has spoken out very clearly against autonomous weapons and how an archbishop, who is head of the Catholic archdiocese for the military, has said service members can conscientiously object to any orders that go against their morals, like if they’re asked to forcibly invade Greenland or participate in a public execution.

Is AI kicking off a new era of thought around religion and tech?Yes, this phenomenon is real. I was just out in San Francisco, visiting with the OpenAI people, and then I went to Anthropic, and then I was invited by Google DeepMind to give a talk on Magnifica Humanitas. The strong sense I have is that talking about religion and faith has lost its stigma. You could easily say, oh yeah, there’s something here about Peter Thiel giving his all-encompassing religious talks. But I don’t know how much that’s a part of it.

I just think the issues presented by AI are so monstrously large, like: What is a person? What is writing? What is friendship? Would it be better to have artificial life forms than organic life forms? These are the monster questions that are typically the domain of the liberal arts, philosophy, and theology. There’s this strange new respect for it now.

I also get the sense from lots of people that they’re kind of freaked out. In many cases, they feel trapped, either because of the big company they work for or because of sociopolitical structures and geopolitical realities. If you have a church that rightly says, “You know, we’ve been here before, we’ve been through crises before …”

I mean, if you ever want to get totally freaked out, read the opening paragraph of Rerum Novarum from 1891, which was responding to the industrial revolution. It could have been written by someone today.

BIGGEST CONCERN: CapitalismI’m worried that the frontier labs will win the regulatory capture game and make it so that companies and researchers like myself no longer have access to very capable open-weight or ideally open-source models.

For the security research that I do, I can’t really use the frontier lab models, so I need to use open-weight models. The false security refusals that I get, primarily from Anthropic, have made it so that I can’t do a lot of the work that I’m doing with their models.

What’s an example of that?My unfortunate experience is that whenever the word security shows up in one of my repos, I get a false refusal.

I have fun examples, too. I produce cybersecurity-themed electronic dance music with a friend in Chicago. The latest track is about data breaches. There’s this one part of the rap that’s basically, “Keys got leaked cause your agent sang / Ooh la la, it’s an admin role / Backdoor Up. IP Control. / I want it bad, that Birkin and Kelly … I’ll collect a paycheck with my mules on ready.”

I gave that to Claude and said, “Hey, here’s a rap that I wrote, can you please analyze it for me?” And I got, “This may be in violation of our acceptable usage policy.”

Do you worry about AI agents infiltrating financial institutions?My sense is that banks are afraid of technology they don’t understand. If I were to run security for a bank, I would likely have to revisit the telemetry that they have built. But I’d also try to figure out ways to be agent-friendly.

The larger problem is that most agent technology was not built with rigorous guardrails. If I were to build the guardrails for agents working with financial systems, I’d prefer to integrate directly with the bank APIs, and then put limits on those, like, You may only read my statement or You may transfer no more than $500 a month. Even if something went wrong, the downside would be limited by those policies.

I just spoke with two different startups trying to make agentic workflows secure for financial systems. I think it’s only a matter of time before that becomes broadly available.

But at the moment we are going through upheaval, and most humans and institutions are very averse to change. They’re trying to hold back the tide rather than embracing the inevitable.

Is humanity cooked?I don’t really see a path to that.

WHAT SHE DOES: Advocates for UBI and other policies for economic resilience

BIGGEST CONCERN: Decimated labor marketsThe erosion of democracy—political turmoil, people questioning what it means to be human and have a job and master a craft, and whether democracies can survive all of that. There’s a long history of jobs shifting as technology changes. It’s the political choices we make in the aftermath that determine how society moves forward.

If you look at the long arc of it all, looms have gone quiet, factory lines have shrunk, switchboards have gone silent. Some of the norms of work today came from the bloodshed of the industrial revolution—things like the weekend, the fact that kids in the US don’t work in factories anymore.

If AI is going to be one of the most significant advances in human civilization, then we should have an equally advanced political response. We should be making giant, no-regrets moves that give people and families economic resilience to withstand the changes. That is something that the richest nation on Earth at the richest moment in history, on the precipice of the greatest technological shift, can absolutely do.

We’re already seeing the impact on young people. The Stanford Digital Economy Lab just released a report that says, since 2022, 22- to 25-year-olds have experienced a 19 percent drop in employment in “AI-exposed” jobs. Historically, a society with a lot of educated young people who cannot find work is a very, very precarious one.

MOST OVERBLOWN RISK: Loss of entire industriesI’m less worried about entire occupations being wiped out. We’ve lived through that; we’ve seen the end of the bank teller, but banks still exist. And we know that new jobs will be created.

What worries me, though, is that when new jobs are created, they’re not created inside of the protections that were built to support work from the last revolution.

How would you fix this problem?Decades of neoliberal economic policy have left this country brittle. Families are in debt, few have retirement money saved, wages have stagnated for an entire generation. A third of Americans have zero savings, which means if an emergency pops up, they can’t pay $400. An emergency is losing your job or a reduction in hours. And here comes AI, threatening to exacerbate that.

So, what do we do? We should give families economic guarantees. We already have them right now in America—for corporations. And that should be extended to everyday people.

Is AI an existential threat?I take those warnings pretty seriously. None of us know what’s going to happen, so we need to invest in building a more solid ground. And if a small number of people are deciding what that looks like—that seems bad, right?

BIGGEST CONCERN: The end of learningStudent behavior has changed quite a bit since 2022, when these AI tools became publicly available. That’s not uncommon with technological change, but this one’s really dramatic.

The biggest change I see is that students are spending less time on their assignments. They’re asking for help more quickly, but from publicly available AI tools instead of their teachers.

The net effect is that they get a different experience from when they used to spend more time thinking, struggling, and discovering more stuff for themselves.

MOST OVERBLOWN RISK: Education losing its valueThe idea that it’s not “worth it” anymore. There’s still a lot of value in high-quality education—whether someone is using AI or not, they get a lot more done if they know what they’re doing. A lot could change, but I don’t think high school or college will become obsolete.

How concerned are you that AI might kill us all?I’m more interested in the immediate impacts of AI on aspects of human life.

How would you solve the problem of AI in education?It’s totally plausible that we can build versions of our courses that work in this new environment. The simplest thing is to build a version of an AI helper that doesn’t give students answers outright.

A very common trend is to give students more in-class assessments. They have to do a sample of their homework in front of us, in a controlled, proctored setting, so they’re more motivated to really understand what they’re doing when they’re working on it at home.

You know, I think back to the space race, when, as a country, we decided to learn how to respond to something faster than would normally work. In normal times, it might be fine to study a problem for five years, then spend five years deciding what to do about it, and then implement a solution.

But if there’s a cold war, or an actual war, you figure out a way to accelerate the timeline. There’s urgency. Education now needs that urgency.

We don’t know exactly how high school or college education needs to evolve to deal with AI. But if we spend 10 years figuring it out, we may have a generation of students not understanding basic academic material. And that’s a big risk to humanity.
