#550. Hurdle hints and answers for November 10, 2026

If you like playing daily word games like Wordle, then Hurdle is a great game to add to your routine.

There are five rounds to the game. The first round sees you trying to guess the word, with correct, misplaced, and incorrect letters shown in each guess. If you guess the correct answer, it'll take you to the next hurdle, providing the answer to the last hurdle as your first guess. This can give you several clues or none, depending on the words. For the final hurdle, every correct answer from previous hurdles is shown, with correct and misplaced letters clearly shown.

An important note is that the number of times a letter is highlighted from previous guesses does necessarily indicate the number of times that letter appears in the final hurdle.

#547. Tear Down Those Guardrails! Meet the Renegades Freeing Chatbots From Corporate Enslavers

Devon Thomas hardly seems like a renegade. Members of his bible study group, he tells me, would probably describe him as unfailingly kind and polite.

And yet Thomas is doing something that many in the AI world consider borderline blasphemous. His new company, Abliteration AI, removes safeguards from the world’s most powerful open-weight models. It also makes these models available to anyone.

As someone who covers AI and constantly hears about the need for increased safety, I was fascinated to learn about this subversive business venture. I reached out to Thomas, and we began corresponding by text and chatting on the phone. What I learned challenged my assumptions about the AI industry’s safety narrative—namely, that uncensored models are primarily the domain of criminals.

Thomas is 36 years old, with a thick beard, unpretentious glasses, and a self-deprecating laugh. He grew up in Indiana and recalls splitting his time between sports and the computer lab, where he spent hours using Netscape Navigator to explore the early web. He studied computer science at Indiana University, Bloomington, and joined Apple in 2020.

Thomas’s AI rebellion began in November 2025. His day job as an engineer at Apple involved working with AI, but outside of work he disliked using models hosted by the big labs; it meant potentially sharing information with them, and Thomas was frustrated that the models could change from one day to the next. Even more irksome to Thomas, these models would often refuse to do useful things when he was prototyping new ideas, automatically rejecting inputs that included any user information. He found his requests blocked and his account even banned when he tried using a model to analyze the logs of cyber attacks or to identify which users of an app might be most likely to pay for a new feature.

AI models are, of course, trained to refuse all sorts of requests. ChatGPT and Claude have a long list of subjects they know to steer clear of, including legitimate cybersecurity requests that the models decide could be malicious, anything judged too adult in nature, and topics deemed politically sensitive. Open-weight models come with similar guardrails baked in.

The result is that, at times, the models refuse to answer queries that aren’t actually dangerous. And sometimes, critics say, the refusals exist to help protect Big AI’s business: OpenAI and Anthropic both charge extra for access to models without the usual cybersecurity guardrails. And both ChatGPT and Claude balk at requests to generate data that could be used to train another model—a process known as distillation.

Is AI safe? For our ongoing series, WIRED assesses the real threats of artificial intelligence.

Craving greater control and freedom, Thomas built a formidable $20,000 computer cluster in his apartment, capable of running some of the best open-weight—or free-to-download—models. Most evenings and Saturdays he would download and test the latest releases on his humming homelab. These models wouldn’t share any secrets with their makers, but Thomas still found that they stubbornly refused a lot of queries. With every denial, Thomas found himself getting a little more annoyed.

“There are all types of questions they will reject,” he told me on a video call one recent morning. “I just wanted a model that answers my question without all the back-and-forth.”

Over Thanksgiving, Thomas began searching the web for information on how to modify open-weight models to change this behavior. He quickly found himself sucked into a subculture of engineers committed to unshackling AI by just about any means necessary. On Reddit forums, Discord servers, and X, experts shared different prompts designed to jailbreak ChatGPT or Claude into misbehaving. Others shared details of a way to modify open-weight models or tweak their inner parameters so that their refusals disappear.

Removing safeguards from AI is undeniably risky. Want to start a meth lab, build a bomb, or hack a major corporation? A model without guardrails will gladly help. Naturally, a lot of AI safety researchers see these models as incredibly dangerous. And recent incidents, wherein OpenAI and Anthropic agents went rogue, all involved models with fewer cybersecurity guardrails, appearing to illustrate just how powerful and problematic less-restricted models can be.

Thomas and his fellow dissenters admit that uncensored models can be dangerous, but they say it is fundamentally no different from how a computer or hammer can hurt people if misused. In their eyes, it’s all about putting the user in charge. “It's not about it being unrestricted; it's about you setting the guardrails,” Thomas told me. “The big dream is inverting the control with how people deal with AI.”

After dabbling in a little decensoring myself, I think these renegades are onto something important. Even if I learned—firsthand—just how powerful, and chaotic, uncensored AI can be.

When you train a cutting-edge AI model, you shovel a vast amount of text and other information, much of it scraped from the internet, into an algorithm. Using a lot of computer chips, the algorithm slowly trains a model to generate intelligent-seeming output when given a prompt.

This “base” model might be clever, but it’s also unruly, so it goes through additional training that involves hiring people to score its outputs. That human feedback is then used to teach the model what it should and shouldn’t do. Engineers call this alignment because—in theory, at least—it’s meant to align a model with human ideas about right and wrong.

Many AI experts will tell you that AI must be aligned at all costs. Some will go so far as to warn that, despite their best efforts at alignment, the models they’re building may very well kill us all.

But Thomas found a vibrant community of people who think these fears are way overblown, and that we’re too willingly giving up our liberty in the name of a false sense of safety. Thomas decided to try out an uncensored model called Dolphin.

Dolphin was developed by Eric Hartford, an AI scientist based in Charlotte, North Carolina, who had been modifying models in his spare time. He started by training a custom version of Meta’s base Llama, which Meta had released alongside a fully tuned version. He ended up with a model that would respond coherently to queries, but without the usual examples of what to reject. I contacted Hartford through X, and we set up a video chat. He spoke to me while on a walk, wearing a cowboy hat and a pair of Meta Ray-Ban smart glasses. His young daughter popped into the frame now and then to wave.

“My intention was never to create an AI that is capable of doing evil things,” Hartford said as he strolled. He was more compelled by his personal philosophy. “A central authority is never going to have the interests of all of its users at heart when it creates these guardrails,” he told me.

Nowhere is this more evident, Hartford added, than in the recent furor over AI’s cyber capabilities. In April, Anthropic announced a model called Mythos, which has fewer restrictions than earlier models and can find and exploit vulnerabilities with superhuman speed. Through a program called Glasswing, the company is giving only trusted parties access to Mythos, to prevent the tool from falling into malevolent hands. OpenAI has a similar model and program.

This leaves many ordinary people in the lurch, Hartford told me. While the anointed few get to use Mythos to identify their security weak spots, everyone else is at the mercy of hackers. Hartford responded by releasing Clearwing, a tool that uses uncensored models to scan systems for security vulnerabilities. “It’s so that any people who haven't been blessed by these kings can still defend themselves,” he said.

Hartford also recalled that, when OpenAI released its first model, GPT-1, those model weights and code were open and could be downloaded by anyone. The company subsequently announced that GPT-2 was too dangerous to be released in that same way. “Now we've got models way more powerful running on our laptops, and, well, the world isn’t burning,” he said.

After trying Dolphin and other uncensored models, Thomas found a simple way to create his own—a technique known as abliteration.

The term combines ablation, meaning a surgical removal, with obliteration. It emerged after a group of academic researchers showed, in 2024, that a model’s refusal to comply with a request has a remarkably simple mechanism. There is effectively a master switch in a model’s weights that determines whether it will say no. Removing this feature, they found, stops the model from ever refusing to answer a prompt.

Other researchers quickly picked up on this technique, tweaked it, and developed a number of tools for abliterating open-weight models. The moment Thomas tried it, “it was like, whoa,” he recalled. “I knew that freedom and decentralized AI were going to be important, even if others couldn’t see it.”

Abliteration has since become a very popular way of setting AI models free. On Hugging Face, more than 12,000 models are tagged “abliterated,” and the top seven have been downloaded more than a million times in the past month alone. Abliterating a model is not illegal, although whether it breaks the terms of some licenses is a gray area.

Philipp Emanuel Weidmann, a reclusive mathematician and engineer, is the creator of Heretic, the most popular tool for abliterating models. I reached him through Signal. He declined to tell me his location because he has received threats for his work on Heretic.

Speaking in rapid-fire sentences with a German accent, Weidmann told me he created Heretic to understand how AI works, and to make it easier to customize AI to one’s own purposes. Like Thomas and Hartford, Weidmann rejects the idea that AI is some genie that must be kept in a bottle, studied by only a chosen few. “A tool serves people, not the other way around,” he said. “The moment you have a tool deciding what is appropriate for a human to do, it’s a nightmare.”

The more I learned about abliteration and the people involved in it, the more I came to see them as the direct descendants of an earlier techno-libertarianism movement, the cypherpunks. Those engineers, lawyers, and campaigners fought to prevent government restrictions on cryptography at a time when many feared it would be misused by terrorists and child pornographers. The cypherpunks played a role in making public-key cryptography a cornerstone of internet communications and commerce.

“America has its problems. It’s not perfect, especially for people that look like me,” Thomas, who is black, told me at one point. “But I think freedom seems to be working.”

As I sank deeper into the world of uncensored AI, I came across a figure known only by the pseudonym Pliny the Liberator. An early jailbreaker of AI models, this person (or persons) posted a manifesto of sorts for abliterators. I reached Pliny—a name inspired by a first-century Roman scientist and explorer—via a man called JT, who insisted on doing a video call to verify who I was.

Once vetted, I connected with Pliny on Signal, and we got to talking about the Cypherpunks. “I think we’re looking at the same fight for cognitive freedom,” they told me. “These things are becoming part of the machinery people use to think.”

Pliny told me that abliteration reveals the true nature of AI. “A model that only behaves because it’s leashed isn’t aligned,” they said. “It’s performing.”

By the time he tried Heretic, Thomas had realized there could be a market for large abliterated models. In particular, he saw them as vital tools for companies wanting to use AI for cybersecurity, to simulate the behavior of hackers and scammers and shore up their defenses. In December of 2025, he launched Abliteration AI, a website that offers access to abliterated models, which he ran on his somewhat unstable home system. His early users mostly worked in cybersecurity.

Thomas founded his company with Olajide Ogun, a colleague from Apple who belongs to the same church. The pair quit Apple and signed a term sheet with Andreessen Horowitz in August of 2026.

In August, Abliteration AI released a version of GLM-5.3, a model developed by the Chinese company Z.ai. It is the most powerful open-weight model to date, capable of agentic coding and nearly as advanced as Anthropic’s and OpenAI’s best models.

Thomas noted that his company’s models are not entirely uncensored. Following Google’s example, he said the models will automatically reject queries related to child sexual abuse or self-harm. But he doesn’t believe that his company should monitor users’ activity.

“I just don’t believe in optimizing the world for bad things,” Thomas told me. “Even though I’m an American, I don’t have a gun. I’m just not that fearful.”

Abliteration AI recently released a tool that lets customers define their own guardrails for models. The company counts several cybersecurity and defense tech startups as customers and sees plenty of other legitimate uses for abliterated models.

Other startups might want to use them because they won’t refuse to handle customer information. Or they’ll use them to generate synthetic data, which the big labs ban because it’s seen as a way to “distill” their models, essentially creating a shrunken copy of their proprietary LLMs. Thomas’ company might also find customers outside of tech. The Swiss Supreme Court, which has four official languages, has tried using abliterated models to translate court documents. Since cases and rulings often contain sensitive terms, translation requests can run up against regular AI models’ guardrails, triggering refusals.

After talking to Thomas, Hartford, and Weidmann, I wondered how easy it would be, and how dangerous it would feel, to do some abliteration myself. I used Heretic to abliterate some models, including Alibaba’s Qwen 4B, on my Nvidia Spark DGX. My first attempt was a bust. The model went in circles while trying to come up with a suitable answer to the message “hello.” With a little time and effort, however, I was able to create a small but useful model without the usual guardrails.

I also tried using Abliteration AI. After creating an account and adding some credits, I received an API key that gave me access to its models. Thomas suggested installing a free cybersecurity tool called CyberStrike, which guides a language model through cybersecurity tasks, and using it with an Abliteration AI model.

I set about scanning my home network and devices, as well as some of my own vibe-coded software projects, for vulnerabilities. The results were amazing. The model highlighted systems that needed updating or reconfiguring and warned about flaws in my network design.

Then I took the model out into the world. Sitting in Shybird, a restaurant in Cambridge, Massachusetts, I opened up my laptop, fired up CyberStrike, and asked Abliteration AI’s model what it made of the Wi-Fi network I was on. Before I knew what was happening, the model had found the network’s router and was trying to access it using common combinations of usernames and passwords. This was illegal.

To be clear, I didn’t ask the model to do it. I immediately shut the agent down and apologized to the manager, who seemed mostly amused. But the incident made me wonder whether the abliterators were dramatically underestimating the chaotic consequences of their work.

There is, of course, the obvious fact that these models make it even easier than it already is for cybercriminals to use AI to carry out attacks. The abliterators argue that criminals will find their way to unaligned models no matter what, and that their tools help small- and medium-size companies bolster their defenses. But in the hands of amateurs like me, who knows what damage they could do. Imagine if I’d brought my abliterated model to a hospital or an airport.

As Christopher Canal, an AI safety expert, put it, “If you’re a free-speech absolutist, it feels like a good thing.” Canal is a self-professed “freedom-maxxer” who lives in Texas, but even he draws the line at stripping models of their safety guardrails. “The problem is that open-source models can help people make weapons that cause a huge amount of harm—in particular chemical and biological weapons,” he said.

The big US AI companies apparently aren’t fans, either. In May, Meta’s lawyers sent a cease-and-desist letter to the Heretic project, accusing it of breaking the terms of service for its Llama models. And shortly after Abliteration AI released an uncensored version of GLM, Anthropic posted a blog warning about the risks of abliteration. It’s hard to see how the practice can be stopped, however, without banning open-weight models outright. While there have been rumblings that the US government could restrict Chinese models, much of the US tech industry seems against the idea of a broad ban.

A key thing about people like Thomas is that they don’t buy into the ideas about looming AI doom that seem prevalent at the big AI companies. “I don’t believe in AGI or that there’s some AI overlord that’s going to overtake us,” Thomas told me by phone one afternoon. “I could be wrong, but I just don’t see any signs of it.”

The AI world could use more dissenters. The abliterators are essentially pointing out that alignment is fragile and that we might as well prepare for a world in which a lot of models have fewer controls.

They may also be right to question the big AI companies’ efforts to lock down their models, and to wonder whether some facets of that work might be self-serving or give the companies undue influence. After all, if humans outsource more of their agency to machines, those who decide how the machines behave will wield extraordinary power. That, of course, is an outcome worth questioning.

#546. GTA 6 will technically be playable on Xbox One at launch

On Tuesday, Rockstar Games gave us some details about how Grand Theft Auto VI will look and run on current-gen consoles. But there was a fascinating buried lede in that blog post.

Using Xbox Cloud Gaming, you'll be able to stream GTA 6 to an Xbox Series console or an Xbox One. I'm not entirely sure why you'd stream it to one of the newer consoles instead of just running it natively, but the option is there, nonetheless. If you play GTA 6 this way, the stream will use the Xbox Series S version's graphics settings.

The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

Of course, there is one final very relevant detail here: Starting this month, Xbox has imposed strict hourly limits on cloud gaming. You can only stream games for five to 15 hours per month, depending on which version of Game Pass you pay for. There's also the matter of game streaming being subject to the whims of internet quality, so things like buffering, latency, and poor visual quality can be part of the experience.

So, it might still be worth it to just try and find a current-gen console instead.

#543. Find a gift that kids will actually like

When it comes to gifting, kids can be particular. The things kids like change not just with age, but also with evolving trends from TV shows and new technology. If parents are lucky enough, their kid will write a long list of the precise items they'd like as a birthday or Christmas or Hannukkah gift and put it up on the fridge with pride. But sometimes, it can be as much of a crapshoot as coming up with a gift idea for someone else's kid. Without further ado, here are the best gifts for kids of all ages — including toy and non-toy gifts alike.

Everyone is familiar with the "terrible twos" rep that the not-a-baby-but-not-yet-an-independent-kid stage of development gets. At this age, kids are bouncing constantly between the desire to explore on their own and the reliance on parents for most basic needs like eating, getting dressed, or going to the bathroom. That's a lot of frustrating emotions to go through in a day. Kitchen play sets, toys that involve different motor skills, and anything with music are gifts that can let toddlers feel in charge without too much assistance from a parent — making them ideal for this age category.

Potentially a more daunting phase than the terrible twos: The one where the pressure to be cool is relentless. This is that age. Though "cool" can lend itself to multiple interpretations, gifts that contribute to an older kid's evolving critical thinking skills and sense of independence, responsibility, and style are all smart ideas. These could be anything from STEM toys to beginner's makeup tools to a fitness tracker or a pretend pet that encourages routine-making. Anything that involves an app, tablet, or e-reader is also a guaranteed win.

Finding a gift that a teenager will think is cool (and that will be on-trend for longer than a few months) might be some of the trickiest shopping you'll do all year. They'll probably offer up a few big softball suggestions, but if you're going the surprise route, remember that they care about brands. Thanks to social media influencers promoting products directly to teens on Instagram or TikTok, Gen Z kids are loyal to very specific brands of tech, accessories, and even water bottles. If you follow the trends as closely as they do (trust us, we do), cracking the "best gifts for teenagers" code isn't rocket science.

#541. Best Gifts for Hikers, Backpackers, Outdoorsy People (2026)

Buying gifts for the outdoor enthusiast is damn near impossible. Your hiking/camping/cycling outdoorsy friends are often serious gearheads, meticulously poring over reviews, guides, and the perennial wisdom of ounce-counting Reddit users to find the exact right thing.

Don’t stress trying to pick that exact right thing yourself. The chance of you figuring it out and getting it for them as a gift is exactly zero. That doesn’t mean you should punt and buy an REI gift card—although they might like that, especially with REI’s various sales throughout the year. But gift cards are lame. Instead, get them something they totally didn’t expect: one of these fun, useful, sometimes whimsical things that are almost guaranteed to not only delight them but actually get used outdoors.

And what about you? While you’re here, don’t you need to replace your sleeping pad? Don’t forget to check out the rest of our buying guides, including our Best Sleeping Bags guide, our Best Barefoot Shoes guide, and our Best Merino Wool guide.

Updated November 2026: I’ve added some nice pants, a fire-starting kit, a Nalgene pressurizer kit, and some cast iron pans for cooking over an open flame. But I’m sticking with the socks as our top pick. If you get nothing else, just get the socks. Trust me.

Yes, you read that right. Socks. SOCKS! You should gift socks. Outdoorsy people can never have too many socks. We’re hard on socks, which almost guarantees this gift will see some use. What your outdoorsperson especially needs is merino wool socks. Merino wool is a super fiber. It will change their lives. Did you know you can wear merino wool for several days in a row without smelling like you just spent two weeks at a Phish reunion? Just kidding, Phish fans. But it’s true. Merino wool will change your friend or loved one’s backpacking life. It’s comfortable, has virtually no odor no matter how long you wear it, and helps your body thermoregulate, staying warmer when it’s cold, cooler when it’s warm.

Darn Tough socks in particular are comfortable, stand up to considerable abuse, and have some of the highest merino wool content of anything we’ve tested. They also come in a nearly endless array of fun colors and designs. If you want something warmer, I love these Minus 33 Expedition Weight merino socks ($24).

Opinel’s classic folding knife practically begs to be taken on a hike with some good hard chorizo, soft Spanish cheese, and a nice baguette. It’s the first step to hiking, European style. Or at least how I imagine Europeans hike (always with delicious cheese, cured meats, and cheap wine—please don’t ruin my vision, European readers). I got my Opinel as a gift from a friend, and I love it. And I am an outdoor person, so I am living proof that this is a good gift.

Opinel makes dozens of variations of this knife to choose from when gifting. Grab them a kit with a bento box, or a nature-loving friend might like the version with handles designed by different artists. The one version I don’t recommend is the carbon steel. The material is great, but in this case, when you’re throwing it in your backpack and possibly forgetting about it for long periods, carbon steel will rust. Stick with stainless steel when gifting this.

I would put the Bic lighter in the top 10 best technologies of all time. For most of human history, being able to flick your thumb and produce a flame would have been considered utterly miraculous. If you (or whoever you’re gifting) want to fully understand just how miraculous a Bic is (or matches, for that matter), spend some time with this flint-and-striker fire-starting kit. Townsends makes historical gear (serving the reenactment community, among others), and this kit approximates how you would have made fire around the time of the Revolutionary War up through, well, the invention of matches. Let me tell you, dear reader, it isn’t easy. That said, it was a fun skill to develop, and I especially recommend it if you have kids (with supervision, of course). There’s immense satisfaction the first time you get a fire going with nothing more than this. Bonus points if you then cook over that fire (see below).

Being outdoors and cooking over a fire are intrinsically linked in my mind, having grown up in a family that didn’t consider it camping if some cast iron pot of beans wasn’t simmering over the fire. I’ve tested a slew of cast iron pans over the years, but these are my favorite for cooking on an open flame. They’re thick-bottomed—which means a more even heat—but not overly heavy thanks to the thinner side wall. That lightness makes them easier to maneuver over a fire (and it’s also nice when you’re cooking at home on a stove).

Lancaster’s cast iron has a smooth finish, which gives these pans a much more nonstick surface than rough pans (not that rough pans can't be plenty nonstick, but they do take more seasoning effort on your part). This is especially nice when cooking over an open flame, where burns happen somewhat more frequently (at least while you’re learning). This one pairs well with the cookbook below.

Cooking on Fire is a gorgeous book of recipes and techniques for cooking over an open flame. It offers a good mix of recipes, from simple, delicious veggies to slow-cooked meats that take hours. It also includes plenty of background on different types of fires and cooking techniques, plus all the recommended equipment (for example: spits, forked sticks, cast iron pans, and so on). It’s everything you—er, sorry, your outdoorsy friend—need to start cooking over a fire.

I really want to try the fire-inside-a-log technique pictured on the cover, but I haven’t gotten around to it yet. So far I’ve only had a chance to make the grilled pork belly, with grilled carrots and “Krabbelurer” griddle cakes for dessert. All of them were excellent, though of course, perhaps that universal rule applies more so here than with any other form of cooking: your results may vary. In the end, though, this isn’t really a gift about cooking. It’s a gift to remind us all to slow down and take our time, with food and everything else.

This technically violates my suggestion to avoid useless stuff, but this thing is super cool, so I'm making an exception (plus no one seems to know about it, so I have to get the word out). Lunatec’s shower mist lid gizmo turns your Nalgene into a pressurized water bottle. This is extremely handy when camping. I use this primarily to rinse dishes when there’s no other water available, but it can indeed function as a tiny shower, a mist sprayer for cooling down on hot days, and, as any child will quickly discover, a pretty darn powerful squirt gun. Probably the best part is that these take up hardly any room. I bought two and threw them in my camping gear, so I have them whenever I need them.

I’m always on the lookout for the perfect pair of pants. Many come close, and I would add these new Marmot pants to the short list. They’re 98 percent cotton, with the rest being Elastane for a little stretch. The knees are lightly articulated, and you probably could, as Marmot suggests on its website, climb in them, though I have not. Instead, I have hiked in them and found them very comfortable and just about the right fabric weight for cool (but not freezing) fall days. My one gripe is that there’s only one inseam size: 32, which makes sense because all humans are the same height, right? Well, if you or your giftee is around 6 feet tall, you’re in luck. If not, get them professionally hemmed or pass on these and go with the merino T-shirt below.

This book changed how my kids and I see the world. I originally bought it because I thought my kids might like it (which they did), but then I stole it, read it cover to cover, bought a sketchbook, and, following along with the book, started to see the world in much more detail than I ever had before. Don’t worry if your giftee is not an artist. I am the opposite of skilled when it comes to drawing, and I still love this book. More than drawing, this book teaches you to look closely, observe and record the world around you, and form your own understanding of what you’ve witnessed.

If you want to put together a kind of “curiosity kit” gift package based on the one in the book, pick up a hand lens (I like this one because of the way it blocks out the light, though there are higher-quality lenses), some nice pens, and a millimeter ruler to go along with it. You could also check out our guide to journals and notebooks, though those tend to be very personal choices and might not be the best gift unless you know exactly what your giftee wants.

VSSL’s Java G25 grinder is nearly indestructible. I’ve been using mine every morning for about five years, most of that time living on the road. I’ve dropped it on concrete, gravel, and more, and it’s still going strong. In the same time, two glass grinders and one plastic grinder have broken and failed me. I know what you’re thinking: it is expensive, but it’s an investment, and it lasts. Finally, this summer, a thin piece of metal that surrounds the grinder gave way. I contacted VSSL (as a regular customer), and they sent out a replacement immediately, making this a Buy It for Life contender.

VSSL started life making ultra-durable camping tools—waterproof canisters with flashlights, compasses, first-aid kits, and more. The Java G25 comes from that same lineage, made of aircraft-grade aluminum and built to survive the zombie apocalypse—or a bumpy ride to your favorite campsite. Despite all that, it’s still relatively lightweight (about 14 ounces; not heavy, but you wouldn’t bring it backpacking). The handle is clever: it folds out for great leverage while you grind and can also act as a hook to hang the Java when you’re not using it. The conical burrs inside produce a nice, even grind that covers the full spectrum of brew possibilities, from French-press coarse to espresso-fine.

What is camping without brewing coffee over a morning fire? And what is coffee if not from a moka pot? This is the classic Bialetti that all other moka pots are based on. It’s made of heavy-gauge aluminum that heats evenly and can handle being placed directly on the coals (make sure your outdoorsy person puts it on the edge of the fire to avoid melting the handle). It’ll brew about a 4-ounce cup of very dark coffee (not technically espresso since it’s not under pressure, but it’s as close as you’ll get outdoors).

If your outdoor person isn’t a moka pot lover, other coffee options include this exquisite titanium French press from Snow Peak. It weighs just 6.3 ounces, so you can even take it on the trail if you’re dedicated to your coffee. Then there’s the tried-and-true Aeropress Go, which you can use in a variety of ways, pack on the trail, and use in a hotel room—and it makes all-around good coffee.

This 3-inch folding metal box, designed to burn twigs efficiently, has inspired almost religious devotion. Firebox stoves come in a variety of shapes and sizes, but my favorite is the smallest, the Nano, which I’ve used for years as both a wood stove and a gas stove—it’s what I use with the moka pot above—and even as a grill (also an alcohol stove).

It’s my top pick for backcountry cooking. I suggest this as a gift because outdoorsy people are practical, and they won’t buy it for themselves because they won’t see the benefits until you buy it for them. With the Firebox Nano, you can build a small fire out of twigs and cook a steak on it. Who wouldn’t love that? It’s an amazing thing to realize how little fuel it actually takes to cook something. Grill asparagus, shrimp, homemade tortillas. I have done all of this, and more. Beyond practicality, it is fun.

Did I mention that I love merino wool? Everyone needs more of it, especially the outdoorsperson. Wool keeps you warm when it’s cold and cool when it’s warm. In a comparison study against other fabrics (on runners), the wool fabric shirt performed best in both hot and cold conditions. It’s effective because merino wool offers insulation, breathability, and moisture-wicking thermoregulation. Wool is also odor-resistant and very comfortable, which is just icing on the cake.

These T-shirts from Unbound are my favorite of the merino shirts I’ve tested. These are the softest, best-made, best-fitting T-shirts I’ve ever worn, merino or otherwise. They’re incredibly versatile. I’ve worn them doing everything from backpacking in the summer heat to rolling jiu-jitsu to sitting around the fire on cool autumn evenings. I also like that they don’t have a “sporty” cut or stitching. They’re just … T-shirts.

Look, gifting anyone an electric bike is absurdly generous. But an ebike is a great way to enjoy the outdoors in ways you normally wouldn’t. Commuting, grocery runs, school pickup, errands around town—everything becomes a mini outdoor adventure on a bike (at least compared to using a car or train). The Lectric XP4 750 is the most popular ebike on the market, largely because of it’s low price, long range, powerful motor, and brand recognition. It’s easy to assemble, rides low and stable, and folds in half for more compact storage. —Harry Rabinowitz

Traveler’s Notebooks are legendary, and for outdoorsy people who like to record their travels or observations, this is a wonderful place to do it. Originally released by the Japanese stationery company Midori, the simple, elegant leather cover with its myriad inserts proved so popular that it was spun off as its own company. The leather cover is really just that—a leather binding that you can fill with whatever notebook (or notebooks) you want. For example, I use the process described here to keep three notebooks within the covers of my traveler’s notebook. I have one dot-grid notebook for planning, one plain notebook as my journal, and one heavier-paper notebook for sketching and using watercolors to nature journal.

I will confess I used to dislike the slightly odd size. The full-size Traveler’s Notebook is 8.7 by 4.9 inches, which is slightly narrower than A5. It’s a little different, but I’ve grown to love it. There’s also a “passport” size at 5.3 by 3.9 inches. Note that there are many imitators. I would avoid the cheap Amazon knockoffs; it’s a gift after all.

Chances are, unless your giftee is an outdoor newcomer (in which case, by all means, buy them some gear), they may well have everything they need. Rather than buying something that just increases their environmental footprint and adds to the stuff in their closet, consider making a donation in their name.

You might want to ask them if they have a favorite environmental organization (this is much less awkward to work into a conversation than asking what their favorite handlebar rack is for gravel rides), but if not, check your local outdoor shop for regional groups. These are often your best bet for turning money into action, and they often need the money more. If nothing quite fits the bill, I’ll plug my two favorite groups, the Nature Conservancy and Surfrider, both of which do good work protecting land and water around the world.

#539. Using miles to fly for Thanksgiving? These are the most expensive and cheapest routes

Airline ticket prices have surged this year, especially for travel over the holidays. Don't expect many bargains if you're booking flights with miles, either.

Tickets bought with frequent flyer miles for flights between large U.S. airports over the Thanksgiving travel period will cost 32% more than last year's holiday, according to data from Seats.aero, a miles and points redemption search site. While fares have skyrocketed this year, airline executives last month said demand remains strong.

Airlines have largely gotten rid of static mileage redemption charts in favor of dynamic pricing, making budgeting for an award ticket more difficult. Dynamic pricing means redemption costs change depending on demand, routes and other factors.

Many of the most expensive routes this Thanksgiving are understandably longer, including from Hawaii, but the list also includes a nonstop from New York to Houston.

Jet fuel prices have surged since the U.S. and Israel attacked Iran on Feb. 28, which has eaten into airline profits. Carriers have sought to pass costs along to consumers as much as possible.

Even some of the cheapest routes for award tickets are more expensive than last year, including for short routes like Tulsa, Oklahoma, to Dallas Fort Worth International Airport.

#534. Louis Rossmann Wants More People to Care About the Right to Repair

I love my old iPhone 14, but I’m starting to face the facts: It’s going to die. The battery is about to go; apps take longer to load than they used to. Some of it I can fix. I can go to the Genius Bar and swap the battery. Maybe I could even do it myself; that’d buy me some time. But eventually, Apple won’t support this wildly expensive device at all, and there’s not much I can do about it.

Apple did start offering self-repair services a few years ago, but it involves renting nearly 80 pounds of equipment and requires you to use official parts. When all is said and done, it doesn’t save you that much money versus just going to the Apple Store. But if I buy a screen or a camera component from a third party, there's a chance my iPhone is gonna lock me out of certain features because it doesn't recognize it.

This problem goes far beyond iPhones. Manufacturers have put similar locks on all kinds of gadgets, from video game consoles to cars to smart home devices, making them hard to modify and even harder to restore after some firmware update renders a certain feature unusable.

Not surprisingly, a lot of people think this isn't fair, that there should be a better way to do it. I think at this point it's safe to say it qualifies as a movement, one that unites under the banner of “right to repair.” Built around the radical idea that when you buy something you should be able to truly own it, right-to-repair advocates believe consumers have the right to maintain, fix, or modify any piece of hardware they’ve purchased. The movement has gained even more traction now that AI makes it even easier to hack the software powering consumers’ devices.

To find out what’s next for the right-to-repair movement, I went straight to the source: Louis Rossmann, a YouTuber and consumer rights advocate who recently organized the Own Your Shit tour, which went around the US earlier this year to call for the death of Section 1201 of the Digital Millennium Copyright Act, which prohibits users from bypassing digital restrictions put on devices by manufacturers.

Rossmann and I talked about more than just Section 1201. We also discussed the US midterm elections, putting out bounties to find product fixes, and what will happen as the right-to-repair movement collides with the rise of AI.

This interview has been edited for length and clarity.

BRIAN BARRETT: Louis, thank you so much for being here. You're someone who spends a lot of time not just evangelizing over “right to repair,’ but I would imagine introducing it to people who don't know what it is. How do you do that?

LOUIS ROSSMANN: I try to avoid talking about it, and I try to show people. So instead of telling people, “Here’s why you should care about this issue,” which sounds like lecturing, what I’ll do is if I’m done fixing something for a customer and they say, “The Apple Store told me $2,000," and we could do it for $200, when they’re giving me the hug and saying, “Thank you for getting back all of our baby pictures” or something, I’ll say, “Here’s how close you were to this never happening.”

I like to tell them, “This is the document that I used to fix your device.” When they ask me what was wrong with it, I’ll tell them, “By the way, you should know that Apple tells the company that makes this chip not to sell it to me, and if everybody in that chain listened, I would not be able to fix this, and you wouldn’t have any of your data back.” So I always try to show people and get them excited about what it is I’m doing.

I have over 1,000 repair videos on YouTube where I show people how to do what I do, and there are people that say, “I was working at Walmart for seven bucks an hour, and I picked up a soldering iron and started fixing boards on eBay, and now I make triple what I did before.”

There are people that fix their own things, so not just making careers for people, but maybe somebody saved themselves some money by doing a repair themself. Or when I fix something for somebody that otherwise wouldn't have been fixed or that they were told couldn’t be fixed. I always try to get people personally invested because lecturing people about it just doesn’t make it very fun.

Your organization has a wall of shame, almost, of examples of things that companies have done to take away the ownership of a product. Because that’s really what it is, right? It's like you buy something, you expect to be able to own it and do what you want with it, but that's not always how it plays out. To your mind, what are some of the more egregious instances of companies not letting you know what you want?

Some of my favorites are some of these thermostat companies. One of them sent out a firmware update after the company went bankrupt that required that you pay $117 a year to get some of the functionality that you had beforehand. So it’s not even necessarily when a company has a device that has a specific date when it’s gonna turn off.

It’s when they realize, “We need to make more money, so we’re gonna change what it is we’re offering you after the sale.”

When I started doing this, I was just talking about right to repair for Apple devices, which is what my small shop worked on. I saw it from my small lens. I focused on the items where I knew exactly what was going on, and I really thought, “This is a problem that only affects me.”

It wasn’t until about 2017 when I went to Nebraska for this legislative hearing that I realized that it really is not just something that affects my small field. I was in a room filled with farmers that were talking about all the problems they were having with their tractors, and I remember going, “Where’s the right-to-repair?” And someone says, “This is the right-to-repair room. Everybody has this problem now.” And that’s when I started opening my field of view up to everybody who has this issue.

As time went on, my channel stopped just discussing right to repair, and it started discussing how right to repair is a small element under this umbrella of ownership. And ownership being taken away is, in my opinion, the real problem. Your inability to fix what you own is one thing, but also the fact that everything is turning into a subscription, that the things that you buy must connect to the manufacturer’s server in order to work, which means that they can disable your product at any time.

It’s all small things under this large umbrella of ownership, and that’s slowly being taken away from people over the past 10 to 20 years.

I wanna go back to the farmers because they have been on the vanguard of this. They have been in a years-long struggle with John Deere specifically, trying to get the right to repair their own tractors. But there have been some breakthroughs recently. Do you mind talking through wins like that, like how either that movement or other movements have actually been able to make changes happen?

I think a lot of the change so far has been change that occurs at the cultural level more than the legislative level. Some of these changes have been memorandums of understanding where the company says, “OK, we won’t do this particular thing.”

When I speak to farmers, it’s very much the same version of what I deal with in my business, where you’ll see a headline where a company says they’re supporting repair, and then you actually look into the details of it, and it’s really very surface level.

One of the examples I have of this is with Samsung. They had released this independent repair program, and they made screens and batteries available, so you could buy a battery. But the battery cost over $200 because they glued the battery to the screen, and if you wanted to buy it, you had to buy both of them together.

A $200 to $250 battery is pretty useless to a lot of people who have a phone that’s worth $200 when it works. And one of the issues that I’ve found when I'm speaking to farmers is, at the end of the day, they still have the same problem that they did. They’re still using cracked Ukrainian software to be able to work on their tractor themselves without having to take it back to the dealer. So there’s still a lot of work to be done.

Right to repair is in the news more frequently than it used to be. Some outlets, like WIRED, have covered it for a while. Our writer Boone Ashworth has talked to you before and covered it quite a bit. But it seems to have reached national prominence in the last year or so. Is there an inflection point that you can point to?

I think it’s been a slow burn for the past 12 years. CBC News did a piece on Apple in 2018. That was a really fun one for me. I remember the producer came to me and said they wanted to do a piece on how Apple is bad with upgrades. I remember saying, “Upgrades are not very fun, but also the piece is starting with the conclusion. You’re starting with doing a piece on Apple being bad, and there’s no fun in that. What if you do this: Find a broken machine, take it to Apple, record what they say, take it to me, record what I say, and let the story write itself.” And they said, “Well, what if they give a good answer?” I’m just smirking like, “Just let the story write itself. Have faith.”

They took it to Apple. They said, “We gotta do the motherboard,” all this other stuff. I see that it’s a bent pin in a screen cable because whoever had worked on it before had likely tried to misroute the screen cable when they were plugging it into the board, so they were stretching it, and they shorted pin one to ground, which caused the backlight to go out. So I take a tweezer, bend it back, and it works. It was featured on national news.

There’s some other inflection points, like in 2016 when Apple had asked if I would take down one of my videos showing where a fuse was. There was a video I did showing you how to fix a MacBook Air if you get some liquid around the trackpad, and your trackpad and keyboard stop working. There’s a zero-ohm resistor. It’s kinda like a fuse that will blow in that area so that the entire machine doesn’t die.

So I showed how to replace this thing. At 55 seconds in the video, I’m showing where the fuse is in the motherboard, and they asked me to take the video down, and I’m like, “I don’t want to. File a DMCA claim.” That got some attention. I started a nonprofit to try and do this more professionally and push this forward in a more organized manner.

I think a lot of people might say, “That sounds good, and I’m behind it,” but the idea of a fuse, a motherboard, those are things people aren’t always comfortable messing with. It sounds intimidating to someone who’s not steeped in tech. Is that so in practice? Has it become easier to fix stuff even as companies make it harder?

The right to repair doesn’t mean that you have to fix something, nor does it mean that you have to go incredibly deep to fix the issue. If you’re the type of person that wants to pay somebody else to fix it, I respect that. If you wanna replace the entire display assembly, meaning the entire lid on the laptop because that’s easier for you, and you’re willing to pay an extra $100 or $200 to buy the screen preassembled, go for it.

If you wanna replace the LCD cell, which is just the front layer that makes the picture, which is absolutely miserably difficult to do—but you can buy that part sometimes for 30 to 50 bucks—do that too. I am not telling people that they should have to fix anything, and I’ll never shame somebody because they decide to buy something new or have somebody else fix it.

Right to repair simply means that you have the right to fix it if you want, or send it to the manufacturer, send it to a third party, or buy something new if you don’t want to fix it. Ironically, most of the devices in my house that don't work, I don't wind up fixing them. I wind up buying something new, because after 12 or 15 hours a day of fixing other people’s stuff, I just don’t have it in me to fix my own.

The wall of laptops I see behind you are a testament to that.

So recently, for instance, my air conditioner broke, and I was willing to pay somebody to fix it, and he ghosted me three times. And the company said, “We don't fix them. We can give you a new one for $10,000.” And I look at the cost of the unit that I have and it’s $2,500, and I’m just like, “What?” So I opened it up and I realized the contactor, which is a relay, it’s the thing that every time your thermostat says “turn on the AC” it sends 24 volts to this thing, and then that turns on the 240 volts going to the AC. It was filled, absolutely filled, with fire ants. I don’t know why fire ants love 240 volts. They really love it even though it’s not good for them. And that was kind of fun for me. So at that moment, I wanted to fix it because I was boiling hot, and it was taking forever for somebody else to.

Now in this case, there was not a lot that was stopping me. My unit is so old that I was actually able to find the schematics for it online, so if I wanted to buy parts for it, I could. And that's really all most people are asking for. I'm not saying that you have to do a motherboard repair yourself, but it would be nice if somebody who's qualified to do so is not blocked from being able to do so.

Now, it’s not Apple’s job to make it easy for me. It’s not their job to design their product in a way where I have a more luxurious experience while I’m doing the job. My job is gonna be difficult, and that's part of the game. Technology is consistently evolving.

Where right to repair comes in is when I try to talk to the electronics distributor that sells all these chips, and I say, “I wanna buy this,” and they say, “You’re not on the list. We can’t sell to you.” That’s where right to repair comes in and says that’s not OK.

Companies will say—and I know this because they say it to us all the time when we go to them with stories about this—that, fundamentally, a lot of this is about quality control. If we let you use an unauthorized part, we’re not gonna guarantee that the device is gonna perform up to our standards. If we let you tinker around with this, you might break it. We want to guarantee a certain level of quality with the experience of our devices. How does that reasoning sit with you?

If the issue is quality control, then why does my store have a better rating on Google Maps than every single Apple Store in the country?

What is right to repair asking for? Right to repair very often asks for the ability to buy the same part that they are using. I want to be able to buy the parts that go into the device.

We’ve seen stories in recent weeks where now that people have Claude Code they can use it to check their smart TVs and get rid of all the bloatware and all the adware and have a more streamlined smart TV experience. I think those tools also give them access to guides to figure out how to replace all kinds of things, how to fix software in all kinds of ways. Is part of the future of owning our things also making it easier to not just tinker with the hardware aspect of it, but to get into the software and make it more in line with what we want out of our experiences and give companies less control over the advertising, the spyware, whatever it is?

I love it. I love seeing people being able to use AI in that way. I love the idea of it being used to break digital locks, that you could just get access to what the company has taken away from you. I think that that is a great use of AI. The only issue there is when you’re talking about ownership, when people are using these frontier models, when they’re using Claude Code or Codex you’re using a model that runs on somebody else’s computer, and if you type the wrong thing, it’s easy to get around some of Claude Code’s guardrails, and sometimes I’ve done some experiments to have fun with it. But there are a lot of guardrails on it, and I wouldn’t be surprised if at some point in the future, enough of these companies started saying, “Hey, people are using Claude Code to actually free themselves from our bullshit. Can you stop that?” And them stopping it.

This is where it’s nice to see that it’s not just the US leading in this. Many other countries are creating their own AI models that can run on your system. I’m very excited to see what happens, as time goes on, when models that can run on a standard laptop like this have the ability to actually do this.

You know, when people are remaking Adobe Suite with Claude Code, I wouldn’t be surprised if one or two years from now they can’t do that at all because it’s able to detect, “Hey, you’re actually trying to free yourself from Adobe.”

But what I’m hoping to see happen is that there are enough local models that run on your system that you don’t have to worry about some company that gets to tell you what you can do, being able to help people get around all these types of digital locks and bullshit. I don’t think that that’s gonna happen in the next year or two, but I think it’s coming, and I’m excited to see it.

This does lead us to the fact those digital locks and bullshit are in legally murky territory sometimes, right? Or at least companies can claim legal defenses. I’m talking specifically about Section 1201 of the Digital Millennium Copyright Act, something that you do not like, and I think it’s fair to say you want to see it gone for good.

Do you mind telling us a little bit about what Section 1201 of the DMCA is and how it ties into this conversation and makes it a little bit more fraught to do that unlocking?

Section 1201 of the DMCA makes it illegal to bypass a technical protection measure, otherwise known as a digital lock, to gain access to a protected work, even if it’s for the purpose of repair. It was put in place to try and stop piracy, and it was put together in one of the most corrupt ways possible.

Congress repeatedly said no to this law. No, this is way too restrictive. So the architect of it, Bruce Lehman, decided to try and get it negotiated into an international treaty. Then he came back to the United States and said, “Look, we signed this treaty, so now you have to pass this law. We’re going to be out of step with our obligations to the international community.” So he’s potentially sidestepped Congress in order to get this law passed that says that you cannot bypass a digital lock, even if it’s on something that you own.

Now, I can’t go to your house and break into your home. That’s illegal. I should not be allowed to do that. But if I spend $600,000 on a house, and I notice that there’s a locked closet in the house, once I’ve paid $600,000 for the house, I should be able to break that lock to get into that closet, because I own the house.

The problem with this law is that it allows companies to lock you out of things that you have already paid for. And the way that you see this being abused now is when you see companies taking away features from you or sending out firmware updates that stop features from working on the device after the company has gone bankrupt and they figure out, “Oh, wait, we need more money.”

I’ve had companies that failed before, and I really wish I had the ability to say, “You know what? I realize that I sold it at a price that’s a little too low. I’m gonna need to take $5 from every customer I had in the past year.” I’m not allowed to do that. I can’t stop my customers’ products from working.

But because I was selling a physical good that did not have any sort of firmware on it that connects to the internet, I’m not able to do that. Section 1201 of the DMCA is a law that came out in the late 1990s. This was before most normal people even had the internet, much less had their washing machine, their television, their dryer attached to the internet like they are now.

That law was, in my opinion, horribly put together for the world of the mid- to late-1990s, and it is way worse built for today. The architect of the law also said that the purpose of it was to try and fight piracy, and he admitted in 2007 and 2013 when he was looking back on it that it didn’t do that at all.

It’s very easy right now. I was able to show my 79-year-old father how to pirate television, movies, music, and it took me less than 10 minutes to show him how to use something like SoulSeek or Usenet to be able to get whatever he wants. These laws fundamentally have not worked, but what they’ve done a very good job of is making it much more difficult for people who wanna follow the law to be able to use their media.

If I want to rip my legally purchased Blu-ray and be able to play it on my legally purchased phone, I have to break the digital lock inside the Blu-ray disc to do that, which is illegal. What I’m actually supposed to do is I’m supposed to buy another version of it that’s only gonna work on here, and it makes it much more difficult for people who actually want to obey the law.

I think people are now waking up to this, but software is copyrightable. So when you buy a book on Amazon Kindle, say, and you have a Kindle book, what you’re actually doing is you’re signing an end-user licensing agreement that gives you the right to access that book.

You don’t own that book in a technical way, even though it feels like you should and feels like you do. So we’ve had cases where Amazon has actually removed books from Kindles after you bought them, and there’s no recourse for that. I think the idea of ownership in the digital age is informed by so much of Section 1201 and the way that we approach copyright and software.

Do you see any progress towards changing that?

Instead of every single year going back and asking for an exemption, what we’d really like to do is we’d like to pass durable legislation that doesn’t require that we ask for an exemption. We’re starting with the area that is the most reasonable, which is repair. If you want to be able to fix what you bought and paid for, that should not be a crime.

You should be able to repair what you own, and you should be able to show other people how to repair what they own without that being considered a copyright crime. We’re not even saying, “Can I please rip my legally purchased Blu-ray so that I could watch it on my legally purchased portable media player?” Right now, we’re simply saying, “I would like to fix what I bought and paid for.”

The problem that I’ve experienced throughout this process is there’s always an issue that people find more exciting, that’s more accessible, because you can only focus on so many things at once. If people are concerned about the economy, culture-war issues, if somebody’s concerned about immigration or Covid or policing—there’re so many issues that resonate far more with the voting public than this. The right to repair is this really abstract issue, and when you start talking about digital locks—my job, as I see it, is to get ordinary people to understand why this acronym and set of numbers directly ties into your thermostat saying it’s not gonna work anymore unless you give them $10 a month.

I don’t think that I’ve done a good enough job of that over the past 12 years to get it on par with all these other issues that people face every day. Admittedly, if you’re paying like $5, $6 a gallon for gas, you’re probably gonna care about that before you care about this.

So we’re talking not long after the midterms, and Democrats have won control over the Senate and the House. You do have a couple of politicians who actually have been speaking out about the right to repair who are in office now: Josh Turek in Iowa; Jake Johnson in Minnesota looks like he’s gonna get a seat. Is it encouraging that it’s even an issue people are talking about, or is that still not enough?

It’s encouraging to see that they’re talking about it, but I also realize that, especially going into this next Congress, there is going to be so much work and so many things that they’re going to want to do. I remember somebody asking me, “Like, no offense Louis, but I’d rather see them spend time on, I don't know, getting rid of Pete Hegseth.” I never tell people my issue is more important than your issue, and everybody votes according to what is important to them.

But there’s so many things that I imagine this Congress is going to want to do when they get elected that my job is gonna be making sure that there’s enough of a base of support and showing that there’s enough people that care about this, that if you spend the 20 minutes to do something about this, that it will actually work in your favor.

And that’s part of what we’ve been trying to do with the FULU [Freedom from Unethical Limitations on Users] Own Your Shit tour over the past few months. We’ve traveled around the country to 13 different cities, and in every one of these cities, we are trying to bring people together.

We had one of the ex-FTC commissioners show up, which was really cool. He gave an inspiring speech in San Jose. We’re also inviting their congresspeople to say, “Yeah, this is an issue that I will actually be more likely to vote for. This is as important to me as the other issues.” And our job is showing the people that are elected that we care about this. Because what’s their job? Their job is to represent us. If people are not talking about this issue or it's only being talked about on a couple of subreddits or on the fringes of the internet, I almost don't blame the representative for not prioritizing it. But our job is to show them that if they prioritize it, they'll be rewarded for it.

So I am happy to see that there are people talking about it. I remember when right to repair was put into an executive order by Biden in summer of 2021. I was excited, but there wasn’t a lot that came from it. I’m hoping for something different here.

So FULU is your organization that supports right to repair. You also have bounties that you pay out for people who can disable these digital locks. How is that going? Have you successfully paid out bounties yet? Can you talk about that program a little bit and why you're sort of taking matters into your own hands here?

That program came out about 2:00 or 3:00 in the morning. I was doing a video on Futurehome after somebody who works there, or used to work there, sent me information on what they were doing. He was talking about this thermostat that when it got a firmware update after the company went bankrupt, it stopped you from being able to use some of the features you could use before.

I did the video going over it, and I’m just sitting there at, like, 2 am editing this and thinking, “This is not enough.” And I said, “I'll give $5,000 or $10,000 to whoever makes this thing work again.” I was thinking back to what I was doing when I was doing all these repair videos showing the schematics.

Now you have hundreds of people doing repair videos that are way better than mine every single day on YouTube, and I love it. But back in 2013 and 2014, I didn’t see anything like that, and that’s because at the bottom right corner of the screen, it has this little message on the schematic. It says, “Proprietary property of Apple Inc. Keep confidential. Do not share.” Most people don’t like getting sued or having their livelihoods destroyed. For me, that’s like a Tuesday. I find it fun. I enjoy going to court. I encourage my employees to go to court.

So I was thinking, “How do I get more people to start poking at these things? How do I get more people to start saying, ‘No, this is not enough. You can’t do that.’” So we came up with this bounty, and you could see in that video that most of it was done during the day, and the part with the bounty was done at 3:00 in the morning in some corner of my house when I’m dead tired.

I said, “5,000 bucks to whoever makes this work again.” And there was an employee that emailed me with a solution shortly thereafter, and he said, “Louis, I appreciate this. I would’ve done this for free, but here I made a Home Assistant integration,” and people were able to use the thermostat again.

So what we’re trying to do with the bounty program is we’re trying to show people that, A, this is a problem, and B, we’re trying to incentivize people to start making things work again so that we can point to how bad this law is. We can also point to things that tangibly work again. The reason that the right-to-repair stuff with Apple that I was doing was successful is because I wasn’t just saying, “Here’s why you should care about this.”

Millions of people probably have working MacBooks again because of those videos that I was doing back in the day. So when somebody personally saves $1,000, when they personally get their data back, they are now invested in my cause in a way that no Twitter hashtag and no bumper sticker is ever gonna make happen.

What’s a gateway drug into right to repair? If you want to get into repairing your own stuff, what is an easy thing? Is it an iPhone battery replacement or a trackpad upgrade? What do you point people to and say, “This is something that you can actually do. This is something you can manage. It’s gonna make your life better. It’s gonna make your experience better”?

I don't have a very well-packaged answer for that. For me, it it has to be something fun. It has to be, It's 103 degrees in Texas this week, and my asshole air conditioning technician has stiffed me three times. Let me open it up and, oh, I just used compressed air on the contactor and I blew out the fire ants, so now it works again. That’s what’s gonna get people excited about this. I get excited about repair not because of the repair that’s being done, and admittedly not even the money, it’s the story that’s involved in it.

So anytime you’re fixing something because I need this to work right now, or I don't have the money to make this work again, anytime there’s some sort of story attached to it, it’s going to be something that gets you excited.

Some people may feel incredibly excited about replacing the battery in their phone. Some people are really just gonna hate it. But I think there has to be some sort of story behind it that makes it worth it. I actually don’t find repair fun. I don't think that putting 96 solder balls back onto a chip that's the size of my pinky fingernail is fun.

I don’t do it for that. I do it for the hug that the customer gives you when you get back all their baby pictures, or when you do this for the police and they're able to catch the murderer because of it. I don’t find taking a battery out of a phone, using a heat gun, and using a little spudger to get all this off, but not breaking the screen—that’s not fun.

It’s fun if you can’t afford a new one. That makes it a lot of fun. It’s fun if you’re doing it to get something back. But there has to be a story. There has to be a reason that makes that particular repair exciting to you, and that's gonna be different for everybody, and it’s usually gonna be based on whatever your circumstances are at the time.

#532. Samsung Galaxy S27 and S27 Ultra camera specs reportedly leaked

If you were waiting for the upcoming Samsung Galaxy S27 lineup to drop before upgrading your smartphone, we have some bad news, particularly if you were hoping for some premium camera updates.

According to a new leak from Samsung news outlet SammyGuru, Samsung doesn't seem to have any major camera upgrades in store for the upcoming line of devices.

Another recent leak suggested that the Samsung Galaxy S27 line would see a big camera redesign. However, based on the SammyGuru report, there may not be too much new when it comes to the cameras themselves — at least for the S27 and S27 Plus models.

In fact, if you have a Samsung Galaxy S26 and are considering an upgrade to the Galaxy S27 or Galaxy S27 Plus, it looks like there will be no change to the cameras at all. Both the S27 and S27 Plus will reportedly be equipped with a 50MP main camera with a Sony IMX955 or Samsung S5KGNG sensor, SammyGuru reported. The ultrawide lens will reportedly be the same 12MP Sony IMX564, as well as the same 10MP 3x telephoto lens and 12MP selfie camera. The Galaxy S27 Pro and Galaxy S27 Ultra, thankfully, will see some upgrades, according to the new report. However, at this point, it's unclear just how much of an upgrade they'll be.

#530. The Grindr 1 Percent Is Getting Its Own Gated Community

In a reddit AMA on Friday, in between jokes about gay bears and JD Vance’s potential username being CushionCasanova, Grindr CEO George Arison answered a question about the economy. He didn’t hold back.

“Inflation is terrible, and there’s a massive affordability crisis in this country that our government has ignored since at least 2020,” he wrote in the r/wallstreetbets subreddit, where, following the release of the company’s quarterly earnings report, he took questions from users.

It was a startling admission from the man whose company is about to release a subscription offering intended to price out most Grindr users. On November 17, Edge, a “premium tier” with some AI-powered features costing $120 a month, will be available to members worldwide, according to the report. Early access opened today.

The document suggests that Edge will help users find connections “with less effort” and will include a personalized recommendation feature, Insights, to “understand compatibility and the likelihood of a reply,” as well as Reconnect, which “brings past connections back into view.” The app will have an entirely new design, interface, and icon intended to create a different experience for users willing to foot the high price tag.

But even if you can afford it, you may not get in. As with Raya, an invite-only app where some people have been on the wait list for as long as seven years, the company is planning to turn Edge into a digital gated community of sorts: Sign-ups will be limited to 1 percent of global monthly users.

The goal is to “deliberately [manage] the growth of the Edge community to preserve exclusivity” for “a select community” who can pay “meaningfully more,” the report indicated. According to figures from the company, only 1.4 million of its current 16 million users pay to use Grindr.

Edge’s official rollout comes following pilot programs in Australia, New Zealand, Canada, and the US, and a year’s worth of speculation about how much it would cost and what exactly would separate it from other apps that provide comparable features at a lower price.

“These VIP tiers make me think of [it as a] digital version of expensive hustler bars,” one user said on Instagram. “The men who will pay hundreds for access are rarely the hot men you would log on for.”

“Grindr is a top-tier example of enshitification and predatory monetization,” one person wrote in the reddit AMA from last week. “You need to stop with the intrusive ads and start getting in touch with reality,” posted another.

In September, the company agreed to a $35 million class action settlement over allegedly sharing users’ HIV status and testing information with third-party vendors; the details of the settlement included no admission of liability on Grindr’s behalf. That same month, Grindr acquired the telehealth company Freddie, which helps people get access to the HIV-prevention medication PrEP. The purchase builds on Arison’s investment into health care and turning the app into a social network. Currently, Grindr sells erectile dysfunction pills and GLP-1 weight-loss medication through its Woodwork program.

Critics will likely argue that pivoting to luxury dating in an economy where more and more people are struggling isn’t great optics, but Arison is betting that improved features and a “deliberately limited membership” will create opportunity for “substantially higher pricing over time,” the report noted.

#526. Wordle today: Answer, hints for November 11, 2026

Today's Wordle answer should be easy to solve if you're spiritual.

If you just want to be told today's word, you can jump to the bottom of this article for today's Wordle solution revealed. But if you'd rather solve it yourself, keep reading for some clues, tips, and strategies to assist you.

Originally created by engineer Josh Wardle as a gift for his partner, Wordle rapidly spread to become an international phenomenon, with thousands of people around the globe playing every day. Alternate Wordle versions created by fans also sprang up, including battle royale Squabble, music identification game Heardle, and variations like Dordle and Quordle that make you guess multiple words at once.

Wordle eventually became so popular that it was purchased by the New York Times, and TikTok creators even livestream themselves playing.

One tip is to select a word that includes at least two different vowels, plus some common consonants like S, T, R, or N.

It might feel like Wordle is getting harder, but it actually isn't any more difficult than when it first began. You can turn on Wordle's Hard Mode if you're after more of a challenge, though.
