#525. Ring’s Indoor Drone Camera Is Back—and Now Powered by AI

No one's home tonight, but the sensor on your home office window upstairs alerts your phone: “motion detected.” Maybe it's just your cat or a flicker of a passing car’s headlights. Instead of shrugging it off, you ask Alexa to send your Ring Drone Cam to check it out.

The tiny, 165-gram drone lifts out of its charging dock, buzzing loudly, and autonomously flies upstairs, giving you a first-person view on your phone’s screen as it scans the room. Since it's dark in the house, the Drone's spotlights kick in, making it look like someone's physically in the home and moving around—a potential deterrent to would-be thieves. All clear? It heads back to its base to recharge.

That's the peace of mind Ring wants to offer with its latest home security solution, which it announced today for $250, alongside the Ring Rover Cam, a $1,000 roving security robot designed to help businesses monitor warehouses and factories. Both fall under the company's new Ring Robotics brand. The Rover Cam launches first on December 16, followed by the Drone Cam on January 27.

The Ring Drone Cam flies out of its charging station to monitor the home.

This isn't the company's first at-home drone camera. CEO and founder Jamie Siminoff says he had the idea for a flying sentry in 2016 before Amazon bought Ring. The company previously announced an indoor security drone in 2020—the Ring Always Home Cam—and nearly launched it last year. But Siminoff says the team kept running into edge cases that forced them to add more sensors. Those additions required more computing resources and made the drone more power- and battery-hungry, which made it harder to fly. The solution? Move the intelligence off the vehicle and into the cloud.

The Drone Cam has enough smarts to land if it loses internet connectivity, but the rest of its intelligence now sits entirely in the cloud; the drone sends video straight to Ring’s servers, where a custom AI Pilot agent tells it where to go. That lets the drone be lightweight and nimble.

It will avoid people and objects, and if you accidentally stick a finger into the propellers, Siminoff says, you will feel it, but “you'll be fine.” If your cat manages to swat it out of the air, however, that's a different story. A Ring spokesperson tells WIRED customers shouldn't “fly it” if it's damaged and should contact support for help (no word on repairability). The Drone Cam runs a motor health check before take-off every time and won't fly if it detects a problem.

Users can't manually control the drone but can also set up custom flight routes and patrol schedules. It can automatically respond to alerts from security sensors you may have set up in the home, like motion, door, or window sensors. It will not leave your home, as it needs a Wi-Fi network to operate and uses the Alexa Home Map you may have created with existing Echo devices to help it navigate. It can fly five to eight minutes per charge. (The final production model will be black, not white like in the photos.)

“There are times when you do want to just be able to see everywhere around the home, but you don't necessarily want a [fixed] camera," Siminoff says. “That's why we invented Ring Robotics.”

Because it doesn't work outdoors and since it's well below the 250-gram threshold, the Drone Cam doesn't need to be registered with the Federal Aviation Administration. Drones have also struggled in the US after the Federal Communications Commission banned DJI from importing foreign-made drones without certification. But Siminoff says the Drone Cam complies with all US regulations and is assembled in Texas.

But what if a bad actor gets access to the Drone Cam? Siminoff points to the company's TAKE technology, or Throw Away the Key Encryption. It encrypts video in transit and rotates keys every few minutes, but Ring temporarily holds the key to run cloud-based AI features before deleting its copy. That brief server-side access window makes it less foolproof than true end-to-end encryption. (Every Ring account requires two-factor authentication to log in.)

Hackers have also invaded smart home devices and peeked through robot vacuum cameras, baby monitors, and other gadgets, so it's not hard to imagine a similar situation with the Drone Cam. To counter the threat of surreptitious surveillance, Siminoff says, the Drone Cam is loud by design—the extremely loud buzz of its propellers means a bad actor couldn't remotely snoop without tipping off people in the home.

Concerns don't just stem from third parties trying to access your devices, though. Amazon's Ring has been accused of normalizing suburban surveillance, partly stemming from the company's cozy relationship with law enforcement, and it has even handed Ring videos to cops without warrants.

“I think the companies who make these sorts of surveillance products are invested in us feeling less safe at home,” Klosowski says. “If there is a way to sell a product that helps alleviate the problem they are creating, I think they will do it.”

The Rover Cam is designed to patrol warehouses and factories.

The telescoping camera pops out to scan the area.

Ring Robotics' second robot is designed for businesses, not consumers. The Ring Rover Cam might look familiar—it shares many of the same hardware guts as Amazon's Astro, the short-lived home robot that didn't do much. Amazon had even pivoted it as a $2,350 security robot for businesses before eventually bricking them 10 months after launch.

Siminoff says the robot was great, but the software needed polishing, and people largely wanted to use Astro for security—that's why the Rover Cam ditches the cute face seen on the previous bot. It has the same AI Pilot as the Drone Cam, which helps it navigate through spaces and avoid obstacles. A telescoping 4K camera pops up to monitor its surroundings, and the Rover beams a light circle to the ceiling so you can always glance up to see where the short bot is on the factory floor.

It responds to sensor alerts, can automatically check an area, features two-way talk, and has a built-in siren. It covers up to 5,000 square feet and returns to its charging dock after its patrol ends. (It can do about 30 minutes). It's significantly cheaper than Astro was, but buyers still face the lingering risk that Amazon could pull the plug if Ring Robotics doesn't pan out.

#524. NYT Strands hints, answers for November 11, 2026

Today's NYT Strands hints will be easy if you're a fitness instructor.

Strands, the New York Times' elevated word-search game, requires the player to perform a twist on the classic word search. Words can be made from linked letters — up, down, left, right, or diagonal, but words can also change direction, resulting in quirky shapes and patterns. Every single letter in the grid will be part of an answer. There's always a theme linking every solution, along with the "spangram," a special, word or phrase that sums up that day's theme, and spans the entire grid horizontally or vertically.

By providing an opaque hint and not providing the word list, Strands creates a brain-teasing game that takes a little longer to play than its other games, like Wordle and Connections.

If you're feeling stuck or just don't have 10 or more minutes to figure out today's puzzle, we've got all the NYT Strands hints for today's puzzle you need to progress at your preferred pace.

#522. Poppi founders are going after PepsiCo's Gatorade with new beverage startup Jetty

After disrupting the soda category with Poppi, founders Allison and Stephen Ellsworth are now taking aim at hydration with their new brand, Jetty.

Starting Monday, Jetty drinks and ready-to-mix electrolyte packets will be on shelves at Whole Foods grocery stores and available online at Target and Amazon.

The new, self-funded venture directly pits the Ellsworths against PepsiCo, which bought Poppi for $1.95 billion in 2025. Pepsi owns Gatorade, which dominates the U.S. sports drink category with about 60% market share, according to Numerator data.

Poppi shook up the soda category with prebiotic fiber, low calorie and sugar counts and its fun marketing aimed at Generation Z and millennials. Jetty looks to upend the hydration category through a similar strategy. To do so, it will face off not only against Gatorade but also a swarm of other upstarts looking to get in on the electrolyte-fueled trend.

For decades, Gatorade and other sports drinks have pitched their products to athletes who need electrolytes and fluids after an intense workout, although many consumers drink them every day without even sweating. Jetty is aimed at people who need a hydration boost, but not the same level of electrolytes or sugar as a traditional sports drink.

"No one has really created a product for just everyday hydration — literally everyday hydration for normal people," Allison Ellsworth, Jetty's chief brand officer, told CNBC. "Like I woke up this morning, I didn't run a marathon."

Following Poppi's sale to PepsiCo, Ellsworth stayed on as an advisor to the brand, although she said she is no longer involved in the day-to-day operations.

After 10 years building Poppi, Ellsworth said, she finally had free time. As Ellsworth tells it, she went to the doctor for the first time in a decade. She started using a GLP-1 medication and lost 60 pounds.

Ellsworth's husband, Stephen, now CEO of the new brand, noticed she wasn't hydrating, even when she came back from the gym.

GLP-1 medications such as Ozempic and Mounjaro can make people less thirsty. But because staying hydrated can also help mitigate some of the drugs' side effects, such as nausea and headaches, it can become more important.

"I physically struggle getting liquid into my body," Ellsworth said.

Plain old water is often less appealing to consumers, even dehydrated GLP-1 users, but more flavorful options with added benefits can be tempting. About 44% of GLP-1 users report consuming more functional beverages, according to EY-Parthenon's third annual survey of more than 3,600 current or former GLP-1 users.

Ellsworth sees a broader appeal for Jetty, too.

"Our core audience is going to be millennial moms and Gen Z," she said. "Gen Z just isn't relating to these old school brands, the legacy of the Gatorades and the Powerades. They're just not cool."

Consumers broadly have become more interested in staying hydrated.

Dollar sales of hydration and electrolyte products climbed 20% to $2.6 billion over the 52 weeks ended Oct. 4, based on data from SPINS, which tracks new universal product codes, or UPCs. The category also has a strong repeat buyer rate of 62%.

However, the Ellsworths aren't the only ones to notice the potential in hydration. Big food and beverage companies have been looking to hydration, too.

Kraft Heinz debuted Capri Sun juice pouches and Kool-Aid packets with electrolytes this spring. In April, Pepsi unveiled an overhaul of the Gatorade brand, focused on making it more appealing to a wider swath of consumers, with more potential occasions like a "fresh morning start" or an "afternoon walk." And Electrolit is a top performer for its distributor Keurig Dr Pepper, with 40% retail sales growth in the company's third quarter.

All of that means more competition for shelf space and consumers' "share of throat," an industry term for beverage market share.

To increase Jetty's visibility across the grocery store and to maximize convenience for shoppers, Jetty will be available in both powders and bottles.

Single-use electrolyte mixes are growing at a much faster clip than the ready-to-drink category, Ellsworth said, thanks to Unilever's Liquid I.V. and smaller upstarts like LMNT. On Amazon alone, powders are a $1 billion category, according to Ellsworth.

The ready-to-drink, or RTD, sports beverages are a much larger market, but the category's sales are shrinking as consumers reach for other hydration options instead. Unit sales of sports drinks have fallen 9.1% from 2022 to 2026, according to Numerator data. And Coca-Cola has written down the value of BodyArmor by more than $1.7 billion after buying control of the brand for $5.6 billion in late 2021.

"Me and my husband know how to do RTD," Ellsworth said.

Jetty will launch with five flavors, including watermelon, orange mango, fruit punch, black raspberry and white cherry. All of them contain 100 milligrams of sodium, 310 milligrams of potassium and 100 milligrams of magnesium in a 15.2-ounce bottle or 15-gram packet.

In addition to electrolytes, Jetty products also contain B vitamins, 3 grams of fiber and 6 grams of sugar. It doesn't use artificial sweeteners or dyes, its founders say.

For comparison, a 20-ounce bottle of traditional Gatorade Thirst Quencher has about 270 milligrams of sodium and 80 milligrams of potassium, plus 34 grams of sugar. The sports drink brand is also phasing out artificial colors and promoting lower-sugar versions.

"What's really interesting about this product and this opportunity is we now can capture all those lapsed drinkers — or that generation that didn't even ever drink it in the first place," Ellsworth said.

Poppi's new owner knows about Jetty and the market it is trying to capture, too.

"I don't think [Pepsi] gets it, but what they do get is that the movement is changing, and they think that just by taking dyes out or doing 90% less sugar, they're going to capture this audience," Ellsworth said. "But that is very big corporate thinking."

She added that she believes Pepsi has missed the mark on advertising to Gen Z consumers across its portfolio, such as in its campaigns for Sprite competitor Starry.

Perhaps as an extension of that, Poppi's performance has been underwhelming under PepsiCo's ownership.

The food and beverage giant now estimates the fair value of potential performance-based payments to Poppi's prior owners is $12 million, Pepsi said in a regulatory filing in October. At the time of the deal, Pepsi promised an additional payment of $300 million if Poppi hit certain targets by the third quarter of 2027. The Ellsworths declined to comment on the filing to CNBC.

This time around, with one successful brand under their belts, Jetty's founders say they have learned from past mistakes. Those lessons should help Jetty as it launches at a much larger scale than Poppi did.

Three different claims lawyers have signed off on Jetty's packaging, according to Ellsworth. Poppi paid $8.9 million to settle class-action litigation alleging false advertising over its "gut healthy" claims.

For now, Jetty is entirely self-funded with $2.5 million, although Ellsworth said that the brand will likely raise money in 2027. This time, though, she said investors will probably look less like traditional venture capitalists.

"I want to bring in investors that bring value," Ellsworth said. Instead of a venture capitalist who is willing to invest $15 million, Jetty would rather consider investors who can only write a check for $1 million but have access to celebrities. "So then you're getting access to talent on a cheaper level and people that are invested in the brand."

And Jetty has also learned a few lessons from Gatorade. For example, the brand is already at work on a lemon-lime version — the original flavor of Gatorade.

"We'll eventually do it," Ellsworth said. "But it was really important at the beginning just to stick to flavors that we love, for it to taste good."

#521. NYT Connections hints today: Clues, answers for November 11, 2026

The NYT Connections puzzle today is not too difficult if you like to hit the green.

Connections is the one of the most popular New York Times word games that's captured the public's attention. The game is all about finding the "common threads between words." And just like Wordle, Connections resets after midnight and each new set of words gets trickier and trickier—so we've served up some hints and tips to get you over the hurdle.

If you just want to be told today's puzzle, you can jump to the end of this article for today's Connections solution. But if you'd rather solve it yourself, keep reading for some clues, tips, and strategies to assist you.

The NYT's latest daily word game has become a social media hit. The Times credits associate puzzle editor Wyna Liu with helping to create the new word game and bringing it to the publications' Games section. Connections can be played on both web browsers and mobile devices and require players to group four words that share something in common.

Each puzzle features 16 words and each grouping of words is split into four categories. These sets could comprise of anything from book titles, software, country names, etc. Even though multiple words will seem like they fit together, there's only one correct answer.

If a player gets all four words in a set correct, those words are removed from the board. Guess wrong and it counts as a mistake—players get up to four mistakes until the game ends.

Players can also rearrange and shuffle the board to make spotting connections easier. Additionally, each group is color-coded with yellow being the easiest, followed by green, blue, and purple. Like Wordle, you can share the results with your friends on social media.

It's available for $295, and while it’s not currently on sale, unlike the brand's larger fridges, you can get it for $50 off when you buy a full-sized Rocco Classic or Essential.

#515. VisionQuest could have done anything, and it just did this

Like the synthezoid at its center, Marvel's VisionQuest contains multitudes. It's a genre-bending adventure, a reflection on flawed father-son dynamics, and an ambitious attempt to tie up the loose ends of the so-called WandaVision trilogy. But seemingly above all else, it's a sequel to no one's favorite Avengers movie, Avengers: Age of Ultron.

VisionQuest brings James Spader's evil Ultron back to the MCU, and at first, I wasn't complaining. Spader's gleefully villainous performance is one of the sole bright spots of Age of Ultron. It holds up just as well in VisionQuest, with Spader chewing the hell out of every withering one-liner.

Want more film and TV news and reviews delivered straight to your inbox? Sign up for Mashable’s Top Stories newsletter or get Mashable push alerts today.

Episode 7 revealed that the show's main villain, the Client, was also played by James Spader, meaning Ultron's human avatar is modeled after him. Turns out, the Client is Virgil Horton, grandson of Phineas Horton (Bettany), creator of the world's first synthetic man.

Virgil was once the partner of Josephine Carrington (T'Nia Miller), whose consciousness was used to create the synthezoid Jocasta (Miller). As the two worked on their revolutionary AI tech, they coded themselves into their work, explaining why Ultron's human avatar looks like Virgil. Now that Virgil has apprehended both Vision and Jocasta, he plans to put his mind in Vision's body so he and Jocasta can live together forever, using their nanobot tech to rule over all of humanity.

Neither Vision nor Jocasta wants this future, so they team up with Tommy Maximoff (Ruaridh Mollica) to defeat Virgil. In the chaos, Virgil shoots Jocasta, who subsequently throws Ultron's drive into the nanobot swarm, unleashing him in his physical form. His first move? Killing Virgil in a feat of Spader-on-Spader violence.

In one fell swoop, Ultron dispatches VisionQuest's Big Bad, leaving a villainous void ready to be filled. That's a role Ultron is all too happy to step into, and so begins VisionQuest's endgame: Ultron's nanobot swarm flying towards London, ready to implant itself in its citizens and kill them from the inside out.

For a series that tried to twist and bend the rules of what a Marvel show could be, this final showdown is frustratingly familiar, right down to Vision and Ultron duking it out in their synthetic forms. Yes, Ultron can dissipate into a cloud of nanobots now, but that doesn't change the fact that this feels too much a rehash of an Avengers film that came out 11 years ago.

Under this sheen of familiarity, even the best parts of VisionQuest's climax feel like a drag. In a callback to WandaVision, Vision tries to use the power of sitcoms to help Ultron process his grief over losing Jocasta. Ultron hijacks the program, resulting in a 500-episode montage that comes with its fair share of clever sight gags, along with a catchy theme song. ("Don't kill the world!")

Yet all the montage really does is remind us of how perfectly WandaVision was able to parody sitcoms. More than that, it reminds us of the power of good episodic television, something every Marvel show has tried to capture, before almost always falling short and feeling like a truncated eight-hour movie instead. With its Age of Ultron-meets-therapy climax, VisionQuest is no exception, even if it has its fair share of highs. (Tommy's awkward running around the table in the finale will live in my head for a while.)

VisionQuest is the last new MCU show on Marvel's docket until 2028's Zodiac, and aside from Loki and Daredevil: Born Again, live-action MCU series don't have the best track record of renewal. Still, that doesn't stop VisionQuest from signalling to a future for its characters. Tommy connects with his long-lost twin Billy (Joe Locke) and heads back to New Jersey with him by the season's end. Could the pair be part of a young Avengers team down the line, or will the MCU leave them by the wayside, along with other potential young Avengers like Kamala Khan (Iman Vellani) and Kate Bishop (Hailee Steinfeld)?

Tommy also asks Vision if there's any way they could potentially bring the late Wanda (Elizabeth Olsen) back, reuniting the entire WandaVision family. Meanwhile, Ultron is still out there, along with Jocasta's hard drive. Both sequences suggest that neither women are truly gone from the MCU — if only VisionQuest gets the opportunity to explore further. In the meantime, though, its final impression is a mixed bag: a combination of some truly inventive world building, marred by the insistence on replaying a decade-old story.

#514. This New Linux-Based Desktop Operating System Is Built for AI Nerds

Kirt McMaster knows that his new operating system—Kora—won’t be for everyone, and he’s fine with that.

Kora, announced on Tuesday, is designed as a desktop alternative to Windows or macOS for “the AI native, not mass market,” he says. McMaster, the company’s CEO and cofounder—who also cofounded Boost Mobile—likens his strategy to how Steve Jobs targeted creators when overhauling desktop publishing with the Mac.

This “next-generation” desktop OS can integrate context from user interactions across multiple AI tools. Targeted to creators and developers, Kora’s central idea is to bring AI models together at the system level and help power users interact more seamlessly with multiple providers, rather than siloing everything off into desktop apps and browser tabs. It’s scheduled to launch sometime in 2027.

By tapping into your data and context at a machine level, Kora could streamline some of the current speed bumps of using AI tools on a desktop. “All your context, all your memory is on your machine,” says Franck De Girolami, one of Kora’s cofounders. He gives the example of someone designing a project with an OpenAI model inside Kora’s interface, switching to Anthropic’s tools to deploy code, and leaning on a local model to save money on AI tokens.

Kora is a standalone operating system built on the Linux kernel that runs directly on hardware. The startup has partnered with Qualcomm to bring Kora to Snapdragon PCs next year, but it also supports x86-64 architecture, so you can likely install it on current Intel- or AMD-based PCs.

To bridge the software gap, built-in compatibility layers will allow users to run standard Windows apps. The company is also developing its own native apps for basics like contacts, calendar, and email, alongside a software development kit (SDK) for third-party developers. Along the bottom of the home screen is a dock, as you would expect on a desktop OS. This is where users can switch between email, web browsing, and launching games.

The OS is designed around what the startup calls a "neural file system.” Based on example screenshots, the user experience may feel like messaging a chatbot or AI agent. File data is indexed by meaning so you can ask questions about the broad context of files by typing into an OS search bar, without necessarily looking up specific file names. For example, you could search for a business contract saved somewhere on your device based on a small detail you remember.

Kora grew out of McMaster’s startup Playtron, which launched its own gaming OS, GameOS, last year. GameOS is primarily built to run on portable handhelds, like a Steam Deck, with PC support as well. That architecture now lives inside Kora as GameFrame, letting users run PC games—provided their hardware can keep up.

The broader pitch caters to PC gamers and power users. “That’s always our thing—next-generation creators who are using these tools daily to build new applications and services, but they want everything else that they can get from a Windows PC or what’s currently available on macOS,” McMaster says.

Taking on the giants is difficult, especially with a team of under 100 people, but Kora’s leadership knows a thing or two about launching operating systems. Kora’s three founders—McMaster, De Girolami, and John Lagerling—each bring their own expertise. McMaster cofounded and helped lead Cyanogen, an open source Android fork that gained millions of users before shutting down around a decade ago. De Girolami helped develop Playtron’s GameOS. Lagerling previously worked on Android at Google.

Kora isn’t the first to rethink the desktop operating system in the AI age. Google launched its Googlebook OS and compatible laptops in October, designed around Gemini AI. Even so, McMaster doesn’t see that OS and its Android support as satisfying power users who want to run AI models that require serious chip power. The company is optimizing Kora for desktop configurations with at least 64 gigabytes of RAM, which is twice the memory capacity of the top Googlebook.

“We can evolve much faster than macOS and Windows,” McMaster says. “When you’re a billion-user incumbent, it’s impossible to tip the boat, man. It’s just really hard to make big changes. Over time, there’s this event horizon where building a new kind of OS for intelligence can become something very compelling.”

#512. Restaurant chains refocus on food quality to bring back diners

Juicier chicken tenders. Revamped sauces. Richer and creamier Frosties.

Across the restaurant industry, chains are turning their focus back to their food in the hopes that better quality will win back diners.

Over the last two years, consumers have dined out less frequently and grown pickier about where they choose to eat, based on data from Black Box Intelligence. In that time, "value" has been the buzzword du jour for restaurant executives, who have explained to analysts and investors that the word encompasses more than menu pricing alone.

Now, industry CEOs are turning to their corporate chefs to try to beat rivals on quality and taste, as they look to expand their definition of value. Discounts and one-off promotions can lift traffic for a time, but the actual product still matters for restaurants' long-term growth, as well as franchisee profitability.

From Wendy's to Chipotle Mexican Grill, chief executives touted new or upcoming improvements to menu items on their quarterly earnings conference calls in recent weeks.

"We think the product's improved and even better," Chipotle CEO Scott Boatwright told analysts on the company's call in late October, referring to the burrito chain's brisket, which returned to menus for a limited time Thursday.

For some chains, better food is a key step in ambitious turnaround plans.

For example, "quality food at real value" is one pillar of Wendy's five-part turnaround strategy that the chain unveiled on Thursday.

"We're also rebuilding our food offerings and have begun testing improvements to quality and craveability of our core items, including thicker, crispier bacon, a richer and creamier Frosty, whole leaf lettuce on sandwiches and improved salads," Wendy's CEO Bob Wright told analysts on the call Thursday.

That same day, the company reported its seventh straight quarter of same-store sales declines and publicly rejected a $1.4 billion takeover bid led by Nelson Peltz's Trian Partners. Wendy's said the proposal did not take into account the potential value that its turnaround plan could unlock.

There is precedent to suggest that better-tasting food can contribute to higher sales.

In 2009, Domino's Pizza changed its recipe, swapping out its sauce, adding garlic to its crust and switching to shredded mozzarella with a hint of provolone. Along with blunt commercials that compared its previous crust to cardboard and old sauce to ketchup, the changes helped to spur U.S. same-store sales growth of 9.9% the following year.

Now, some of the same team that led the Domino's turnaround is copying the playbook at Burger King's U.S. restaurants.

Part of the burger chain's multiyear comeback plan has focused on revamping its core menu items. It started earlier this year with its flagship offering, the Whopper, which received a better bun and a new mayonnaise. And in September, it rolled out an overhaul of its nuggets, switching to 100% white meat, crispier breading and a more flavorful marinade. It also reformulated its sauces.

"We've tried to look at our biggest categories first," Josh Kobza, CEO of Burger King parent Restaurant Brands International, told CNBC. "We've also thought about the ones where the fixes are more straightforward and less operationally involved."

Following the changes, Whopper sales were up by a double-digit percentage in the third quarter, Kobza said on the company's earnings call in late October. And nugget sales climbed more than 35%. Burger King said its U.S. same-store sales jumped 8.4% in the quarter.

Like Domino's, Burger King is highlighting diners' past gripes with its Whoppers and nuggets in its ads, which star Burger King U.S. president and former Domino's executive Tom Curtis.

Better-tasting food isn't just for chains that are in trouble.

Even Chili's, which has seen its same-store sales soar 52% over the past three years, is improving more of its existing menu options. After tackling its baby back ribs, burgers and nachos, it will turn next to salads and steaks, Brinker International CEO Kevin Hochman told CNBC.

"We're going to be doing more dry aging of our beef to make it more tender, versus doing it in a plant," he said. "It's a little bit more expensive, but we think the taste is going to be a whole lot better."

Chili's rival Applebee's launched a juicier chicken tender during its latest quarter, according to John Peyton, CEO of the chain's parent company Dine Brands. In 2027, Applebee's plans to overhaul more of its protein options.

"We think proteins are, number one, our biggest opportunity," Peyton told CNBC. "Number two, they're a big focus of the way people are thinking about eating right now, leaning into proteins and a little bit less with the carbs."

Chicken is also a focus for McDonald's, which is testing hand-breaded wings, strips and a sandwich in a handful of Chicago-area restaurants. It plans to expand the trial to more locations in the U.S. and Ireland in early 2027.

Moreover, McDonald's is trying to improve its coffee, with fresher beans and upgraded equipment, executives said at the company's investor day in September. Both chicken and coffee are fast-growing categories, offering more opportunities to lift sales than McDonald's signature beef burgers.

While the burger giant isn't in full turnaround mode like rivals Wendy's and Burger King, its U.S. business has struggled in recent quarters after its value strategy didn't gain the traction the company had hoped. In the third quarter, its U.S. same-store sales fell 0.2%.

In addition to menu tests, McDonald's is also leaning into training to improve the quality and consistency of its core menu items. Starting in October, the company launched "Make It Golden," a multiyear training program for corporate employees, restaurant workers and franchisees.

Ultimately, improved menu items mean little if the recipe changes aren't well executed.

#511. VisionQuests game-changing mid-credits scenes, explained

Like many an MCU movie before it, VisionQuest's finale continues Marvel's proud tradition of mid-credits scenes teasing the franchise's future, including one that's set to shake up the game in Avengers: Doomsday.

VisionQuest's first mid-credits scene offers a look at what might be next for one of the the show's leads, should the series get a season 2. Its second mid-credits scene is far buzzier, teasing the return of a beloved MCU figure, just in time for Avengers: Doomsday. Here's what happens.

Want more film and TV news and reviews delivered straight to your inbox? Sign up for Mashable’s Top Stories newsletter or get Mashable push alerts today.

VisionQuest's first returns to the site of the show's final battle: Horton Manor. There, Phineas Horton (Paul Bettany) created the world's first synthetic man, all those decades ago.

In the final battle, Horton's grandson Virgil (James Spader), aka VisionQuest's sinister Client, perished at the hands of Ultron (Spader). Before he died, Virgil shot synthezoid Jocasta (T'Nia Miller), who was based on the consciousness of his former partner Josephine Carrington (also Miller). But Jocasta may not be gone for good.

The mid-credits scene shows Ultron residing at Horton Manor, now in his human form thanks to Josephine's nanobot technology. (He's trimmed the beard, though.) Since his human form mirrors Virgil's, has Ultron assumed Virgil's identity to live among humans? Or is he remaining fully himself?

One thing's for sure: Ultron is still holding onto Jocasta. Literally: He's holding the drive that contains her code.

As he holds it, he takes a contemplative beat. The music swells menacingly. Then he walks off screen. To do what? VisionQuest certainly doesn't tell.

Perhaps he's getting ready to rebuild and reboot Jocasta, so the pair can finish their dream of ruling the world. Whatever his goal, the foreboding music suggests that it may have been a mistake to let Ultron hang around.

VisionQuest's second mid-credits scene takes things all the way back to the climax of Doctor Strange in the Multiverse of Madness, when Wanda Maximoff (Elizabeth Olsen) used her Scarlet Witch powers to bring down Mount Wundagore, sacrificing herself in the process.

After replaying Wundagore's collapse, VisionQuest flashes forward to the present, when a hooded figure lifts the rubble off of Wanda's corpse.

"Scarlet Witch, awaken," he says. "Your power will be mine."

A green glow illuminates Wanda's face, and she wakes with a gasp, officially bringing the Scarlet Witch back into play in the MCU.

The hooded figure is undoubtedly Dr. Doom (Robert Downey Jr.). In the extra scenes shown in Avengers: Encore, he pulls a similar move on Bruce Banner (Mark Ruffalo), telling him he's here to "collect" him before spiriting him away in a mysterious green glow. Is Dr. Doom building an army of the most powerful Avengers? If so, his team is already looking pretty stacked.

In the VisionQuest finale, Tommy Maximoff (Ruaridh Mollica) appeared hopeful at the possibility of getting his mother back, somehow. Surely this is the worst case scenario, right?

So far, Disney has not renewed VisionQuest for a second season.

Aside from Loki and Daredevil: Born Again, it's rare that Disney renews the MCU's live-action series. Based on that precedent, the chances for more VisionQuest are very slim.

However, that doesn't mean we won't see these characters again. WandaVision spawned two "sequel" series in Agatha All Along and VisionQuest, so the story of Ultron or Billy (Joe Locke) and Tommy could continue in a new series. Obviously, Wanda is being set up to appear in Avengers: Doomsday, which means a family reunion could occur sooner rather than later.

Currently, the only live-action MCU series in development is 2028's Zodiac, so it's unlikely we get any further Ultron updates before then.

#509. Hurdle hints and answers for November 11, 2026

If you like playing daily word games like Wordle, then Hurdle is a great game to add to your routine.

There are five rounds to the game. The first round sees you trying to guess the word, with correct, misplaced, and incorrect letters shown in each guess. If you guess the correct answer, it'll take you to the next hurdle, providing the answer to the last hurdle as your first guess. This can give you several clues or none, depending on the words. For the final hurdle, every correct answer from previous hurdles is shown, with correct and misplaced letters clearly shown.

An important note is that the number of times a letter is highlighted from previous guesses does necessarily indicate the number of times that letter appears in the final hurdle.

#508. 4DX Movies Are Entering Their Thirsty Romance Era

Following the temporary collapse of the fan fiction website A03, extremely horny millennial women are on the hunt for other ways to get their rocks off—and Regal Cinemas execs are more than happy to help.

On Tuesday, the movie theater chain announced that for one night only, on November 16, it will be screening 4DX versions of episodes 5 and 6 of Heated Rivalry, the smash-hit gay hockey show adapted from the bestselling novels by Rachel Reid that first aired on the Canadian network Crave but blasted into the mainstream when it came to HBO Max. The screenings will be at 59 theaters across the US, as well as a handful of theaters in Canada and the United Kingdom.

The screenings are intended to give fans the opportunity “to go to the cottage and really experience” Hollanov all over again, says Jack Grimes, senior director of alternative business development at Regal.

The 4DX screenings are upping the ante for the fan-favorite Heated Rivalry screenings, which have been selling out in movie theaters across the US for the past month. Though the series is widely available to stream, fans have flocked to theaters to shout along to t.a.T.u’s “All the Things She Said” during the club scene in episode four and post photos of the Heated Rivalry–themed popcorn buckets featuring stars Connor Storrie and Hudson Williams in a sweaty, shirtless embrace. Thus far, such public displays of down bad-ness have been incredibly lucrative: as of November 8, Regal has earned $1.4 million from its limited theatrical release, per Variety’s reporting.

Still, Heated Rivalry isn’t an obvious candidate for a 4DX release. Originating in South Korea in 2009, 4DX technology provides an immersive experience for the viewer, such as motion-enabled chairs; environmental effects like wind, snow, and rain; or even scents, such as the smell of gunpowder or explosives during battle scenes. It’s mostly been used for big-budget action-adventure movies and thrillers such as The Odyssey and Avatar: The Way of Water.

Recently, however, there’s been increasing demand for 4DX big-screen releases for not just conventional action movies but also straightforward dramas, comedies, and even romances. Put another way, there’s evidence that a not-insubstantial number of the filmgoing public would like nothing more than a two-hour license to hump their seats.

For instance, as the result of what was essentially an extremely online in-joke, last month the film distributor Black Bear hosted 4DX screenings of the romantic black comedy Wicker, starring Olivia Colman as a woman who falls in love with a straw-plaited man (Alexander Skarsgaard). The screening featured such immersive special effects as water splashing in the audience’s faces when Colman’s character empties out her chamber pot, as well as synchronized motion-activated “thrusts” in the audience’s seats during sex scenes.

Grimes tells WIRED that the idea to produce 4DX Heated Rivalry screenings was in part inspired by the intense social media response to Wicker.

“We think the demographic overlap is really significant between the two,” he says. “They’re both very female-driven, in terms of what the viewership looks like.” The sales numbers for the Heated Rivalry screenings “emphasized that we needed to bring this to life,” Grimes says.

In general, filmmakers are increasingly becoming more creative about how they incorporate 4DX effects into their work. According to a Vulture interview with Jaehyun Jeong and Jaesang Yoon, two producers for the special effects company CJ 4DPlex, Paul Thomas Anderson specifically requested “sexual movements” for a scene in One Battle After Another in which Sean Penn’s character masturbates.

When asked about which specific effects will be used for Heated Rivalry, Grimes says CJ 4DPlex is in the process of coding the 4DX version, so he couldn’t say for sure. But he says it will likely “lean a lot into the motion effects,” albeit “where it makes sense to do so and where it's going to be tasteful to do so.”

Does that mean the chairs will thrust during sex scenes, as some randy fans have speculated? “I mean, it's, it's something that we saw in Wicker, so I wouldn't say that it's out of a stretch of imagination to say it's going to happen here,” Grimes says.

Unfortunately, the effects will perhaps not be quite as explicit as they were in Wicker, as it’s worth noting that episodes 5 and 6 are significantly less graphic than those earlier in the season. But it’s still tempting to consider how, exactly, Regal plans to incorporate special effects into the screening. Chirping crickets during the cottage scenes? Ice chips flying around when Kip rushes down to the rink to make out with Scott to the adrenaline-pumping percussive strains of “I’ll Believe in Anything”? An unidentified substance spraying in the audience’s faces when Ilya finishes off Shane while he’s on a call? The mind reels.

#503. Who is Khamûl in The Lord of the Rings: The Rings of Power?

In The Lord of the Rings: The Rings of Power Season 3, the villains are running the show.

Sauron (Charlie Vickers) is comfortably in control of most of Middle-earth, and he's got a whole host of allies to help him keep a firm grip on the world. Among these allies are the Nazgnagôl, or Ring-servants: human men who have pledged themselves to the Dark Lord and received a ring of power in exchange.

The title of Nazgnagôl is a show-only invention, but it does come rooted in J.R.R. Tolkien's works. Anyone who's read Tolkien's The Lord of the Rings or watched Peter Jackson's film adaptations knows that the nine men who accept Sauron's rings eventually lose their humanity and become the Nazgûl, Ringwraiths at Sauron's command. Think of The Rings of Power's Nazgnagôl as the first step in that transformation. They've already got the black cloaks down. Next up? Corruption and an eternal life of undead servitude. Fun!

Want more film and TV news and reviews delivered straight to your inbox? Sign up for Mashable’s Top Stories newsletter or get Mashable push alerts today.

Over the course of its four-episode premiere, The Rings of Power Season 3 introduces several Nazgnagôl. Galadriel (Morfydd Clark) believes one of them to be the king of the Lossoth, who live in the far north of Middle-earth. Later, young Theo (Tyroe Muhafidin) accepts a ring and becomes a Nazgnagôl himself, sparking Witch-king rumors in the process.

But The Rings of Power puts extra emphasis on one Nazgnagôl in particular: Khamûl (Zubin Varla), an Easterling in Rhûn who seeks to buck the Dark Wizard's (Ciarán Hinds) authority. Unlike many Rings of Power characters, or the title of Nazgnagôl itself, Khamûl is canonically part of Tolkien's work. Here's how this mysterious newcomer fits into Tolkien's larger legendarium.

Khamûl is the only named Nazgûl in Tolkien's work, although he isn't explicitly named in The Lord of the Rings trilogy. Instead, Tolkien dives into his background in Unfinished Tales of Númenor and Middle-earth, where he reveals that Khamûl is second-in-command to the Witch-king of Angmar. He's also known as the Shadow of the East and the Black Easterling, each title alluding to his origins in Rhûn.

Unfinished Tales also highlights Khamûl's specific role in the Nazgûl's hunt for Frodo Baggins in The Fellowship of the Ring. He's the Nazgûl who seeks out Frodo in Hobbiton and who almost catches Frodo and his friends at Bucklebury Ferry. Darn that pesky water for getting in the way.

In the Third Age, Sauron also sent Khamûl to hold the fortress of Dol Guldur in Mirkwood, which features in the Appendices of The Lord of the Rings and played a large part in Jackson's Hobbit trilogy.

All of these events are thousands of years away when Khamûl dons his ring in The Rings of Power. As soon as he does, though, his fate is sealed, and he's in for thousands of years of serving Sauron and playing second fiddle to the Witch-king of Angmar — whoever that may be.

#502. Eli Lilly’s Foundayo obesity pill eases sleep apnea in late-stage trial, paving way for approval

Eli Lilly on Monday said its obesity pill Foundayo significantly reduced the severity of obstructive sleep apnea, the most common sleep-related breathing disorder, in patients with obesity, in a late-stage trial.

The drugmaker plans to file by the end of the year for U.S. approval of the daily pill for moderate-to-severe obstructive sleep apnea, or OSA, in adults with obesity or who are overweight. The results make the pill — which rolled out in the U.S. in April — one of several GLP-1 treatments to show promise in treating the condition.

OSA refers to interrupted breathing during sleep due to narrowed or blocked airways, which can cause drops in oxygen and increases in blood pressure that are associated with serious complications, such as hypertension, heart failure and stroke. The condition affects nearly 1 billion adults globally, including 80 million people ages 20 and over in the U.S., according to the National Council on Aging. Most have never been diagnosed, Lilly said in a release.

Lilly's blockbuster weight loss injection Zepbound became the first medication to win U.S. approval for OSA in December 2024. Patients have limited treatment options outside of that drug and many use cumbersome machines that provide positive airway pressure, or PAP, to allow for normal breathing.

"Foundayo is already making a difference for people living with obesity, and with plans to submit these results to the FDA by the end of the year, we could have the opportunity to help people living with OSA," Dr. Thomas Seck, senior vice president of product development of Lilly Cardiometabolic Health, said in a release.

The trial tested Foundayo in two independent studies, the first of which included 363 patients who were unable or unwilling to use PAP therapy. The second study included 349 people who were on PAP therapy for at least three months at the time of screening and continued that treatment during the trial.

Patients in both studies who took Foundayo started at a low dose and increased it gradually at four-week intervals to different maximum dosages.

Researchers specifically examined how much the pill reduced the so-called apnea-hypopnea index, or AHI, which records the number of times per hour a person's breathing shows a restricted or completely blocked airway. The index is used to evaluate the severity of obstructive sleep apnea and the effectiveness of treatments for the condition.

People who took Foundayo and were not using PAP therapy had an average of 21.1 fewer AHI events per hour at 52 weeks, compared with an average reduction of 4.8 per hour for the placebo, according to the results.

Foundayo also met the trial's second goal in that group, leading to an overall AHI reduction of 43.8% compared with a decrease of 6.3% for the placebo. Lilly said 31.5% of the participants in the Foundayo group met the criteria for remission or improvement to mild OSA.

Meanwhile, patients who were on both Foundayo and PAP therapy had an average of 18.3 fewer AHI events per hour at 52 weeks, compared with an average reduction of 5.5 per hour for the placebo.

Foundayo led to an overall AHI reduction of around 27.5% in that group, compared with a decrease of 3% for the placebo. The company said 19.6% of the participants on Foundayo and PAP therapy also met the criteria for remission or mild OSA.

Foundayo also reduced what's known as the sleep apnea-specific hypoxic burden — a measure of how deeply and for how long oxygen levels fall during sleep. That metric fell by 55.9% in people not using PAP therapy and 45.8% in those using it.

Lilly said the pill also helped patients lose roughly 11% of their weight across groups at 52 weeks and helped to lower a marker of inflammation.

The company said the safety data and how well patients tolerated Foundayo were generally consistent with previous trials on the pill.

Around 12% of people on Foundayo who were not using PAP therapy discontinued due to side effects, compared to 3.8% with the placebo. Meanwhile, 6.8% of those on both Lilly's pill and PAP discontinued the drug due to side effects, compared with 0.6% in the placebo group.

The most common side effects were gastrointestinal, including nausea, diarrhea, constipation and vomiting, and were generally mild to moderate in severity. That's consistent with other GLP-1 medications.

#498. Did The Lord of the Rings: The Rings of Power just reveal the Witch-king of Angmar?

Somebody call Éowyn, because The Lord of the Rings: The Rings of Power may have just unveiled the Witch-king of Angmar.

The first four episodes of The Rings of Power Season 3 introduced new key players in the battle between good and evil in Middle-earth. Among them are the Nazgnagôl, or Ring-servants, human men who have received a ring of power from Sauron (Charlie Vickers). The rings will eventually corrupt their spirits, turning them into the undead Nazgûl, or Ringwraiths, from J.R.R. Tolkien's work.

Want more film and TV news and reviews delivered straight to your inbox? Sign up for Mashable’s Top Stories newsletter or get Mashable push alerts today.

The most powerful of the Nazgûl is the Witch-king of Angmar, head of the Ringwraiths. Like most of the Black Riders, the Witch-king of Angmar's origins are unknown. His second-in-command, Khamûl the Easterling (Zubin Varla), is the only Nazgûl to be named in Tolkien's legendarium. Tolkien also wrote that three Númenóreans became Ringwraiths, although he leaves any further details out. That means The Rings of Power has a lot of leeway when it comes to crafting backstories for any of the Nazgûl, including the Witch-king himself. In Season 3, episode 2, The Rings of Power appears to put that backstory in motion, heavily hinting that the Witch-king is none other than Southlander Theo (Tyroe Muhafidin).

Introduced in Season 1, Theo has always had a complicated relationship to the darkness of Middle-earth. Look no further than his discovery of and fascination with the broken orc sword hilt that eventually triggered the eruption of Mount Doom.

Theo's connection to darkness only grows in Season 3, when he and the Middle-earth men of Pelargir, grown sick of Númenórean rule, take Sauron up on his offer to receive a ring of power. Sauron forces the men to fight to the death to decide who will bear the ring, but once he notices the scar from the dark blade on Theo's arm, he steps in to kill his final competitor and gift Theo the ring.

Thanks to the sword mark and some good old-fashioned black magic, Sauron learns that Theo and his long-dead father are the latest in a line of an ancient noble house that once swore to serve Mordor. According to him, that stewardship came with "certain powers" passed from father to son.

Naturally, that revelation is a lot for Theo to take in, as is Sauron's claim that his late mother Bronwyn (Nazanin Boniadi) killed his father upon learning of his ties to Mordor. As Theo struggles to process his family history, Sauron tells him: "You have it in you to be a conqueror. A sorcerer. A king."

The pairing of "sorcerer" and "king" all but screams "Witch-king" to Tolkien fans in the know. That wording can't be a coincidence, especially since, as soon as Theo pops the ring on, he's on track to becoming a Nazgûl. That means Theo is one of eight potential candidates to become the Witch-king of Angmar. (Remember, Khamûl is second-in-command to the Witch-king, taking him off the board.) He seems an especially likely candidate given Sauron's whole speech about Theo's mysterious inheritance, and again, the deliberate use of "sorcerer" and "king" in quick succession.

Of course, there's always a chance that this is just Sauron doing what he does best, which is deceive. Maybe he's just spinning a tale to seduce Theo fully over to the dark side. Yet the wording is too purposeful for this to be a total fake-out. The Rings of Power has also invested so much time in Theo and his ties to Middle-earth's darkness. For the Witch-king to be anyone else at this point would feel far less earned.

Plus, The Rings of Power has pulled a comparable move before when it came to heralding the arrival of a classic Lord of the Rings character. In Season 1, it used a Lord of the Rings film quote to imply that the Stranger (Daniel Weyman) is Gandalf, before revealing it outright in the following season. Chances are, the "sorcerer" and "king" lines are similar examples of not-so-subtle foreshadowing, and if Theo doesn't wind up the Witch-king of Angmar, I'll eat my hat. Or rather, my very pointy, very fashionable Nazgnagôl helmet.

#495. ‘We Have to Take Over’: Far-Right Extremists Want to Seize Control of the GOP

On Election Day last week, white nationalist Nick Fuentes urged his supporters on X to “get out and vote Democrat today.” The post racked up over 12 million views. Hours later, in the wake of the Democratic sweep, Fuentes took credit for the victory. “We did it again” he wrote on Telegram, later calling the phenomenon “the Groyper Effect,” a reference to the nickname given to his followers.

In recent days, Fuentes has claimed the election results are an opportunity for him and his allies on the far right.

“Let the Democrats rule; see how that goes,” Fuentes said during a livestream last week. “There will come a time when the far left will terrorize people, and they're going to push all of them into the arms of the right. Who's going to fight the far left? Only the far right. This is why we have to take over the right wing now.”

In another livestream last week, Fuentes added a warning to the GOP leadership: “It’s our party now … You need to cooperate with us… Groypers are open for business. It has [to] be America First—and if it's not, we'll do it again for the enemy.”

A WIRED review of comments from white-supremacist, Christian-nationalist, and other far-right groups in the wake of the midterm results shows much of the same. While the groups are uniformly attacking Donald Trump’s failure to prioritize white, Christian, American values, they also believe that this sudden shift to the left will result in a backlash that will see more people embrace far-right groups and ideologies.

“Many prominent figures in racist, extremist movements have taken to declaring the MAGA movement dead since the midterm elections, claiming that the results validate their previous criticisms of the Trump administration and opens new opportunities for their movements to push disaffected voters toward their ideological beliefs,” Jared Holt, senior researcher at Open Measures, tells WIRED.

While some of the Republican party’s extremist candidates were rejected during the elections last week, many did win their elections.

The Institute for Research and Education on Human Rights tracked 535 candidates running in statewide races who were members of at least one far-right Facebook group. The researchers found that of the 476 races that had been called by last Friday, 338 of those candidates were victorious, representing a 71 percent win rate. (The vast majority of these races saw GOP incumbents retain their seats.) In Florida, for example, 34-year-old citrus farmer Marshall Rawson, who attended the Unite the Right rally in Charlottesville, Virginia, in 2017, easily defeated his Democrat opponent in the state’s 10th District State House race.

Many states also passed ballot measures that could further far-right agendas, including anti-transgender rules, onerous voter ID laws, and anti-immigrant measures.

“Arizona, Colorado, Nebraska, and Washington approved bans on trans athletes in female sports, so girls in Washington state, for instance, will now have to go through a genital exam or pass a DNA test before they can participate in youth sports,” Devin Burghart, executive director with IREHR, said on Friday during a briefing with reporters.

The success of these ballot measures was celebrated by extremist groups as evidence that Americans wanted what the current GOP could not deliver.

The Proud Boys, the street-fighting group that Immigration and Customs Enforcement operations made largely redundant in recent years, were optimistic following the losses. “The ultimate rebuke of the current Republican establishment was citizens voting them out of office, then doing their job for them by passing ballot measures on issues the Republicans should have already handled,” the Cape Fear Proud Boys wrote in their Telegram channel.

“What this signals to me is even in those places where they lost, they're getting 41-47 percent of the vote, [so] there's still a mass base to try to be mobilized,” Chuck Tanner, research director at IREHR, said during the briefing. “That's the lesson that some of these groups are taking out of it, is that there's still that mass base.”

And it is the existence of this base, extremist groups claim, that leaves the Republican Party vulnerable to takeover from the inside.

“Now is the time, if you're a nationalist, to start looking at moving on the Republican Party where you live,” Warren Balogh, a white supremacist from Pittsburgh who attended the Unite the Right rally in Charlottesville in 2017, wrote on his Telegram channel. “And by that I mean, organizing your guys and going in to do a hostile takeover.”

“This may be the best thing that has happened to the Republican Party in a half-century,” the Cape Fear Proud Boys declared in a post on Telegram days after the election. “Now you will learn to placate us, or you will continue to fail. Understand this: You’re going to run people we like, and you’re going to do what we say.”

But other extremists appear less inclined to wait until the next general election in 2028, preferring a more direct takeover.

"I'm not saying that we will win through democracy. I'm just saying we'll win,” Joel Webbon, a Christian nationalist pastor said last week in a video posted online after the election. Webbon and fellow far-right pastors, as well as guest speaker and noted conspiracy theorist Alex Jones, are set to meet at a conference in Texas this week where attendees will discuss, in at least one talk, “battle plans” for “Christian nationalists on the right to be able to seize power.”

Experts WIRED spoke to said they have not seen any explicit calls for violence from the far-right groups they are tracking, but some figures are already looking ahead to January 3, when Congress is set to certify the midterm election results.

Far-right activist and pardoned January 6 rioter Jake Lang, however, is planning an event in DC on January 3. It is unclear how many people are planning to attend, but one of the Christian nationalist pastors listed by Lang as an official speaker has said he won’t be speaking.

Lang, who spent last weekend disrupting a cat convention that he thought was a furry convention, did not respond to a request for comment.

#494. The Dark Wizards true identity in The Lord of the Rings: The Rings of Power is a Tolkien deep cut

If there's one thing The Lord of the Rings: The Rings of Power loves more than a wizard, it's a wizard with a secret identity.

In Season 1, the series introduced the Stranger (Daniel Weyman), who later became known as everyone's favorite Middle-earth wizard: Gandalf. In Season 2, The Rings of Power also brought in another wizard: the Dark Wizard (Ciarán Hinds), who rules over the Eastern land of Rhûn. While the show kept his true name shrouded in secrecy throughout Season 2, it wastes no time revealing it in Season 3, and the answer is a fairly deep-cut character from J.R.R. Tolkien's legendarium.

In a discussion with the Secret Fire (voiced by Allan Corduner), the creative force behind all Middle-earth, Gandalf learns that the Dark Wizard's name is actually Pallando. Like Gandalf, he is an Istar, or "Wise One," who was sent to Middle-earth to help win the fight against Sauron (Charlie Vickers). According to the Secret Fire's handy-dandy exposition dump, Pallando arrived in Middle-earth with a partner. But his ambitions led him to kill his wizard buddy, a move so vile it cursed Rhûn and turned it into a desert wasteland.

Want more film and TV news and reviews delivered straight to your inbox? Sign up for Mashable’s Top Stories newsletter or get Mashable push alerts today.

That's a lot of lore to take in. Like a lot of characters and plot in The Rings of Power, not all of Pallando's story is actually canonical to Tolkien's work. However, several bits and pieces of it are, including the character of Pallando himself. Here's what you need to know.

In the history of Middle-earth, the Valar sent five Istari to Middle-earth. The most familiar of them are Gandalf the Grey, Saruman the White, and Radagast the Brown, all of whom appear in varying degrees in The Lord of the Rings and The Hobbit.

The two remaining wizards were known as the Blue Wizards, or Ithryn Luin, so named for the blue of the robes they wore when arriving in Middle-earth. The Rings of Power captures that blue in its flashback to their arrival, as well as in the stone of Pallando's staff. According to Unfinished Tales of Númenor and Middle-earth, their names are Pallando and Alatar, although in later works he also called them Morinehtar and Rómestámo.

In a 1958 letter, Tolkien wrote, "I really do not know anything clearly about the other two [Istari]," explaining the gap in details between the Blue Wizards and their Istari compatriots — a gap that The Rings of Power is excited to fill in.

Tolkien did not write that Pallando killed Alatar, nor did he write about Pallando setting up shop in Rhûn. However, he did write in the same 1958 letter that he believes Pallando and Alatar were sent "as emissaries to distant regions, east and south." The pair's involvement in Rhûn does fit within that statement, then.

In that letter, Tolkien also wrote, "I fear that they failed, as Saruman did" in their quest to curb Sauron's rise, and that he suspected they founded "secret cults and 'magic' traditions that outlasted the fall of Sauron." Again, The Rings of Power's take on the Blue Wizards technically fits the bill here. Pallando's corruption and murder of Alatar would mark an abject failure of their quest, and he did end up founding his own cult out in Rhûn.

Where The Rings of Power is truly moving away from Tolkien's work is Gandalf's new quest to remind Pallando of his true purpose in Middle-earth. Will he succeed in bringing the one Blue Wizard left back into the light? Plus, now that both Blue Wizards are accounted for (RIP Alatar, we never knew you), what are the odds The Rings of Power introduces the two remaining Istari in its coming episodes? I don't know about you, but I'm officially on Saruman and Radagast watch.

#492. Goodyear Tire CEO Mark Stewart steps down as company continues turnaround plan

Goodyear Tire & Rubber Co. announced Monday that CEO Mark Stewart would be stepping down from his role effective immediately.

Board member Roger Wood is taking over as interim CEO and president, Goodyear said.

The leadership change comes as the tire company is in the midst of a multiyear transformation plan.

Stewart was hired in January 2024 to lead the company, with the goal of revitalizing the legacy American business to make it more efficient and profitable.

The company has faced disappointing results thus far, reporting last week third-quarter results that saw net sales down 2.3% year over year and an adjusted net loss of $31 million. Goodyear failed to reach a 10% operating margin by the end of last year, which was a major goal of the turnaround plan.

Goodyear said it formed a special committee of the board earlier this year to conduct a "strategic review of the company's businesses and operations focused on identifying opportunities to strengthen performance and drive shareholder value."

It said Monday it will continue that work by evaluating strategic and operational initiatives that include further optimizing the company's business mix and footprint, improving efficiency and investing in new products and capabilities that support long-term growth.

"Goodyear has made meaningful progress in becoming a more efficient and focused company, and the board believes this is the right moment to transition leadership to accelerate that progress and capture additional opportunity," Goodyear Chair Joe Hinrichs said in a press release.

The company has faced headwinds from tariffs, higher raw material costs and increased competition from China.

Wood has been a member of the company's board of directors since July 2023 and was previously the co-CEO of Tenneco as well as CEO of Dana Holding Corporation.

The company will start a search for a permanent replacement as part of its strategic review.

Shares of Goodyear were down roughly 4% in early trading Monday.

#490. The Body review: Yellowjackets meets The Crucible

Mix the teen-girl ferocity of Yellowjackets with the moral panic of The Crucible, dress it up in a blinged-out Juicy Couture tracksuit, and you'll get The Body.

The beguiling new series from Netflix and creator Quinn Shephard (Under the Bridge) whisks viewers into the halls of St. Mark's Catholic School in 2004, where the school's all-girl dance team is about to face a reckoning of Biblical proportions.

Want more film and TV news and reviews delivered straight to your inbox? Sign up for Mashable’s Top Stories newsletter or get Mashable push alerts today.

In the lead-up to Regionals, the St. Mark's Lions have recruited a new dancer, devout Grace (Sofia Wylie), to fill in for injured performer Maddie (Shirley Chen). But one good audition doesn't mean she automatically makes the team. Grace has to be initiated first. And what better place to do it than at a Halloween party at an abandoned convent known as Devil's House?

There, Lions captain Anya (Kristina Bogic) and her best friend Elise (Sara Boustany) cook up a perverse communion, where they swap the body of Christ (communion wafers) for the body of Satan (ecstasy pills). Then, they playfully beg the Devil himself to take Grace's soul as a sacrifice for a victory at Regionals.

Of course, it's all in good fun. But by the end of the night, Grace has fallen into a coma, and Anya finds herself relying on outsider Lisbeth (Geena Meszaros) to help cover up the team's tracks. Now, the Lions must close ranks to cover up their dark secret.

As strange visions begin to plague the team members, they have to face a disturbing possibility: Did they actually summon Satan?

For the most part, The Body keeps the possibility of the Devil's true involvement ambiguous. Anya, who has the most to lose if the truth comes out, is haunted by visions of Grace. But are these apparitions truly Grace speaking from beyond the veil? Or just a manifestation of Anya's guilt? And are her teammates actually seeing Grace too? Or just claiming they've had visions, Crucible-style? (While The Body doesn't invoke Arthur Miller's play by name, Shepard's 2017 feature directorial debut Blame centers on high school rivals reading The Crucible, making The Body feel like a natural extension of her prior work.)

Instead of putting together a full mystery box to provide a definitive answer, The Body prefers to examine the impacts of this stressful situation on the fraught ecosystem that is the Lions dance team. The visions become a kind of social currency. Those who "receive" them feel as if they've been "chosen" by Grace. Those who haven't wonder why Grace hasn't visited them (or fake visions of their own to join the club).

The effect on the viewer is a slippery one, placing us in a constant state of debate. Should we put our faith in Grace and the supernatural? Or should we recognize that Grace is being used as a step on the rung to popularity and assimilation?

Amidst this growing hysteria, a fraught triangle forms between Anya, Elise, and Lisbeth. Anya and Elise's hangouts frequently cross the line between platonic friendship and attraction, but Anya's Catholic guilt prevents her from truly accepting her desires for Elise. Her inability to commit fully to her friend drives a wedge between the two. So when Anya needs help on Halloween, it's not Elise who's there, but Lisbeth.

Coming from an intense religious background — complete with speaking in tongues and snake handling — Lisbeth is on a mission to make new friends and get on the dance team at any cost. If that means sowing further discord between Anya and Elise, so be it.

The tension between the three serves as the delicious dramatic engine that powers The Body. With Blame, Shepard already proved herself adept at dissecting the cruelties teen girls can inflict on one another. She continues that study here, with the help of her three excellent leads. Bogic excels as the increasingly frazzled Anya, melding high school archetypes like "popular princess with a heart of gold" and "mean girl" into a character who feels grounded.

Boustany instantly electrifies the screen as TV's next Cool Girl. Her sardonic affect recalls Sophie Thatcher's memorable performance as Natalie in Yellowjackets in the best possible way.

Meszaros catapults between sympathetic and downright scary. Her Lisbeth is as slippery and unpredictable as the snakes the character once handled. Together, the trio leads a talented ensemble of young performers whose dynamic feels ripped from a high school hallway.

That dynamic elevates The Body, with every Lions practice or locker room debrief bursting with rich interplay between the team. Each insult and loaded glance feels real. And when The Body truly lets its girls loose — be it through underhanded backstabbing or violent rituals — the results are vicious and captivating. Even as The Body spirals towards a series of nebulous, even frustrating loose ends, its core characters keep the ship afloat.

Through it all, The Body walks a mesmerizing tonal tight rope. The series is primarily a psychological thriller, turning high school hallways into claustrophobic nightmares where any slip could be game over for the Lions. The claustrophobia extends to The Body's examination of religion as a controlling force. Anya struggles with internalized homophobia. Lisbeth's mother (Louisa Krause) berates her daughter for straying from God. Thanks to this emphasis on religious guilt, images of Christ on the cross soon begin to feel more like signs of judgmental surveillance than of salvation. Crucifix necklaces feel more like weights on the soul than signs of faith.

The Body balances these headier debates about religion with its dark comedy sensibilities. Look no further than the rude sniping between rival dance groups, each with their own highly suggestive, yet supposedly faith-based dance routine. (The Lions' routine, set to Kylie Minogue's "Confide in Me," breaks up the girls' gyrations with a lift in the shape of a cross.)

The Body's attention to nostalgic, early 2000s details is also one of the show's biggest blessings. AIM chatrooms and killer needle drops abound. Plus, the show's title cards are gloriously bedazzled, Juicy-style.

Between these elements and a throbbing choral soundtrack, there's a transgressive fun to The Body's aesthetic, and to the series as a whole. Atmospheric, unsettling, and amusing in equal parts, The Body is nothing short of a devilishly good time.

#487. Ethereum Foundation sued for firing cancer patient who objected to mandated suicide pledge

The Ethereum Foundation (EF) is being sued after firing an employee shortly after she objected to an alleged mandated suicide pledge. The dismissal also came within weeks of her revealing her cancer diagnosis.

EF is a non-profit that supports the cryptocurrency platform Ethereum. Filed to the District Court of Colorado on Nov. 4, the complaint claims EF fired Protocol Support team lead Kayleen Fulton (also known as Nixo) after she coordinated a collective letter opposing a suicide pledge all employees had been directed to affirm. The "Source Seppuku License" had been included in a document entitled "The Ethereum Foundation Mandate," which was distributed to all employees on Feb. 25:

"By inclusion of this license, the author, maintainer, editor or (re)distributor (the 'actor') of this software SHALL uphold the following pledges:

To always make freely available and publicly accessible a full and accurate copy of the source code and associated documents of this software, including all modifications hereto by the Actor.

To take his or her own life with a sword upon failure to uphold any of the pledges in this license, or upon modification or removal of any part of this license.

May we ritualistically fall on our own swords if we fail to uphold our solemn promise to Ethereum and the Infinite Garden."

Multiple EF employees allegedly objected to the license, one noting that a colleague had just killed themselves in 2025. These criticisms prompted EF's board to revise the mandate. However, rather than remove the Seppuku License, the finalised 38-page mandate issued on March 13 reportedly reformatted it into a Comic Sans-style font, made it less prominent, and illustrated it with cartoon cats and an anime girl pointing a gun at the reader.

EF employees were allegedly given until March 25 to pledge their commitment to the mandate via an online form, with failure to do so considered a resignation. EF co-executive director Bastian Aue reportedly did state that the mandate's reference to ritual suicide was not intended to be literal, however did not remove it from the document.

"Employees were thus asked to accept, on management’s word in a chat thread, that a clause the board had just spent a week redrawing rather than deleting did not mean what it said," read the complaint.

Fulton refused to sign the pledge, and worked with colleague Trent Van Epps to draft a letter to EF's board voicing their objections. Signed by several dozen other workers, it reportedly stated that while they agreed with the mandate's "general direction," they could not agree to it as it stood.

"We will continue to perform the duties with which we are tasked and do not resign," read the letter Fulton emailed to all EF staff on March 25. "Many of us have signed the commitment to the mandate under duress in fear of losing their jobs and livelihoods. We consider their responses on the poll to be coerced and meaningless."

Aue allegedly told staff that he accepted the letter as committing to the mandate while voicing "frustrations with the process and form." However, EF fired both Fulton and Van Epps on April 6, less than two weeks after the email was sent, reportedly with no cause given. While none of the letter's other signatories were immediately dismissed, the complaint states that most had resigned or been fired by September. The best tech deal this week The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile Products available for purchase through affiliate links. If you buy something through links on our site, Mashable may earn an affiliate commission.

The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

The Ethereum Foundation Mandate is currently available on EF's website, including the Source Seppuku License.

Fulton and Van Epps' termination came within an hour of Aue allegedly issuing a company-wide statement on how to raise concerns, including use of tone. In it, he reportedly stated that if employees' confidence in EF was shaken, "it is likely better for both you, the EF, and Ethereum if you look for a way to contribute to Ethereum from outside the EF."

According to the complaint, EF board member Vitalik Buterin subsequently told Fulton in May that "if you reward making a stink with outcomes, then everyone will know that essentially making that kind of a show both internally and externally is the way to cause outcomes inside of the EF."

"Mr. Buterin’s account… explains [Fulton's] termination as a deterrent: the organizers of the Protocol letter were removed so that the remaining staff would learn that organizing does not 'cause outcomes inside of the EF,'" claimed the complaint. "Defendants acted intentionally, willfully and wantonly, with malice, and with reckless indifference to Ms. Fulton's federally and state protected rights."

The lawsuit claims Fulton had never been criticised for her work, and that her supervisor had rated her performance at EF as "greatly exceeding expectations" seven months prior to her dismissal. Her termination also occurred amidst her cancer diagnosis, which she was informed of on March 19 and disclosed to EF on March 24. Fulton considers this diagnosis as a parallel motivation for her termination, as Aue allegedly stated during her dismissal that a cancer patient would could not offer the "exclusive and energetic focus" EF required from employees.

"Ms. Fulton has suffered and continues to suffer emotional distress, anxiety, humiliation, and loss of professional standing as a result of being fired in the first weeks after a cancer diagnosis, without explanation, by an organization that had just ordered her to affirm a suicide pledge, while facing the loss of her health insurance during treatment, and then being given three days to sign away her rights or lose even the health stipend she had been offered," read the complaint.

Fulton alleges EF violated the Americans with Disabilities Act, Family and Medical Leave Act, and the Colorado Anti-Discrimination Act, and is seeking backpay and lost benefits, frontpay in lieu of reinstatement, punitive damages, and compensation for emotional distress. Mashable has reached out to EF for comment.

You can reach the Trans Lifeline by calling 877-565-8860 or the Trevor Project at 866-488-7386. Text "START" to Crisis Text Line at 741-741.
