#484. Honda to invest $3 billion in new vehicle assembly plant in Ohio

MARYSVILLE, Ohio — Honda Motor plans to invest $3 billion to build a new vehicle assembly plant in Ohio, the automaker announced Monday.

Honda said the new plant, which will be near its other facilities in Marysville, Ohio, is expected to begin production in 2029, have a capacity for 240,000 vehicles annually and create more than 2,000 jobs.

The facility is expected to be capable of producing traditional vehicles with internal combustion engines, all-electric vehicles and hybrid models, which have been growing in popularity in the U.S.

Honda declined to disclose what vehicles the company plans to produce at the new facility.

The plant is expected to increase Honda's light-duty vehicle capacity to 1.3 million units annually in the U.S. It currently has 12 manufacturing plants in United States, eight of which produce engines or light-duty vehicles for the auto industry.

"This is not simply an investment in another plant. It is an investment in the future of Honda manufacturing in America," Bob Nelson, executive vice president of American Honda Motor, said during the announcement at the company's U.S. heritage center in Ohio.

The auto industry has been trying to localize more production amid President Donald Trump's tariffs and inflated logistics costs that have cut into profits.

Nelson told CNBC after the event that tariffs are affecting the company's business but the new plant is more about Honda's ambitions to grow in the U.S.

"Tariffs are an impact to us, even with the high localization rate that we have," he said. "But the real impact is that we have customers out there wanting Honda product and we can't give it to them. That's the real area."

Nelson said Honda did not work with the Trump administration ahead of Monday's announcement.

Honda's plans for a new plant come roughly two weeks after Volkswagen-owned luxury brand Audi announced it would invest more than $4 billion in a new plant at a rally with President Donald Trump, which Audi CEO Gernot Dollner faced criticism for attending.

Honda's new plant is the company's first new mass production U.S. vehicle assembly plant since it opened a facility in Indiana in 2008. It also opened a $70 million performance vehicle facility in 2016 and a multibillion-dollar joint venture battery plant earlier this year, both in Ohio.

The announcement comes after the automaker in May indefinitely suspended an $11 billion investment plan to produce all-electric vehicles and batteries in Canada. It also canceled three planned EVs for the U.S. market.

American Honda Motor Co. executive Jennifer Thomas recently emphasized the Japanese automaker's commitment to the U.S. and need for additional capacity.

"In order to be competitive in this market, you're going to need to build products here," Thomas, senior vice president of corporate affairs, said Oct. 27 in Detroit. "We are focused on trying to maximize the existing capacity that we have, but we are at capacity, and so we're exploring all options."

Thomas also warned the automotive industry faces a "catastrophic" scenario if the U.S. and Canada cannot reach a new trade deal by the end of this year, when Trump has threatened to double tariffs on Canadian vehicles and parts from 25% to 50% on Jan. 1 if the two countries don't reach a deal.

Honda said it has built more than 33 million vehicles and more than 39 million engines in the U.S. since it started auto production in the U.S. in 1982.

The Japanese automaker currently has more than 30,000 employees in the U.S.

#478. Goldman CEO Solomon says AI financing remains strong despite market jitters

Goldman Sachs CEO David Solomon told CNBC on Monday that financing demand for artificial intelligence infrastructure remains strong despite recent market jitters as investors reassess the risks involving the massive build-out.

Solomon said that his firm has been "very active" in AI capital markets deals over recent weeks, a setup that he expects to continue.

"There's still very strong demand for investments in the AI infrastructure build and the technology around the growth of AI," Solomon told CNBC's Leslie Picker.

Changes in the political environment are among factors that will "create recalibrations and ups and downs, and we're seeing some of that," Solomon said. "But I still deeply believe in the long-term trajectory of the power of AI in our economy to drive productivity."

AI-related stocks, including chipmakers and neocloud providers, have tumbled in recent weeks as investors reassess whether the hundreds of billions of dollars being poured into AI infrastructure will generate good returns.

The selloff, which began before the U.S. midterm elections, intensified after Democrats scored sweeping victories on Tuesday, raising concerns that political opposition could slow the industry's expansion.

The shifting sentiment comes at a key moment for AI model lab Anthropic, which is preparing for a potential IPO that could become one of the biggest tests yet of public-market appetite for AI companies. Goldman is among the banks leading that IPO process.

"We'll see, obviously, if this IPO moves forward, how it all plays out," Solomon said, declining to comment in detail about the IPO. "I would say that the market environment is still pretty constructive."

Solomon also pushed back against political resistance to data center construction, arguing that the infrastructure is essential to the U.S. economy and that it can be built responsibly.

"We certainly don't want this infrastructure going to places outside the U.S., which is what will naturally happen because the demand for compute is there," Solomon said.

You will notice Walmart is missing from our quick-buy links above. While the controller has a product listing on the site, stock sold directly by Walmart for the official $99.99 list price is completely gone.

Every buy button currently active is run by third-party marketplace resellers charging inflated, price-gouging sums. Do not pay those prices. If you search Walmart on your own, always filter the seller option to "Walmart" so you only see genuine retail inventory.

Until the production lines catch up, expect store inventory to pop up in quick, random bursts rather than steady, predictable drops.

The special-edition Pro Controller gamepad features decorative illustrations and subtle gold patterns inspired by the wider Zelda universe. It carries every modern upgrade found on the standard generation-two pad. You get built-in motion sensors for precision bow aiming, HD Rumble 2 for detailed vibration feedback, an integrated amiibo scanner, and a capture button for grabbing screenshots on the fly.

Landing a surprise drop comes down to speed and a little bit of prep:

Dodge third-party marketplace sellers: Sites like Walmart and Amazon let outside merchants charge whatever they want. Always check the "Sold and shipped by" label to ensure you are buying from the actual retailer at the $99.99 list price.

Save your checkout information beforehand: Taking the time to type in a security code or shipping address during a drop will cost you your cart. Keep your profiles logged in with default payment methods saved.

Do not refresh Best Buy's waiting line: When Best Buy runs a drop, it puts shoppers into an automated queue. Hitting refresh resets your spot and sends you to the very back of the line.

Check physical stores in the morning: Online checkout bots can wipe out digital inventory in seconds. Visiting Target, Best Buy, or GameStop right when doors open often turns up fresh shipment boxes that never made it onto website listings.

Retailers love using limited-edition hardware and themed accessories as digital bait during Black Friday and Cyber Monday. It is very likely that stores are sitting on a handful of units reserved specifically for holiday sales events.

The catch is that Black Friday restocks are pure chaos. You are up against overloaded checkout servers, holiday gift shoppers, and scalper scripts all hitting the site at once. If an "Add to Cart" button flickers live on Amazon or Best Buy before the holiday rush officially starts, take the shot immediately so you don't have to fight the crowds later.

Finding one right now is definitely an endurance test, but holding out for that $99.99 list price beats paying double to a reseller every time.

#474. Boeing to cut 5% of commercial airplane workers as production increase takes longer than expected

Boeing said Monday that it plans to cut 5% of its commercial airplane workforce as its production ramp-up is moving at a slower pace than the company expected.

The cuts will be through a combination of attrition and layoffs, Stephanie Pope, head of the commercial aircraft unit, said in a memo to staff.

Pope reiterated that the company is still working to stabilize production rates of its bestselling 737 Max planes, which are made outside of Seattle.

CEO Kelly Ortberg told investors last month that the company won't significantly increase production of the Max aircraft until the second half of next year. He said the company's wing production has been slower than expected.

"Adjusting our production schedules and reducing aircraft output puts us in an overstaffed position," Pope said in her note Monday. "Unfortunately, that means we need to align our staffing levels to our revised business plan, resulting in a reduction of approximately 5% through a combination of attrition and involuntary layoffs, with notifications beginning in December."

Boeing is targeting a production rate of 52 planes a month in the second half of 2027. Ortberg said the company has been trying to stabilize output at a rate of about 47 of its 737 Max planes a month.

#470. Subtle design changes make the 2026 Kindle Amazons best budget-friendly e-reader

After careful hands-on testing, we feature our favorite gear and gadgets as Mashable Selects products in our dedicated shopping guides.

It's been a tough year for e-readers (and all tech) with prices on the rise, so we finally needed some good news. On Oct. 1, Amazon released a redesigned collection of Kindles that we simply couldn't wait to try out. The first to be released is the flagship Kindle. It's the entry point into Amazon's e-readers and the most affordable.

The Kindle (2026) now features a flush-front design with either a plastic or an aluminum backing, and in this new batch of Kindles, Amazon is embracing color with the e-reader available in ube, graphite, alpine sky, and seaglass green. So I got my hands on the newest Kindle to experience the design changes and put it to the test.

Update Nov. 11, 5:00 a.m. ET: Since first publishing this review, we've encountered an issue with the back panel of the Kindle coming apart when removing it from a case. This is part of the overall redesign to make the back panel easily removable so you can replace the battery. We have changed our scoring of the device, lowering its user-friendliness score to a 4 and overall score to a 4.3.

There weren't many changes between the 2022 and 2024 Kindles, but this launch brings much bigger changes that you can actually see.

The 2026 Kindle now has a flush front compared to the 2024 model's raised border around the screen. This provides a uniform look across all Kindles from the basic model all the way up to the Scribe.

A smaller change, but one that I'm more than thrilled to see, is that the design team moved the power button on the Kindle to the side of the device. While it's just a small detail, my colleague Stan Schroeder pointed it out in his Kindle Paperwhite Signature Edition (2021) review, and I did the same in my 2024 Kindle review.

Otherwise, many of the e-reader's specs are the same between generations. The device still has a six-inch screen with 300 ppi resolution and a built-in front light. It has up to six weeks of battery life, but it is not waterproof, nor does it have an adjustable warm light.

The first thing I noticed about the new Kindle is just how featherlight it is — I almost thought the box was empty. Luckily, it was not. Amazon dropped the weight of the basic Kindle by 18 grams (.66 ounces). While it doesn't seem like much, it's a noticeable difference when holding the device.

This lighter weight makes all the difference, especially when traveling. My first weekend with the Kindle, I packed it in my carry-on bag, and it barely added any weight. It tucks nicely into small pockets, too. While it isn't quite palm-sized like a trendy Boox Palma or Xteink X4, it's Amazon's smallest e-reader and still extremely portable. Plus, you can hold it for hours at a time without your arm aching. The best tech deal this week The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

Every time Amazon releases a new batch of Kindles, it claims the new e-readers are faster than the previous generation. The 2026 Kindle is supposedly 30 percent faster than the 2024. While I can't verify that exact amount, I can confirm that these are speedy devices. Books open quickly, and pages turn swiftly. Since I spent so little time waiting for things to load on the Kindle, I had more time to read, which was smooth and easy on the eyes.

The front light on the Kindle is clear and bright (though I do wish it had adjustable warmth like the Paperwhite), and the display responds quickly. When I changed the font size, it took less than a second to adjust. The screen is responsive and definitely seems to open faster than previous models, especially when tapping through the options along the bottom of the screen to adjust your progress status.

The new Kindle is a really seamless reading experience. While I don't have many complaints, two things could make it a perfect e-reader. Since this is the entry-level Kindle, Amazon keeps affordability top of mind, which does mean there are sacrifices. There's no warm light on the basic Kindle, which would reduce blue-light strain on the eyes. Plus, I wish it were waterproof. I'm a beach reader, so the basic Kindle certainly won't be a summer companion for me, as I wouldn't want to put it near a splash zone.

However, adjustable warm light and waterproofing are both features on the Paperwhite, so we really can't fault the base model for not having them. If those are non-negotiables for you, upgrade to the Kindle Paperwhite.

Testing is always ongoing here at Mashable, as we never want our reviews to be based only on first impressions. After using the Kindle for several weeks, I went to remove it from its case when the frame pulled apart. The back panel on the new Kindle is designed to be removable so users can replace the battery.

While I love the intention behind the design choice, in execution, the back panel comes apart whenever I remove it from the case, which exposes the innards of the device. This isn't an isolated case, as I've found instances of Kindle users on Reddit and TikTok who have experienced the same thing with the 2026 Kindle.

An Amazon spokesperson commented on the issue of the back panel saying, "Your Kindle is designed to open along the edge of the display in order to replace the battery. If the back panel shifts when you remove a case or cover, press it firmly back into place on all four corners and along the edges, and keep reading."

Luckily, the back panel coming apart only occurs when removing it from a case, so as long as you're not frequently taking it on and off, this shouldn't be a persistent issue. And even if it does come apart, it doesn't affect the performance of the device. However, we do think this impacts the overall structural integrity of the device and hinders the user experience, which is why we lowered the user-friendliness score on the device from a 5 to a 4.

Amazon's latest Kindle is an exceptional e-reader. It's lightweight and compact, quick and responsive. Sure, it has a couple of misses, like no warm light, no waterproof rating, and the precarious back panel, but those are for the sake of affordability, according to Amazon. At $149.99, it's Amazon's cheapest e-reader. It's also the same price as the 2024 model, so if you're deciding between the two, go for the new 2026 Kindle.

However, if you're looking to upgrade from another Kindle model, I don't think you necessarily need to run out to grab it, especially if you have a 2022 or 2024 model. However, if you want to use the Kindle Click, you will need to upgrade to a 2026 (or 2024) Kindle.

All Kindles and e-readers are tested through daily use and assessed using the same criteria. While using the Kindle, I considered the following:

Size and weight: I evaluated the size and shape of the Kindle, including whether it was easy to read one-handed and whether it fit in small bags.

Special features: I analyzed the Kindle's range of features, including adjustable warmth and brightness and a waterproof rating (or lack thereof).

Price: Kindle models vary widely in price; based on its features, I assessed whether the Kindle's price is a good value.

Ease of use: How effortless was downloading and reading new books? Was it easy to organize the homepage and get library books on the Kindle?

#469. 2023 March Madness: Will UConn or San Diego State triumph in the national championship game that 'means everything?'

The University of Connecticut won its fifth men’s basketball national title with a 76-59 victory over San Diego State University on Monday night at NRG Stadium in Houston.

Senior guard Tristen Newton led UConn (31-8) with 19 points and 10 rebounds while Final Four Most Outstanding Player Adama Sanogo, a junior forward, chipped in with 17 points and 10 rebounds.

“We weren’t ranked going into the year so we had the chip on our shoulder,” UConn head coach Dan Hurley told game broadcaster CBS. “We knew the level that we could play at, even through those dark times,” he added, referencing the team’s six losses in eight games during the regular season.

He said going into the tournament his group had confidence garnered during the season.

“And when you have the type of leaders like Andre Jackson (game-high six assists Monday) and Adama Sanogo, they kept this team together, got us back on track and we knew we were the best team in the tournament going in and we just had to play to our level,” he added.

San Diego State (32-7) was topped by Keshad Johnson who had 14 points.

UConn trailed very early but San Diego State was undone by an 11-minute, eight-second stretch in which they scored just five free throws and missed 12 consecutive shots from the field. The Huskies went from down 10-6 to up 36-24 at halftime.

The Aztecs made a run midway through the second half and narrowed the deficit to five at 60-55 with 5:19 to play but the Huskies scored the next nine to take a comfortable lead into the final two minutes.

“We battled. Battled back to five in the second half, but gave them too much separation,” San Diego State coach Brian Dutcher said. “We had to be at our best. We weren’t at our best. A lot had to do with UConn.”

Senior guard Adam Seiko told reporters they gave themselves a chance with their second half comeback but UConn “just made a little bit more plays” at the end.

“They have a lot of weapons. They were pretty good,” said Matt Bradley, also a senior guard. “To beat them, we had to make shots. I shot poorly. And you had to have a really good game to beat those dudes on the offensive end.”

UConn won each of its six tournament games by at least 10 points, with its closest game being a 13-point win over the University of Miami in the national semifinals.

“I just want to thank my teammates, my coaches who believed in me. If it were not for them I would not be here right now,” Sanogo told CBS.

Jordan Hawkins, who scored 16 points for UConn, talked about winning the crown one day after his cousin, Angel Reese of Louisiana State University, won the women’s title.

“I mean it’s absolutely amazing that we both get this opportunity and I mean the family reunion is going to be great so that’s all I know,” he said.

UConn enters rarefied air as only the sixth team to win five NCAA men’s basketball championships, joining UCLA (11), Kentucky (eight), North Carolina (six), Duke (five) and Indiana (five). All of UConn’s titles have come since 1999 with the most recent before Monday occurring in 2014.

UConn’s women’s teams have won 11 basketball national titles.

CNN’s Steve Almasy and Issy Ronald contributed to this report.

#467. PepsiCo to cut about 1,000 jobs as North American business struggles

PepsiCo is cutting about 1,000 jobs across North America as the company tries to turn around lagging sales in its home market.

"We have a clear ambition for PepsiCo North America: to build a stronger, more future-fit business that delivers industry-leading growth," Ram Krishnan, CEO of PepsiCo North America, wrote in a Monday letter to employees obtained by CNBC. "To do that, we need to be faster, more connected, and more capable, while continuing to invest in growth — behind our people, capabilities and brands."

Over the next week, Pepsi plans to cut jobs as it says it aims to streamline its operations and better connect teams across the company's North American business. PepsiCo employs about 306,000 people worldwide, including roughly 125,000 in the United States, as of Dec. 27, according to a company filing.

The Wall Street Journal first reported the coming layoffs.

In October, PepsiCo cut its full-year earnings outlook, citing the struggles in its home market. During the third quarter, its North American beverage unit saw volume shrink 2%, while its North American food division reported flat volume.

While the company has tried to boost sales by cutting prices and investing in marketing, the turnaround is taking longer than expected, Chief Financial Officer Steve Schmitt said on the company's earnings conference call.

Shares of Pepsi rose slightly in afternoon trading. The stock is down nearly 1% this year, lagging behind the S&P 500's gains of nearly 11% over the same period.

#466. PS5 vs. PS5 Pro: 4 key differences to know before you buy

When you go to a store looking to buy a current-gen PlayStation 5 console, you're faced with two possibilities: the base PS5, or the PS5 Pro. At least, in theory. The PS5 Pro has been very hard to find in stock lately, unless you turn to the resale market.

Besides the promise of more horsepower, it can be hard to suss out exactly what makes the PS5 Pro so different, especially with its hugely inflated price tag. With the promise of an enhanced Grand Theft Auto VI experience, though, many are wondering if they should bite the bullet and pick up Sony's most powerful console. Let's dive into the key differences between the base model console and its professional counterpart.

The first and most important thing we need to discuss regarding Sony's two PS5 consoles is the price. Neither of them is cheap, but one of them is clearly a better value at this point in time.

That would be the base PS5, which starts at $599. It will play all of the same games as a PS5 Pro, including nearly every game released for PS4, and the controller is no different, either. We'll delve into some of the other differences in a bit, but you definitely shouldn't think of this as a last-gen console.

Now for the tough part: The PS5 Pro starts at a staggering $899, and that's just the MSRP. Because of the ongoing PS5 Pro shortages, you will likely have to pay even more on the secondary market.

Now we're going to move on to the areas where the PS5 Pro has an unambiguous advantage over the base model. After all, they aren't charging you $899 for no reason.

One of the biggest reasons to get a PS5 Pro? It comes with 2TB of built-in storage, compared to just 1TB on any base PS5 model. Considering some games like GTA 6 can take up more than 200GB of storage space by themselves, a 2TB starting limit is a huge benefit for the PS5 Pro. You might think 1TB is a lot, and it's certainly adequate if you're really careful about deleting games you're done playing, but that base PS5 drive can fill up quickly. The best tech deal this week The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

To be clear, there is always the option to buy a base PS5 and expand the storage yourself, but that requires buying another SSD. I don't know if you've seen how much memory costs these days, but that could be a boondoggle in and of itself.

While expanded storage is nice, the real selling point of the PS5 Pro is that it's a more powerful console than the PS5. Again, it's not a full generational leap, and both consoles play the exact same games, but the PS5 Pro has access to more horsepower than the base model PS5, and there are some real benefits to that.

For starters, the PS5 Pro has access to PlayStation Spectral Super Resolution, or PSSR. This is an AI upscaling feature that can make supported games look substantially sharper than they would on a base PS5. There are also a bunch of games that have enhanced performance on PS5 Pro, and in some cases, you even get access to new graphics modes that enable perks such as 60 frames per second and ray tracing (which can also be improved on PS5 Pro) at the same time. Ghost of Yotei comes to mind as a recent example of that.

However, the onus is on developers to specifically add support for these special PS5 Pro features, meaning that the list of PS5 Pro-enhanced games is in the dozens, not the hundreds. In the rare instances when Sony decides to actually release first-party exclusives, you can usually count on those to have some kind of PS5 Pro benefits, but otherwise, most games are going to be roughly the same across both consoles.

Last but definitely not least, the PS5 Pro does not come with a disc drive at all. Neither does the $599 base model console, for that matter. If you want a PS5 with a built-in disc drive, you need to spend $649 for a base model with one. Given Sony's recent disdain for physical discs, the inconvenience here might be by design.

That doesn't mean starting-price PS5 or Pro owners have no options, though. You can always buy the official disc drive attachment...for an additional $80. Aside from costing more, it also juts out from the console in a somewhat unpleasant-looking way. None of this is ideal, frankly, but that's what you need to do if you want to keep buying physical games or Blu-ray discs as a PS5 owner.

#463. Review: The 2nd gen Bose Ultra Open earbuds are even better than the originals

When I tell folks in my life that I review headphones and earbuds for a living, I'm usually asked which I like the best. There's only one pair people have specifically asked me my opinion on by name: the Bose Ultra Open earbuds.

I get why — they are the earbuds, after all, that gave open earbuds their moment and kicked off a wave of releases from other brands. And to answer the question I've been asked many times: they're great, and one of the best options if you're looking for the best possible sound on open earbuds, combined with an excellent (and comfortable) design.

All this to say, I was intrigued to try the second-gen open earbuds for myself after their announcement in September. After a few weeks of wearing them regularly, I can confirm: Bose has done it again.

The second-generation Bose Ultra Open earbuds retail for $299, the same as the first-gen Ultra Opens (at least they were spared from any price increases). On the spec side, they come with:

Battery: Nine hours per charge, plus 19.5 hours in the case

Connection: Bluetooth 5.4, SBC and AAC codecs, Google Fast Pair

Colors: Black, white smoke, limited-edition cherry chocolate, olive green, sky pink

Admittedly, open earbuds aren't exactly an audiophile's dream — in most cases, they struggle to achieve the full quality found on traditional earbuds or over-ear headphones. That said, the second-gen Bose open earbuds get about as close as you could hope.

I used them back-to-back with the first-gen open buds, and the difference was immediately noticeable. The thumping bass on Nelly Furtado's "Maneater" was resonant and punchy, whereas on the first-gen buds it fell crunchy and flat. It makes sense — Bose packed a new driver in these earbuds targeted to improve bass performance. It wasn't just the lows that came through well. The discordant electric guitar that rounds out Lucy Dacus' "Night Shift" came through with more clarity, detail, and power. This out-of-the-box sound makes up for the limited three-band equalizer (which feels less egregious on open earbuds, because again, audiophile earbuds they are not).

On the note of power, it's worth knowing these earbuds also get loud. As in, probably too loud to use at full volume in a quiet room without incurring some kind of ear damage. That said, the feature can come in handy if you're in a noisier space while listening — the amount of sound open earbuds let in sometimes requires a higher volume than you would typically use. It's an edge over the somewhat quiet Sony LinkBuds Clip, which are likely Bose's biggest competitor.

Less of a standout, but still worth noting, is the addition of Immersive Audio, which opens up the soundscape. I've never been a huge fan of Bose's offering here, but I found it nicely complemented the open-ear design, especially with live performances. The best tech deal this week The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

It's not the hugest change, but the first-gen Bose buds were starting to lag in the battery life department compared to the rest of the open earbud field. The jump from seven to nine hours per charge is a welcome one, putting these Ultras on par with the Sony earbuds (though Sony gets the edge in 37 hours with case compared to Bose's 19.5).

Bose didn't make any major changes here, and that's for the best. The cylindrical backing on these buds is large enough to provide a secure fit, while small enough to retain a comfortable fit. Plus, it's the perfect anchor for the on-ear button control situated on the top of the cylinder. The button relies on a firm enough press (and a responsive click) that it's basically impossible to trigger accidentally but still incredibly easy to access, making it the best on-earbud controls I've encountered on an open model.

As for the rest of the clip-on bud, it's frictionless to change the position and resecure the buds to get the best possible listening experience. And boy, are these buds lightweight. I forgot to resume my audio after chatting with my roommate for a bit — it wasn't until hours later when I went to adjust my earring that I remembered I was wearing the buds at all.

The more noticeable (albeit small) design upgrade was in the charging case. Bose opted for a more rectangular shape with a flat silicone bottom that allows the case to stand up on its own. Adjusting accordingly, the pairing button is moved to the left side of the case (instead of the lower part of the back of the case), while the USB-C charging port is moved to the right (instead of on the bottom).

At $299, these earbuds cost $50 more than the Apple AirPods Pro 3, Apple's premium noise-cancelling earbuds. While I value the excellent sound quality that these open buds are capable of, outdoing Apple in the price category warrants a moment of reflection in my humble opinion.

Even if these earbuds matched Apple's $249 price point, they'd match the price of Shokz premium open earbuds, and be within spitting distance of the $229 Sony LinkBuds Clip earbuds. In other words, these earbuds would be much more of a slam dunk to recommend if they cost just a bit less.

At the end of the day, if you want the best of the best sound quality on open earbuds, you have to shell out for it — likely more than you would for standard noise-cancelling earbuds. For some, though, the investment will be well worth it. The upgrades in sound quality and design also help the Boses stand out (or, in the case of the battery life, close the gap), putting some more justification behind their price point.

Me personally? I'd wait until Black Friday to see if Bose is feeling generous with their discounts on newer products.

I spent a few weeks using the Bose Ultra Open earbuds regularly throughout the week. During my testing period, I evaluated the following:

Sound quality: I listened to more bass-forward tracks to put Bose's new driver to the test, as well as testing music from across the genre spectrum, from jazz to classic rock. I also made sure to listen to the new Immersive Audio mode and to test the earbuds side by side with their predecessor.

Comfort: Open earbuds are meant to be worn during everything from exercise to holding a conversation. If they're not comfortable, it doesn't really matter that you can hear the world around you — you'll likely just want to take them off. With that in mind, I wore these earbuds for entire workdays, walking around, and during exercise to truly put their comfort levels to the test.

Overall design: Beyond comfort, open earbuds can be annoying if they don't stay secure, have a large carrying case, or have on-ear controls that don't quite hit the mark. The Bose buds thankfully didn't suffer from any of the above.

Value: I evaluated how all of the above factored into the high price point of these open earbuds, particularly considering how these earbuds stack up to their cheaper competition.

#462. Leading ice hockey nations, sponsors oppose Russia's return

Ice hockey's governing body says the impact of a court decision forcing it to reinstate Russia presents "significant practical and sporting challenges." Several leading nations say they won't play against Russian teams.

The International Ice Hockey Federation (IIHF) said in a statement on Wednesday that the impact of last week's Court of Arbitration for Sport (CAS) decision requiring it to reinstate Russia to international play had been "significantly underestimated."

It added that it "maintains" its disagreement with the decision and that it remained "in dialogue with Member National Associations, organizing committees, host authorities and other key stakeholders to determine the best way forward."

Shortly after Russia launched its full-scale invasion of Ukraine in 2022, the IIHF barred its teams from competing in international ice hockey tournaments, citing security concerns. In last Friday's ruling, the CAS said that the IIHF's concerns were "not supported by sufficient evidence."

Wednesday's statement comes after two days of talks by the IIHF Council aimed at charting a way forward following Friday's CAS decision, which appears to have caught most of the international ice hockey community off guard.

Two of the organizing committees the statement refers to are those of Canada, which is due to host the IIHF World Junior Championship starting next month, and Germany, which is to host the IIHF Men's World Championship next May. Both countries are strong supporters of Ukraine in its war against Russia, and both have expressed serious reservations about their upcoming tournaments in light of the CAS ruling.

On Tuesday, Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney ruled out allowing Russians to compete in the World Juniors in Alberta.

"While Russia and Belarus are supporting this illegal, unjustified ​war in Ukraine, as long as that's going on, there will be none of those athletes competing for their countries in Canada," he told reporters at a press conference in Antigua, where he was attending a Commonwealth meeting.

"So, ​if you're asking specifically about the World Juniors, the Russians will not be there."

Last weekend, Frank Lutz, chairman of the German Ice Hockey Federation (DEB) conceded in a television interview that following the CAS decision, the DEB would "also have to consider the option of handing back (hosting rights to) this championship."

In a statement to DW on Wednesday, a German government spokesperson reiterated Berlin's position on the matter from the weekend, saying it viewed Friday's CAS ruling "very critically."

The spokesperson also said  the government would "consult with the German Ice Hockey Federation in the near future regarding the potential consequences of this decision, including its impact on the 2027 Ice Hockey World Championship in Germany."

Meanwhile, the ice hockey association of Finland, the defending men's world champions, has been spearheading an effort to boycott upcoming IIHF tournaments should the Russians be allowed to compete.

"We are currently working to bring as many countries as possible into a united front to tell the IIHF that none of us will play against Russia under these circumstances," Finnish Ice Hockey Association boss Heikki Hietanen said in a statement released on the organization's website on Monday.

"Our stance is that we will not play against Russia as long as the war of aggression against Ukraine continues," added Hietanen, who is also a member of the IIHF Council.

Sweden, Norway, Denmark and Czechia have since made similar statements.

"The Three Crowns will not play Russia in coming world championship tournaments," Swedish Ice Hockey Federation boss Anders Larsson said.

This came shortly after Norway said it would not play Russia at the Men's World Championship and the Danish Ice Hockey Federation issued a similar statement on its website.

"Denmark will – based on the current situation and the information available to us today – not play against Russia at the World Championship in Germany in May 2027," it read. Denmark did not qualify for the World Juniors.

Czechia Ice Hockey Association President Alois Hadamczik confirmed on Tuesday that they too would refrain from playing Russian teams.

Latvia's federation said it “reaffirms its longstanding and firm position" not to play against Russia or its ally Belarus.

Alexander Helwig, chairman of the Austrian Ice Hockey Association told public broadcaster ORF that “as of now, we would certainly not play against Russia."

USA Hockey has yet to comment on the CAS ruling.

At the same time, sponsors of IIHF tournaments are also getting cold feet. Skoda, which has been the main sponsor of the Men's World Championship for more than three decades, told the Associated Press that it would "reassess" its position if Russia takes part.

"We do not support the Court of Arbitration for Sport's decision regarding the participation of Russian national teams in IIHF competitions. With Russia's aggression continuing, we believe the Russian national team's return to the world championship is incompatible with the values we have consistently upheld," the Czech carmaker said.

Skoda isn't alone either, Finnish tire manufacturer Nokian had said it would end its backing if Russia took part and Swedish furniture maker AJ Products, whose logo has been seen on the boards and officials' jerseys at World Championships since 1989, said Monday it would ask for its logo not to be shown.

On Wednesday afternoon, yet another sponsor joined the list, with German workwear company Strauss saying it would "forgo all logo exposure associated with the (men's) tournament" and was considering withdrawing its sponsporship entirely.

With the IIHF Council having failed to agree on how to proceed during its meetings on Monday and Tuesday, President Franz Reindl told Germany's SID news agency on Wednesday, that it would reconvene for further talks on Thursday.

This article was originally published on November 4, 2026. It was updated on November 5, 2026 with the German government statement to DW.

Between automated bot armies scooping digital drops, surprise order cancellations at Walmart, and inventory vanishing in seconds on Amazon, this custom black-and-gold console feels like a myth. But while digital storefronts look completely barren, stock is actively circulating behind the scenes.

Before getting into the restock details, bookmark these retailer pages to check for surprise drops:

Nintendo Store — Out of stock right now, but you can add it to your wishlist to get pinged the second a restock hits.

Amazon — Dropping tiny batches of consoles randomly throughout the day.

Walmart — Stock exists but only for third-party listings selling at way over market price, so make sure your in-stock email alerts are turned on.

Target — Sold out online, but periodic drops have taken place in-store.

Best Buy — Sold out online, but select stores ran midnight openings on Nov. 5 with walk-in stock.

GameStop — Online orders are off; you will have to walk into a store to find one.

The feeding frenzy is being supercharged by Nintendo’s staggered release calendar. While the custom Zelda 40th Anniversary hardware dropped first on Oct. 29, the flagship game driving demand — The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time for Nintendo Switch 2 — arrived on Thursday, Nov. 5.

The remake rebuilds the 1998 classic from the ground up with modernized controls, a fully orchestrated soundtrack, reworked dungeon puzzles, and expanded Hyrule environments. Since the $520 console doesn’t include the game, everyone is rushing to lock down a system before launch day.

Landing limited-edition Nintendo hardware takes a little prep work, but these battle-tested habits will give you a real edge over the bots:

Save your checkout details everywhere — Log in to Target, Best Buy, Walmart, and the Nintendo Store to save your shipping address and default card. Stopping to type a card number or billing zip code during a live drop is how you lose an order.

Hands off the refresh button in Best Buy's queue — When an online drop goes live, Best Buy puts shoppers into a randomized waiting line to filter out scrapers. Touching the reload button resets your spot and boots you straight to the back.

Skip the web wars and head to physical stores — Online checkout bots grab website stock in under 15 seconds. Showing up in person is easily your most reliable path to securing one without paying scalper markups.

Stores like Walmart, Amazon, and Best Buy often hold onto popular gaming hardware to use as headline deals during Black Friday and Cyber Monday week.

With that said, Black Friday restocks are usually the hardest ones to catch. You will be fighting website crashes, gift shoppers, and automated bots all at once. If you can grab a console whenever stock appears, take it. Think of Black Friday as a backup plan, not your main bet.

Console hunting sucks right now, period. But between the surprise micro-drops and physical store restocks, you still have real shots at retail price — just be ready to move the second that stock goes live.

#457. Children’s Place files for bankruptcy protection, seeks buyer

Children's clothing retailer The Children's Place has filed for chapter 11 bankruptcy protection, it said Sunday.

The company is looking to sell substantially all of its assets and is seeking offers from potential buyers by Dec. 9, with a goal to close a deal by the end of December.

The retailer, whose brands include The Children's Place and Gymboree, says it operates two digital storefronts and 514 North American stores, and has distribution in 13 countries through franchise and wholesale partners. The company's performance has taken a hit in recent years from tariff hikes and slower consumer spending, among other factors.

Its stores and e-commerce sites remain open for now, and the company is planning to continue its ongoing inventory sales with court approval.

"For nearly 60 years, The Children's Place has supported growing families with apparel that customers have come to know and love. We recognize the significance of this development for our employees, customers, vendors, and other partners, and we are committed to conducting an efficient and transparent sale process," said Rudy Morando, co-chief restructuring officer of The Children's Place. "In the meantime, we remain focused on serving the families who have trusted The Children's Place for generations."

This move comes days after interim Children's Place CEO Muhammad Asif Seemab and Chief Strategy Officer Muhammad Umair stepped down from their roles. Seemab and Umair will both remain on the company's board. CFO John Szczepanski took on the role of acting president and principal executive officer.

Seemab is also managing director of Saudi-based Mithaq Capital, which took a majority stake in The Children's Place in 2024 when the company was facing financial trouble.

Even with support from the investors, the company has not been able to significantly improve its financial outlook. In its bankruptcy filing, the company reported about $742 million in assets, compared to about $881 million in debts.

In the second fiscal quarter of 2026, its most recently reported quarter, the company's net sales dropped nearly 19% from the same period the year prior. At the time, Seemab said in a news release that during the quarter the company was "stabilizing our customer file" and "driving traffic to our stores and websites," and saw "lower conversion, combined with a very clearance-heavy mix."

He also pointed to tough comparisons to the prior-year period, due in part to back-to-school shopping demand that "was uniquely elevated ... ahead of anticipated tariff price increases."

The Children's Place stock has plummeted in recent years and was trading around 18 cents per share on Monday.

#452. Lucid misses Wall Street’s earnings expectations, expands loan from Saudi Arabia fund

Lucid Group missed Wall Street's third-quarter expectations as the embattled electric vehicle maker continues an "operational reset" aimed at cutting costs and narrowing losses.

The company on Monday also announced a $1.75 billion increase in a delayed draw term loan from Saudi Arabia's Public Investment Fund, the company's largest shareholder.

Lucid stock fell roughly 5% in aftermarket trading Monday.

Here's how Lucid performed in the third quarter compared with average estimates compiled by LSEG:

Despite the top- and bottom-line misses, the company's adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization of a loss of $726 million beat Wall Street's expectations of a loss of $611 million for the third quarter. Its net loss for the quarter was $830.9 million, down from a loss of $978.4 million a year earlier.

Lucid's 2025 third quarter included revenue of $336.6 million, a loss of $3.31 per share and an adjusted EBITDA loss of $717.7 million.

The quarterly results are the first since Lucid CEO Silvio Napoli, who started leading the company in June, announced a restructuring plan that included implementing several cost-cutting efforts and delaying the company's upcoming midsize vehicle from the end of this year until the second half of next.

"Our transformation is beginning to show progress. We are acting with urgency, strengthening the fundamentals and driving greater discipline, while continuing to invest in the priorities to deliver profitable long-term growth," Napoli said in a release.

The company's turnaround plan includes $1.4 billion in cash flow improvements this year as well as focusing attention on a robotaxi program with Uber and Nuro, a factory that's under construction in Saudi Arabia and the midsize vehicle.

Lucid said the capital from PIF is expected to give the company "additional financial flexibility as Lucid delivers on its turnaround program." It is expected to assist in funding "operations and key investments" into the fourth quarter of 2027, including the start of production for the midsize vehicle.

The additional funds boost Lucid's total liquidity to $3.75 billion, up from $2 billion to end the third quarter.

"We are grateful for the continued financial support from our anchor shareholder, which further energizes us to remain focused on execution and on our commitments as we build a stronger, sustainable Lucid," Napoli said.

Lucid said Monday that the company expects to communicate its midterm plan and targets early next year. The EV maker in May suspended its guidance and many targets, citing new leadership and time needed to develop a turnaround plan.

#449. Google Workspace Promo Codes: 14% Off for November 2026

Google Workspace is that ubiquitous modern business tool that almost feels invisible. We use it every day here at WIRED, whether that's for writing first drafts of articles in Docs, analyzing data in Sheets, or sharing files in Drive. And recently, I've been using the Gemini AI implementation that's built right into these tools, which is handy in all sorts of ways.

Google Workspace grants access to all kinds of productivity features that can help streamline processes and increase communication within a team or organization. It's not free, but here's the good news: If you're looking at a Workspace account for yourself (or for your team), Google is offering discounts until the end of 2026 using one of the Google Workspace coupon codes below. You can save up to 14 percent on plans for the first three months, whether you're signing up as an individual or as a business. Either way, the Google Workspace promo code down below is what you want to use.

There are some notable differences between each Workspace plan – I’ll dig into them in the next section – but all three plans come with access to the full slate of Workspace apps. You get Gmail, Drive, Calendar, and Meet, of course, but even the cheapest Starter plan includes basic access to Google’s other apps, such as NotebookLM and Gemini inside the Workspace suite. If you want to save, make sure to click one of the promo codes at the top of this page to secure your discount.

Google offers four different Workspace plans, though only three are relevant for most people. There’s Starter, Standard, and Plus, as well as Enterprise, which is only available if you contact Google’s sales team. There isn’t a minimum number of users on any of the plans, but the bottom three options (Starter, Standard, and Plus) support up to 300 users. If you need more than that, you’ll need an Enterprise plan.

Although you have access to all the apps regardless of the plan you choose, there are some important differences, particularly between the Starter and Standard plans. Both include secure storage through Google Drive, but Starter comes with 30GB per user while Standard comes with a massive 2TB per user. Plus bumps that up to 5TB per user. Across all plans, storage is in a shared pool, so each additional user adds to the total pool of storage your organization can use.

Gemini is also a big difference between Starter and Standard. Although both have access to the Gemini app, Starter only has Gemini in Gmail and Vids. Standard (and Plus) includes Gemini across the full suite, including in Docs, Sheets, Slides, Drive, Meet, and Chat.

Starter is a great option for anyone just getting started, regardless of whether you’re a freelancer or a small business with a handful of employees. In addition to 30GB per user and limited access to Gemini, Starter comes with a few other limitations. It doesn’t support e-signatures in Docs, nor advanced features of Meet, such as noise cancellation. The bread and butter of Workspace is all here, though. You get a custom domain in Gmail, up to 100 participants in Meet, and the full Google suite of productivity apps.

Standard, as the name suggests, is Workspace proper, and it’s built for teams that need room to grow. That shows up most clearly in storage space, jumping from 30GB to 2TB per user. Standard basically cuts all the red tape on Starter. There aren’t any limitations on Gemini (it’s available across the full suite), Meet features like noise cancellation are available, and you can host up to 150 participants in a Meet call. Standard even comes with expanded access to NotebookLM, Google’s AI-driven note and research tool, with different chat modes and up to 20 generative audio overviews per day.

At the top of the range is Plus, which is focused on larger businesses. You don’t get any extra apps with this plan, but you do get 5TB of storage per user, along with up to 500 participants in Meet calls. The real reason to upgrade to Plus is security and admin controls, however. It includes Vault for data retention, along with LDAP access—basically a server for centralized account management, if you aren’t familiar.

Most people should use the Standard plan, not only for the large amount of storage space over Starter, but also due to the extra Gemini functionality. If you need to upgrade to Plus down the line, you can. And, more than likely, you’ll have a member of your IT team begging you to do so.

Google has updated Workspace a lot over the past several months, building in new functionalities via Gemini throughout the suite. But earlier this year, Google announced that it’s bringing all of those functionalities together through Workspace Flows. It’s a no-code automation tool that lets you build automatic workflows; i.e., update a Sheet when there’s a new email in Gmail from a particular address. The interesting thing about Flows, however, is that Gemini is built into it.

You can set up basic automations, which is great given how interconnected Google’s Workspace apps already are, but Flows become powerful when you throw Gems into the mix. Gems are AI agents built with Gemini, and you can create them with specific parameters.

Let me give you an example. Say you need a marketing plan, and an employee sends you a draft. You can have a workflow set up with a Gem that’s been trained on the budget, requirements, and tone of the campaign to quickly know if it can be approved or not. Or, you can create a Gem to scan your email, trained on your organization chart and role responsibilities, to notify you whenever you receive an email that has action items for you.

Flows give you a way to add a bit of context and reasoning to the often rigid constraints of simple automation systems, and they can save you a lot of time in the process. You may be able to gather customer feedback and put it into Sheets easily, for example, but Gemini can automatically sort, summarize, and categorize that feedback so you know what you should tackle first. Flows are centralized in Workspace, as well; you don’t need to install add-ons or configure anything outside of setting up your Workspace account.

Google Workspace Flows is a new tool integrated with Google Workspace. It is powered by Google’s Gemini AI and helps you get tasks done with Gmail, Docs, Sheets, Calendar and Chat. It can fix up emails, make plans, correct sheets, or write a follow-up message. No apps or links are needed.

#447. Skydance outlines its $6 billion in planned cost savings. Here's the breakdown

Skydance is pulling back the curtain on how it plans to achieve at least $6 billion in cost savings in the next three years.

"By combining two global streaming services, two film and TV studio groups, two television portfolios, and overlapping corporate functions, we can reduce duplicative spend across an expense base of more than $50 billion," the company wrote in a letter to shareholders Monday as part of Paramount Skydance's third-quarter earnings release.

Skydance, the company formed by the combination of Paramount Skydance and Warner Bros. Discovery, said it projects at least $2.2 billion in cost reductions from eliminating "redundant back-office and corporate functions," including eliminating management layers and ramping the use of artificial intelligence. Those savings also account for "rationalizing" its office footprint.

Skydance expects at least $1 billion in savings from streamlining marketing and promotional spending. And around $1.4 billion in savings will come from unifying its streaming businesses and broadcast architecture and integrating back-end technology.

A final $1.4 billion in savings will be tied to "optimizing third-party spend," eliminating overlapping expenses, and consolidating purchasing decisions to one centralized group, Skydance said.

"We are confident in achieving these savings, drawing on our playbook from Paramount," the company wrote. "That program is on track to achieve over $2.7 billion in run-rate efficiencies by the end of 2026."

Some of that cost-cutting has already begun.

Last month, Skydance began offering voluntary buyouts to employees ahead of official layoffs. At the same time, the company adopted a five-day-per-week in-office policy. Additionally, last week saw the departure of eight executives — four from Warner Bros. and four from Paramount.

The company has previously said it plans to eventually merge the Paramount+ and HBO Max streaming services into a single platform.

On Monday, Skydance said it would take the first step toward that goal early next year, offering a bundle of the streaming services "at an even greater value."

Co-CEO Ynon Kreiz told investors during the company's earnings call that Skydance is also looking for ways to optimize its content budget. The company spends more than $30 billion annually on shows and films.

"As one company, we can make smarter choices about how we distribute and monetize both new content and library content," Kreiz said.

Skydance noted that it's expecting a $200 million headwind in the fourth quarter from theatrical underperformances at Warner Bros. studio related to already launched titles and upcoming releases.

The company will also utilize AI to speed up production and decrease costs to make films, co-CEO and chairman David Ellison said on the call.

"We think these tools will help unlock more original storytelling by being able to make the budget more digestible to be able to take that amount of risk," Ellison said.

The updates came alongside third-quarter results for the legacy Paramount Skydance company that included revenue of $7.03 billion, which was roughly in line with StreetAccount estimates of $7.04 billion, and adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization of $1 billion, topping StreetAccount estimates of $913.5 million.

However, adjusted earnings per share of 45 cents were not immediately comparable to StreetAccount EPS estimates.

The results don't include the impact of the Warner Bros. Discovery acquisition, which closed after the end of the third quarter.

Skydance detailed business units of the combined entity, including studios, direct-to-consumer and TV, and said it will provide full-year guidance during its fourth-quarter earnings report early next year.

"Going forward, we will provide guidance on an annual basis, as we prioritize long-term value creation, which we believe will be more consistent with our strategic approach," the company told shareholders. "We will continue to provide quarterly updates, including certain forward-looking commentary where applicable, which will give investors visibility into our near-term progress of the business."

Meshnet delivers high speed, low latency, and advanced security for everything from file sharing and work collaboration to multiplayer gaming.

Worried someone might use your name to take out loans and open accounts? NordVPN has your back. With NordProtect, you’ll get instant alerts so you can act fast and protect your identity from everything the wild, wild west of the internet throws your way. You’ll also get credit monitoring with fast security warnings about suspicious credit activity under your name, so you can stop fraud before it becomes dangerous. Plus, there’s 24/7 dark web monitoring with instant alerts about leaks of your email address, SSN, and phone number.

#442. The haunting Masters meltdown that changed Rory McIlroy's career

Editor’s Note:  This story was originally published in April 2023.

Slumped on his club, head buried in his arm, Rory McIlroy looked on the verge of tears.

The then-21-year-old had just watched his ball sink into the waters of Rae’s Creek at Augusta National and with it, his dream of winning The Masters, a dream that had looked so tantalizingly close mere hours earlier.

As a four-time major winner and one of the most decorated names in the sport’s history, few players would turn down the chance to swap places with McIlroy heading into Augusta this week.

Yet on Sunday afternoon of April 10, 2011, not a golfer in the world would have wished to be in the Northern Irishman’s shoes.

A fresh-faced, mop-headed McIlroy had touched down in Georgia for the first major of the season with a reputation as the leading light of the next generation of stars.

An excellent 2010 had marked his best season since turning pro three years earlier, highlighted by a first PGA Tour win at the Quail Hollow Championship and a crucial contribution to Team Europe’s triumph at the Ryder Cup.

Yet despite a pair of impressive top-three finishes at the Open and PGA Championship respectively, a disappointing missed cut at The Masters – his first at a major – served as ominous foreshadowing.

McIlroy shot 74 and 77 to fall four strokes short of the cut line at seven-over par, a performance that concerned him enough to take a brief sabbatical from competition.

But one year on in 2011, any lingering Masters demons looked to have been exorcised as McIlroy flew round the Augusta fairways.

Having opened with a bogey-free seven-under 65 – the first time he had ever shot in the 60s at the major – McIlroy pulled ahead from Spanish first round co-leader Alvaro Quirós with a second round 69.

It sent him into the weekend holding a two-shot cushion over Australia’s Jason Day, with Tiger Woods a further stroke behind and back in the hunt for a 15th major after a surging second round 66.

And yet the 21-year-old leader looked perfectly at ease with having a target on his back. Even after a tentative start to the third round, McIlroy rallied with three birdies across the closing six holes to stretch his lead to four strokes heading into Sunday.

The youngster was out on his own ahead of a bunched chasing pack comprising Day, Ángel Cabrera, K.J. Choi and Charl Schwartzel. After 54 holes, McIlroy had shot just three bogeys.

“It’s a great position to be in … I’m finally feeling comfortable on this golf course,” McIlroy told reporters.

“I’m not getting ahead of myself, I know how leads can dwindle away very quickly. I have to go out there, not take anything for granted and go out and play as hard as I’ve played the last three days. If I can do that, hopefully things will go my way.

“We’ll see what happens tomorrow because four shots on this golf course isn’t that much.”

The truth can hurt, and McIlroy was about to prove his assessment of Augusta to be true in the most excruciating way imaginable.

His fourth bogey of the week arrived immediately. Having admitted to expecting some nerves at the first tee, McIlroy sparked a booming opening drive down the fairway, only to miss his putt from five feet.

Three consecutive pars steadied the ship, but Schwartzel had the wind in his sails. A blistering birdie, par, eagle start had seen him draw level at the summit after his third hole.

A subsequent bogey from the South African slowed his charge, as McIlroy clung onto a one-shot lead at the turn from Schwartzel, Cabrera, Choi, and a rampaging Woods, who shot five birdies and an eagle across the front nine to send Augusta into a frenzy.

Despite his dwindling advantage and the raucous Tiger-mania din ahead of him, McIlroy had responded well to another bogey at the 5th hole, draining a brilliant 20-foot putt at the 7th to restore his lead.

The fist pump that followed marked the high-water point of McIlroy’s round, as a sliding start accelerated into full-blown free-fall at the par-four 10th hole.

His tee shot went careening into a tree, ricocheting to settle between the white cabins that separate the main course from the adjacent par-three course. It offered viewers a glimpse at a part of Augusta rarely seen on broadcast, followed by pictures of McIlroy anxiously peering out from behind a tree to track his follow-up shot.

Though his initial escape was successful, yet another collision with a tree and a two-putt on the green saw a stunned McIlroy eventually tap in for a triple bogey. Having led the field one hole and seven shots earlier, he arrived at the 11th tee in seventh.

By the time his tee drive at the 13th plopped into the creek, all thoughts of who might be the recipient of the green jacket had long-since switched away from the anguished youngster. It had taken him seven putts to navigate the previous two greens, as a bogey and a double bogey dropped him to five-under – the score he had held after just 11 holes of the tournament.

Mercifully, the last five holes passed without major incident. A missed putt for birdie from five feet at the final hole summed up McIlroy’s day, though he was given a rousing reception as he left the green.

Mere minutes earlier, the same crowd had erupted as Schwartzel sunk his fourth consecutive birdie to seal his first major title. After starting the day four shots adrift of McIlroy, the South African finished 10 shots ahead of him, and two ahead of second-placed Australian duo Jason Day and Adam Scott.

McIlroy’s eight-over 80 marked the highest score of the round. Having headlined the leaderboard for most of the week, he finished tied-15th.

Tears would flow during a phone call with his parents the following morning, but at his press conference, McIlroy was upbeat.

“I’m very disappointed at the minute, and I’m sure I will be for the next few days, but I’ll get over it,” he said.

“I was leading this golf tournament with nine holes to go, and I just unraveled … It’s a Sunday at a major, what it can do.

“This is my first experience at it, and hopefully the next time I’m in this position I’ll be able to handle it a little better. I didn’t handle it particularly well today obviously, but it was a character-building day … I’ll come out stronger for it.”

Once again, McIlroy would be proven right.

Just eight weeks later in June, McIlroy rampaged to an eight-shot victory at the US Open. Records tumbled in his wake at Congressional, as he shot a tournament record 16-under 268 to become the youngest major winner since Tiger Woods at The Masters in 1997.

The historic victory kickstarted a golden era for McIlroy. After coasting to another eight-shot win at the PGA Championship in 2012, McIlroy became only the third golfer since 1934 to win three majors by the age of 25 with triumph at the 2014 Open Championship.

Before the year was out, he would add his fourth major title with another PGA Championship win.

And much of it was owed to that fateful afternoon at Augusta. In an interview with the BBC in 2015, McIlroy dubbed it “the most important day” of his career.

“If I had not had the whole unravelling, if I had just made a couple of bogeys coming down the stretch and lost by one, I would not have learned as much.

“Luckily, it did not take me long to get into a position like that again when I was leading a major and I was able to get over the line quite comfortably. It was a huge learning curve for me and I needed it, and thankfully I have been able to move on to bigger and better things.

“Looking back on what happened in 2011, it doesn’t seem as bad when you have four majors on your mantelpiece.”

McIlroy’s contentment came with a caveat: it would be “unthinkable” if he did not win The Masters in his career.

Yet as he prepares for his 15th appearance at Augusta National this week, a green jacket remains an elusive missing item from his wardrobe.

Despite seven top-10 finishes in his past 10 Masters outings, the trophy remains the only thing separating McIlroy from joining the ranks of golf immortals to have completed golf’s career grand slam of all four majors in the modern era: Gene Sarazen, Ben Hogan, Gary Player, Jack Nicklaus, and Tiger Woods.

A runner-up finish to Scottie Scheffler last year marked McIlroy’s best finish at Augusta, yet arguably 2011 remains the closest he has ever been to victory. A slow start in 2022 meant McIlroy had begun Sunday’s deciding round 10 shots adrift of the American, who teed off for his final hole with a five-shot lead despite McIlroy’s brilliant 64 finish.

At 33 years old, time is still on his side. Though 2022 extended his major drought to eight years, it featured arguably his best golf since that golden season in 2014.

And as McIlroy knows better than most, things can change quickly at Augusta National.

#436. Teenage swimmer breaks two world records in four days

Sixteen-year-old Summer McIntosh broke the world record for the 400m individual medley at the Canadian swimming trials on Saturday – just days after breaking the world record for the 400m freestyle.

McIntosh finished the 400m individual medley in a time of 4:25.87, breaking the mark set at the 2016 Rio Olympics by Hungarian swimmer Katinka Hosszú with a time of 4:26.36.

The result continues the teenager’s brilliant week after she broke the 400m freestyle world record last Tuesday – breaking defending Olympic champion Ariarne Titmus’ previous record of 3:56.40, which was set last May, in a time of 3:56.08.

Speaking after her latest triumph, McIntosh shared her emotions.

“That’s the first time I really vividly heard the crowd during my race,” McIntosh said, per Reuters.

“It’s amazing to have all my family and friends in the stands, cheering me on. It really helped me in the last 100 meters.”

The Canadian teen made her Olympic debut aged 14 at Tokyo 2020, where she finished fourth in the 200-meter freestyle.

She bagged gold in the 200m butterfly and 400m medley at the world championships in 2022, as well as two golds at the Commonwealth Games in the 200m and 400m medleys.

She now trains in Florida under coach Brent Arckey with the Sarasota Sharks team.

#432. How GM is trying to talk used car shoppers into buying new vehicles

Affordability challenges are pushing some buyers out of the new vehicle market. Many are choosing used cars instead.

In turn, automakers are looking for ways to compete with many of the online retailers grabbing market share in the used car market and trying to bring some of those customers back into a new vehicle.

New vehicle sales tend to be more profitable for automakers, but reselling trade-ins as used vehicles is highly profitable for dealers. Used car prices are relatively strong, but newer online retailers such as Carvana and CarMax have made the market more competitive for dealers and other incumbent used car sellers.

"The internet and technology and apps on our phone have changed every aspect of retail, and probably one of the slowest areas of retail to have adopted to online and digital shopping has been the auto industry," said Ivan Feinseth, partner, chief investment officer and director of research at Tigress Financial Partners.

General Motors is a key example of automakers' attempts to address this shifting market. The automaker has revamped its certified used vehicle program, which now operates under the CarBravo name. It's essentially an online portal through which Chevrolet, Buick and GMC dealers can list their certified used vehicles and make them available to customers all over the U.S.

About 1,615 of GM's 3,700 dealers are enrolled in the program, according to the company. The only GM brand not involved is Cadillac, which maintains its own program.

"Certified" used or "preowned" vehicles are used cars that have undergone a thorough inspection and reconditioning. They typically come with guarantees and warranties from the manufacturer.

Many automakers have certified used programs for their own cars. But GM, as part of its revamp, is using CarBravo to certify vehicles made by other automakers. A lot of those cars arrive at dealerships as trade-ins, and often dealers will sell them to wholesale auctions, said John Fitzpatrick, senior manager of the CarBravo program.

"Wholesaling doesn't bring nearly the revenue that selling it to a new customer does," he said.

GM has said it is planning to net about 250,000 sales in its CarBravo program in 2026. Fitzpatrick said he doesn't expect that even half of those will end up being new car sales.

But through CarBravo, the customer gets access to GM's dealership network for service, something Fitzpatrick says is a key differentiator between its program and growing online sellers. CarBravo vehicles come with various warranties, depending on the age and mileage on the car.

GM said the program is driving more traffic into local dealerships, where purchases are typically finalized. That creates an opportunity to convert a used car shopper into a new car buyer. So far, the automaker said about 38% of the shoppers initially looking for used cars end up buying new.

A customer who wants to buy a full-size truck can look on the website and compare used Chevrolet Silverados alongside used Ford F-150s and Ram 1500s, for example. The buyer might settle on a used truck from any of those brands, but a conversation with a dealer can push them in a different direction.

"The customer picks up the phone, but in the back of their mind, they maybe always have been a traditional, more new car customer," Fitzpatrick said.

"You've shown the customer, you're going to pay $500 [per month] for this used F-150, but you may pay $510 or $515 for this new Chevy," he said. "And now all of a sudden the customer says, yeah, maybe I can do that because I'd rather have that new vehicle than the used truck."

While the average transaction price of a new vehicle is about $50,000, the average 3-year-old used car costs about $30,000 as of September, according to Cox Automotive.

However, industry analysts say customers base their purchasing decisions on the cost of the monthly payment more than on the sticker price of the car.

New cars often come with longer-term loan options than used ones, Feinseth said, which can result in lower monthly payments.

"On a 1- or 2-year-old car, you may be only stuck with a 3- or 4-year loan," he said. "On a new car, you can get a 5-year, 6-year loan."

The share of new car loans lasting 84 months hit a new record in the third quarter, at more than 1 in 4 loans, according to Edmunds.

Meanwhile, used car prices in some affordable segments have risen, according to Cox Automotive, and about three quarters of consumers express some interest in certified cars.

But the key is building the relationship with the customer, he said. About 50% of the buyers GM has gained through the program appear to be new to the brand, meaning there is no record of them in any of the automaker's customer databases. About 70% had never visited the dealership they ended up purchasing from.

"The goal is really to get that customer engaged with the store, give them the option of building a relationship with the dealer," he said. "The dealer can really help the customer figure out what works for their budget."

It's a boon for dealers too: Customers buying warranty-backed used cars often return to the dealership for service visits — which often account for about half of a dealership's profits. GM can also sell subscriptions to connected vehicle services, such as its OnStar, which provides safety, security and navigation services.

"That is a big part of General Motors' business going forward, is to sell the software and subscription services like OnStar and eventually to the cars that are enabled with Super Cruise," GM's advanced driver-assistance system, Feinseth said.

GM isn't the only automaker trying to use its certified preowned vehicle program to lure buyers.

Detroit rival Ford Motor also certifies vehicles made by other manufacturers through its Ford Blue Advantage program. It is on track to do 450,000 total sales in 2026, more than GM's 250,000 target. It didn't specify how many of those buyers end up purchasing new vehicles.

Toyota told CNBC in an email that about 40% of its used car shoppers end up buying new instead.

Honda didn't give specifics about its program. But Lance Woelfer, American Honda's vice president of automobile sales, said conversions happen "probably every day at dealerships throughout the country."

"But it also is more about that first interaction with the consumer," Woelfer said. "And there are people in any market that prefer to buy a used vehicle, but appreciate the confidence that a [certified preowned designation] can give them."

Woelfer said those customers come back to get their vehicles serviced at a rate about 18% higher than someone who buys a non-certified used car. They also return to purchase a new car at a rate about 7% to 8% higher in the future, he said.

"It's about that first relationship that allows us to then have that customer loyalty for life," he added. "And that's where our focus is. We've been doing it a long time, and we've seen a lot of benefits from the certified business."
