#430. Angel Reese defends gesture directed towards Caitlin Clark after LSU national title win; calls out double standard after being 'unapologetically' her

LSU star Angel Reese defended the gesture she aimed at Iowa Hawkeyes’ Caitlin Clark near the end of the Tigers’ first NCAA women’s basketball national championship victory on Sunday, saying “I don’t take disrespect lightly.”

Reese could be seen approaching Clark before moving her open hand in front of her face – popularized by WWE star John Cena to mean “you can’t see me” – before pointing to her ring finger in a gesture some interpreted as a reference to the place her newly-acquired championship ring might sit.

Caitlin Clark breaks records galore during historic March Madness run

Clark made a similar gesture to another player earlier in the tournament.

The gesture has sparked much debate, especially on social media. Some have criticized Reese, while others have defended her actions, highlighting how there was no public outrage in response to Clark’s gesture earlier in the tournament.

Sports journalist Jose de Jesus Ortiz called Reese’s actions “classless,” while former ESPN host Keith Olbermann called Reese an “idiot” for the gesture.

In the press conference after the victory, Reese referenced the difference in reaction she received as a result of her gesture as compared to the one Clark received.

“All year, I was critiqued for who I was. I don’t fit the narrative,” Reese said. “I don’t fit the box that y’all want me to be in. I’m too hood. I’m too ghetto. Y’all told me that all year. But when other people do it, and y’all don’t say nothing.

“So this is for the girls that look like me. For those that want to speak up for what they believe in. It’s unapologetically you. And that’s what I did it for tonight. It was bigger than me tonight. And Twitter is going to go into a rage every time.

“And I’m happy. I feel like I’ve helped grow women’s basketball this year. (…) I’m looking forward to celebrating and then next season.”

LSU beats Iowa to win its first NCAA women's basketball championship

Reese had 15 points and 10 rebounds in the 102-85 victory and won the women’s March Madness’ Most Outstanding Player award.

In the post-game broadcast, Reese referenced the similar gesture Clark made to a Louisville opponent in the Elite Eight.

In the same game, Clark said to an opponent: “You’re down by 15 points. Shut up,” according to the Bleacher Report.

“Caitlin Clark is a hell of a player but I don’t take disrespect lightly,” Reese said. “She disrespected [LSU’s] Alexis [Morris] (…) and I wanted to pick her pocket. But I had a moment at the end of her game. I was in my bag, I was in my moment.”

After the Championship game, Clark herself said she didn’t notice anything at the time.

“I was just trying to get to the handshake line and shake hands and be grateful that my team was in that position,” Clark said in the post-game press conference. “All the credit in the world to LSU. They were tremendous. They deserve it. They had a tremendous season.

“(LSU head coach) Kim Mulkey coached them so, so well. She’s one of the best basketball coaches of all time, and it shows. She only said really kind things to me in the handshake line, so I’m very grateful of that too.

“But honestly I have no idea. I was just trying to spend the last few moments on the court with especially the five people that I’ve started 93 games with and relishing every second of that.”

LSU head coach Mulkey said she had “no clue” about what transpired.

Among those defending Reese on social media were ESPN’s Holly Rowe and former NBA star Etan Thomas.

“People hating on Angel Reese or Caitlin Clark. Stop. Unapologetically confident young women should be celebrated NOT hated. Get used to it,” Rowe wrote on Twitter.

Former Washington Wizards, Oklahoma City Thunder and Atlanta Hawks player Thomas wrote: “Hold on now!!!! It was cute when Caitlin Clark did it. Y’all didn’t have any issues with it at all. So don’t be all outraged and talking about class and sportsmanship when Angel Reese does the same thing. We’re not doing double standards here.”

Reese said the negative reaction on social media throughout the season has helped fuel her excellent season, having finished averaging 23.0 points and 15.4 rebounds in her first season with LSU after transferring from Maryland.

“Twitter can say what they want to say,” she said. “I love reading those comments. I have all the screenshots of what everybody has said about me all season. What are you going to say now?”

#425. Rivian CEO says new R2 SUV, autonomy make 2027 an ‘inflection year’ as EV maker marks five years since IPO

It's been five years since Rivian Automotive celebrated its initial public offering with CEO and founder RJ Scaringe ringing the Nasdaq opening bell from the company's factory in Normal, Illinois, while the company's sleek vehicles sat outside the stock exchange in Times Square.

It was a euphoric time for all-electric vehicle companies that has since diminished. And much like the EV hype, Rivian's stock price is down more than 80% since the Amazon-backed company debuted on the Nasdaq at $78 per share on Nov. 10, 2021. That includes a nearly 30% decline this year.

A host of internal and external factors have contributed to Rivian's stock decline, but the company continues to be the most promising EV maker to emerge during the early 2020s, as most of its peer group to go public during that time no longer exists.

Rivian founder and CEO RJ Scaringe acknowledges that not everything has gone to plan amid supply chain shortages, geopolitical tensions and slower-than-expected consumer EV adoption, but he said he believes the next year will prove to be a catalyst for the company and investors.

"2027 is really an inflection year for us," Scaringe told CNBC during an exclusive interview. "I'm excited to start to really show what we believe so fully, the potential of the business."

Scaringe's bullishness comes as Rivian ramps up production of its midsize R2 SUV — meant to expand the brand to the mainstream market — and as the company continues to invest in its next generation of vehicle software and autonomy.

Scaringe said the R2, with an entry-level model at about $45,000, remains the core business to be able to showcase its newest technologies. Rivian delivered a record 19,248 vehicles in the third quarter of 2026, up 46% year over year, and said R2 was already its best-selling vehicle by volume, outselling both its R1 flagship products and its commercial van.

"That's going to define the business. The path to profitability, the path to very significant growth in revenue is all built around R2, and then some of the variants that come following R2," Scaringe said.

For the most recent period, Rivian said the automotive cost of goods sold per vehicle improved by more than $12,000 quarter over quarter, helped by a higher R2 mix and better fixed-cost leverage.

Rivian also is in the midst of building a new multibillion-dollar Georgia factory that Scaringe has said is necessary to get the company to the volumes it needs to achieve net profitability.

Its sole factory in Normal, Illinois, is capable of producing 215,000 vehicles annually. That includes up to 155,000 R2s alongside R1 models and electric vans. Rivian said the Georgia plant will be capable of producing up to 300,000 units annually once the initial phase construction is completed in late 2028.

Of course, all of that means additional capital spending and pressure on the company to improve profits.

"We've made the decision to simultaneously work towards profitability … and deeply invest in our technology, and in particular in autonomy," Scaringe said.

Rivian's operating performance is improving, but several Wall Street analysts expect the company's investment requirements to rise significantly in 2027.

Rivian generated $176 million of consolidated gross profit in the third quarter, but its automotive business posted an $80 million gross loss, while its software and services unit generated $256 million of gross profit at a 42% margin.

Wall Street analysts broadly agree Rivian's R2 launch is showing encouraging early demand and lowering manufacturing costs, but they're concerned about whether that can offset rising autonomy spending, the Georgia factory buildout and continued cash burn.

"Funding has long been a key debate for Rivian investors," Deutsche Bank analyst Edison Yu said in an Oct. 30 investor note after the company reported quarterly results that exceeded Wall Street's expectations. "The notion of a multi-year (even if credible) path to cash flow breakeven will be less attractive to investors."

Rivian said during its late October earnings call that its total available future liquidity is expected to be about $13 billion when factoring in available credit and money from partnerships with Volkswagen and Uber as well as a $4.5 billion U.S. Department of Energy loan for the Georgia plant.

Rivian said its cash, cash equivalents and short-term investments balance was $5.5 billion to end the third quarter.

The company may still need to raise more capital. Scaringe said Rivian plans to be "opportunistic" about any additional capital raises, which have previously included equity deals with Volkswagen and Uber.

"As we think about the environment looking out, we want to continue to position ourselves to be in a place to invest in the technology. And so that means we'll be opportunistic as it pertains to capital. But I want to be clear: We're like very focused on getting the profitability," Scaringe said.

The company has significantly upgraded the software capabilities and hardware on its vehicles to prepare them for autonomy technologies, or physical artificial intelligence, as Scaringe calls it.

The next step in that is releasing "point-to-point" driving capabilities, in which the vehicles can drive on their own from destination to destination with the driver acting as a monitor in case of any unexpected problems.

U.S. EV leader Tesla is the only automaker to currently offer those features on its vehicles. Scaringe said Rivian plans to not only catch up to Tesla's capabilities but exceed them as it uses multiple sensor types to allow the vehicle to "see" better, especially in inclement weather.

"That's not like the finish line. ... Think of it as like the starting point where you suddenly have a more apples to apples comparison between Rivian and Tesla," Scaringe said.

The release of point-to-point over the coming weeks and months, first to employees and then to customers, is expected to be followed by autonomous vehicles with Uber by 2028.

Scaringe said he believes such technologies will become a differentiator for the company in the coming years.

"I think it will go from a purchase consideration for maybe 10% of the buying public that's in the top five things considered in making a purchase decision to the No. 1 or No. 2 purchase consideration for the vast majority of buyers," Scaringe said.

Rivian plans to offer more details about its AI and autonomous plans during a second annual investor technology event on Dec. 9.

"We view this as a likely positive catalyst as the event could set a path for AV features driving incremental vehicle demand & higher [software] revenue in 2027, building on 2026 growth," TD Cowen analyst Itay Michaeli said in an investor note last week.

Rivian's stock is currently rated overweight with a $17.70 target price, according to analyst estimates compiled by FactSet. That would be a roughly 26% increase from its Monday closing price but still a far cry from its IPO.

#420. LinkedIn Is Testing a Feature That Lets You See Your Profile Stalkers

Big news for LinkedIn stalkers: If you keep visiting the page of a potential hire—or ex-romantic partner—you might not be lurking as stealthily as you once were.

LinkedIn is testing a "frequent viewer” feature that allows certain users with LinkedIn Premium to know if someone has looked at their profile multiple times. Krista Mollion, the founder of creative agency Unignorable Brands, first noticed the added detail last month.

“I called it the ‘stalker alert,’” Mollion tells WIRED.

Every few days, Mollion, who has nearly 79,000 followers on LinkedIn, gets a notification to “check out who has viewed your profile.” LinkedIn has long allowed users to see who has looked at their profile. If you pay for LinkedIn Premium, like Mollion, you can access even more details on who has visited your page, including a full list of every viewer over the previous year. But now when Mollion visits the Who Has Viewed Your Profile section, she sees certain people tagged as Frequent Viewers.

LinkedIn confirmed the test but declined to share further information about the feature.

For Mollion, the new insight has been a boon. She runs the names of frequent viewers through Claude, asking the AI to identify any she should be paying attention to. Claude groups the viewers into three groups: friends, people who probably want to sell her something, and business development or sales leads.

Mollion reaches out to those who aren’t trying to pitch her and, she says, nine times out of 10 it leads to a good conversation. One frequent viewer was an organizer looking for speakers for an online summit; another was a host seeing if Mollion was a good fit for their podcast.

“This stalker button—the Frequent Viewer—is a way to capture those invisible lurkers who are considering you for an opportunity,” Mollion says. For the average LinkedIn user, the best-case scenario might be the chance to reach out to a recruiter looking to hire for a new role.

It also lets you know if an unwanted individual—like a former boss or an ex—is keeping tabs on you. Since the frequent viewer feature is triggered by someone looking at your profile, not just seeing your posts or comments in their feed or as the result of a search, it’s a good signal that this person is specifically seeking you out. While LinkedIn tends to be “safe-for-work” compared to other social media platforms, Mollion says that there are “definitely weirdos.” She herself has blocked one frequent viewer.

It's been a few days now since Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang admitted to having an affair with an alleged Chinese spy, and Daily Show host Desi Lydic is here to unpack the entire debacle.

"All right, look, I don't want to be sexist, but this is why I always say men shouldn't be CEOs. I'm sorry but they get all hormonal and horny when it's that time of the month — usually the first through the 30th," says Lydic in the monologue above. The host then recaps the many photos of the pair together at social functions, adding, "You guys were really flaunting it. Even the Coldplay couple ducked from the camera."

Lastly, Lydic shows a clip of Donald Trump defending Huang, with the president saying the CEO is "definitely suffering."

"He had an affair, not a terminal illness," says Lydic. "Why is this the one thing you can muster up empathy for?"

#416. The Best iPad to Buy (and What to Avoid) in 2026: Air, Pro, Mini

It’s a weird time for the iPad. Between the introduction of the MacBook Neo and the iPhone Duo this year, the iPad feels like it’s heading for an identity crisis. It doesn’t help that it’s been a relatively light year for iPad updates overall. It can still be a good time to buy an iPad, though, as the current options are still quite good. We’ve tested every iPad model to help you choose the right one for your needs and budget.

Be sure to check out all our buying guides, including the Best iPad Accessories, Best iPhones, Best iPhone 17 Cases, Best Tablets, Best Android Tablets, and Best MacBooks.

Updated November 2026: I’ve updated this article with testing for the new OLED iPad Mini and the rollout of iPadOS 27.

There are four types of iPads on the market. Knowing which model you own matters if you’re considering selling the iPad or looking for compatible accessories. Further complicating iPad identification is that—unlike with the iPhone—Apple doesn’t name its iPads sequentially.

Nonetheless, finding your iPad model is simple. Here’s how to do it, in two steps:

For accessories, retailers usually indicate which iPad model(s) a given case, folio, or keyboard cover is designed to work with. Sometimes, product descriptions will mention compatibility with a specific generation or processor; here’s the information you need to help decode that information and match it with your iPad:

Yes, though it depends on the specific iPad model you have in mind. Consider the price, how recently Apple updated the device, and any forthcoming refreshes.

The company introduced a significant (and highly uncommon for the brand) price increase on June 25 that affected every model in both the MacBook and iPad lines. Apple blamed the hikes on memory and storage shortages driven by extremely high demand for AI data centers.

Every iPad model now costs between $100 and $200 more than before. Here’s the price impact on every device in the current lineup:

iPad (base model) was last updated in 2025. This is the 11th generation and has the A16 chip. Rumors indicate a refresh will arrive in the first half of 2027, and it may sport an A19 chip with 8 GB of RAM (up from 6 GB) and support Apple Intelligence.

iPad Air received an update in March 2026 with the M4 chip. Its next refresh will reportedly come sometime in the first half of 2027, likely with an OLED screen to match the recently launched iPad Mini.

iPad Pro got an update in October 2025 to the M5 chip. It may get an update to the M6 chip this fall, but some reports have indicated the M6 may be delayed until 2027, which would also delay the iPad Pro. Fresh reports say this new iPad Pro will get a vapor chamber cooling system similar to the new iPhones.

iPad Mini was just updated in November 2026, now sporting the A20 Pro chip and an OLED screen.

Consider the iPad’s Compatible Operating System

The current version of the iPad operating system is iPadOS 27, announced at Apple’s Worldwide Developers Conference (WWDC) in June 2026. The public beta for iPadOS 27 dropped in July and officially rolled out to compatible devices on September 14.

Much of the update focuses on Siri. iPadOS 27 introduces a dedicated Siri AI app that uses Google Gemini’s world knowledge and context. The update also improves integration with apps including Camera, Mail, Photos, Messages, Calendar, and more. The most interesting AI feature is in Shortcuts, which lets you create an automated task using natural language that can trigger actions across multiple apps.

iPadOS 27 also refines the highly controversial Liquid Glass design from last year’s iPadOS 26, adding a slider in Settings that allows you to change the opacity. Other notable changes include faster, more efficient performance; user-made Custom Extensions you can create through natural language; custom equalizer (EQ) settings with AirPods; dramatically extended parental controls; and much more. These iPads are compatible with iPadOS 27:

The iPad Mini 1 through 6 and iPad 1 through 10 have either low-resolution screens or are simply too old to support the latest software. Forget buying a 2014 iPad Air 1 or a 2015 Air 2, as those are simply too long in the tooth. As a general rule of thumb, avoid buying a device that came out before 2020.

There’s a chance an older iPad could perform decently, but unless someone is giving you one for free, you’re better off hunting for a newer model at a reduced price. Many older iPads no longer receive software updates, and they’ll feel quite slow compared to your phone and computer.

If you are price-conscious, peruse our guides to the Best Tablets and Best Android Tablets for ideas. For those shopping for a child, our Best Kids’ Tablets guide has good options with kid-friendly features and parental controls.

Apple has always packed a little extra into its iPad Pro tablets compared to its base, Air, and Mini models. It’s still totally fine to buy the iPad Pro M4, iPad Pro M2, or iPad Pro M1 if you can find them, but make sure you’re not paying close to MSRP. If it’s close to what you would pay for a brand-new M5 iPad Pro, the older model isn’t worth the small savings. If you can snag a good deal on one of the aforementioned slightly dated models, you’ll get a powerful machine that can match the current Pro slates in many ways; these devices also work with the first-generation Magic Keyboard with Trackpad.

Older iPad Pro models, like those from 2018 or 2020, aren’t worth buying (if they’re even available). They’re just too old.

Which iPads Support Apple Intelligence and Siri AI?

Apple Intelligence is a suite of artificial intelligence features available in select hardware across iPhone, iPad, and Mac devices. We break down many of the new Apple Intelligence features here if you’d like to learn more. Unfortunately, it’s not available on all iPad models. The following iPads support Apple Intelligence:

Apple has also introduced the more advanced Siri AI, as part of iPadOS 27. Some Siri AI features (such as the more expressive Siri voice or live translation) require at least 12 GB of memory to run completely on-device. This limits which iPad models (and configurations) can support all the Siri AI features. The latest iPad Mini and iPad Air support it. Older iPad Pros also support it, but only in the configurations with more storage. Here are the compatible iPads:

By design, the iPad is primarily for entertainment and content consumption, while the MacBook is for work, school, and content creation. You can use both devices for any of those purposes, but each iPad type shines brightest in its intended uses. That’s why iPads work so well for kids, retired folks, and travelers.

The iPad Pro, however, may be an outlier, as it is truly a pro-level content-creation device. Depending on the task, it could be a laptop replacement. Some power users have a use for its tandem OLED display, faster ports, and M5-level graphics, but it’s a much smaller niche.

In March 2025, the long-awaited iPad (11th-generation, A16) refresh came. It had been three years since Apple released a new base iPad, and the update brought many modern features. It used the same modern design as its predecessor, complete with uniform bezels around a slightly larger 11-inch LCD. There’s a USB-C port, a Touch ID sensor integrated into the power button, a 12-megapixel rear camera, and a 12-MP selfie camera in landscape mode (with support for Center Stage). The cellular model supports 5G networks.

The A16 chip (the same one as in the iPhone 14 Pro, iPhone 15, and iPhone 15 Plus) is plenty powerful, but it doesn’t have enough RAM to support Apple Intelligence, making it the only iPad in the lineup without access to Apple’s AI features. (You might see that as a perk.) You’ll get 128 GB of storage, up from 64 GB, with the option to increase it to 512 GB.

This iPad doesn’t have a fully laminated display, meaning there’s an air gap between the screen and the glass. Because this gap can make Apple Pencil interactions feel a smidge imprecise, it isn’t our pick for artists. You also won’t get second-gen Apple Pencil support; this iPad only works with the first-gen stylus or the USB-C Apple Pencil.

Even though it’s over a year old, the iPad is still the best tablet for most people. Even at its new $449 price, it’s a well-rounded slate that can easily tackle most tablet-appropriate tasks, like gaming, streaming movies and TV, and reading. If you connect a keyboard or the Magic Keyboard Folio, you may be able to use it for work, too (depending on what you do). It’s also perfect for folks who want an entertainment device bigger than a phone, but not as small as an iPad Mini. —Julian Chokkattu

The classic iPad was never meant for serious work—at least not in the conventional sense. But over the past decade, iPad software and accessory upgrades have made the devices more capable of tasks formerly reserved for a laptop or desktop. This evolution started with cursor and mouse support, then came the Magic Keyboard with a full trackpad and an extra port. Then, iPadOS 26 brought windowed multitasking. For students or anyone else who wants to use those aforementioned features to do more with their iPad, I recommend the M4 iPad Air (7/10, WIRED Recommends).

Contrary to its name, the iPad Air isn’t much thinner or lighter than the base iPad. Instead, the iPad Air lets you use an iPad as your primary work or school device. It offers significantly better performance than the base iPad for tasks like video editing or gaming, and it comes in a 13-inch screen size for a larger canvas to work with. Whether you use your iPad for art in Procreate or just write emails, you’ll appreciate that extra screen space. The 12 GB of RAM means you can keep many apps open, and you can even dabble in Apple Intelligence or other on-device AI tasks.

Another reason to get the iPad Air is its compatible accessories. The large Magic Keyboard that comes with the 13-inch iPad Air offers a spacious typing experience. The key spacing and keycap size are close to those of a MacBook’s keyboard. The trackpad and palm rests are still a little small, but the keyboard itself is great.

With such a light update in this latest model, and given the recent price increase, you might also consider buying last year’s M3 iPad Air instead. It’s a valid sentiment; you can buy the M3 iPad Air 11-inch for $499 right now, which is a great deal, especially since that chip is plenty powerful for iPad Air use cases.

The 2026 iPad Mini upgrades nearly every aspect of Apple’s smallest tablet, making it feel increasingly like a “Pro” tablet. Compared to the previous 2024 model, the screen is bigger, it is thinner, and it has more memory. It even has a feature never before seen on an iPad: IP67 water resistance. It’s begging to be taken poolside or to the beach. Another point in its favor for using this iPad Mini outside is the display. It’s now OLED and offers the nano-texture display option, which dramatically reduces glare and reflections. It costs an extra $100, but it’s well worth it.

But the price is definitely of concern. It now starts at $699, up $130 from before. All the features, performance, and tech upgrades are worth that price, but the form factor feels limiting. This tablet size is better suited for leisurely activities like reading, gaming, or casual browsing. The use case for an expensive, super high-end tablet of this size that can handle serious workflows feels far more niche. I have an inkling that if you are integrating an iPad into your creative process, you will want a larger display—and probably one that can be connected to a Magic Keyboard, like the Air or Pro variants.

If you can afford it and just want the best small tablet ever made, the iPad Mini is as good as it gets. And fortunately, you can still buy the previous-gen iPad Mini for around $599. For the price, it’s a solid option for anyone who wants a simple device to play games, browse, and read books.

Most people don’t even need to consider the M5 iPad Pro (9/10, WIRED Recommends). It starts at $1,199 and $1,499 for the 11- and 13-inch models, respectively, and those prices don’t include Apple’s Magic Keyboard, which adds $350. You can buy a very powerful MacBook for that kind of cash. Don’t get me wrong, this tablet is exceptional. The tandem OLED screen is one of the best in the business: it’s bright, sharp, and colorful; it has excellent black levels; and it’s the only iPad with a 120-Hz screen. The speakers sound fantastic, and even the webcam is pretty sharp. You get all of that in an incredibly thin design, though it’s not much different from the M4 iPad Pro.

The M5 chip powering this iPad offers a small performance boost over the M4, though most folks won’t notice it in general apps. If you frequently use AI apps like Draw Things or graphically intense apps like DaVinci Resolve, that’s where the speed improvements matter. The iPad Pro is for people who use these kinds of powerful apps for content creation and work, whether that’s creating 3D objects in Blender or producing music. You still have to decide whether you want the benefit of a tablet experience or, say, the MacBook Pro M5, which can deliver slightly better performance and is overall a better machine for getting stuff done.

The latest iPadOS windowed-apps multitasking system is a great benefit that makes this iPad Pro more functional as a computer. While this is available on all iPads running iPadOS 26 and iPadOS 27, it shines best on the biggest iPad models, which are the 13-inch iPad Pro and iPad Air. When in doubt, try the iPad Air first; if that feels more than sufficient, you'll save yourself a wad of cash. —Julian Chokkattu

The Apple Pencil is a must-have for any iPad owner who takes a lot of notes or likes to draw. (You can even use it to sign documents.) iPadOS also supports handwriting in certain search fields and apps (like Notes), so you don’t need to switch to the keyboard every time you want to type. Apple debuted a new Pencil in 2024, so you now have four options to choose from, depending on the iPad you have. Here are the key differences:

Apple Pencil (USB-C) for $79: The USB-C Apple Pencil is a bit perplexing. It launched in 2023 to support the switch to USB-C across iPads, but it lacks some features. It doesn’t offer pressure sensitivity, so it’s better suited for note-takers than artists. It can attach magnetically to the sides of an iPad, so you don’t need to find a place to store it. However, it doesn’t support wireless charging, so you’ll have to pair and charge it via USB-C. It supports Hover Mode, which allows you to preview what will happen before touching the display. This feature is exclusive to select iPad Pro, iPad Air, and iPad Mini models. The USB-C Apple Pencil is compatible with iPad (10th gen and A16), iPad Air (fourth gen, fifth gen, M2, M3, and M4), iPad Mini (sixth gen and later), iPad Pro 11-inch (first gen and later, including M4), and iPad Pro 12.9-inch (third gen and later, including M4 and M5).

Apple Pencil Pro for $99: Apple’s Pro Pencil is the most feature-packed. It looks identical to the second-generation model, complete with a matte finish, but it also has a few new tricks. A “squeeze” feature opens up a tool palette, and a new haptic engine gives feedback as you squeeze. A built-in gyroscope lets you change the orientation of the tools as you twist them (for finer control), and it supports Apple’s Find My network to track the stylus if you lose it. It’s only compatible with the iPad Air (M2, M3, and M4), iPad Pro (M4 and M5), and 8th-gen iPad Mini. (C’mon, why isn’t it backward-compatible?)

Apple Pencil (first gen) for $99: The first-generation Pencil is quite old (it came out in 2015!) but still works well—as long as you have an older, compatible iPad. It offers both pressure sensitivity (lines become thicker as you press down harder on the screen) and tilt sensitivity (for shading). It has a glossy finish instead of matte (if that matters), and it is also cylindrical, meaning it will roll off surfaces. It doesn’t feature magnets, so you can’t stick it to the tablet for safekeeping. It has a Lightning connector, so to charge it, you’ll have to remove the cap and plug it into the iPad’s charging port. If your iPad has a USB-C port, you can buy an adapter. However, it’s only compatible with the iPad (sixth gen and later), iPad Air (third gen), iPad Mini (fifth gen), iPad Pro 12.9-inch (first and second gen), iPad Pro 10.5-inch, and iPad Pro 9.7-inch.

Apple Pencil (second-gen) for $129: The second-generation Apple Pencil has the same capabilities as the first-gen and USB-C versions, plus wireless pairing and charging (it’ll charge when it magnetically sticks to the top edge of supported iPads). It also adds a double-tap feature that lets you quickly switch between tools by tapping on the Apple Pencil twice. It’s compatible with the iPad Air (fourth and fifth gen), iPad Mini (sixth gen), iPad Pro 11-inch (first gen and later), and the 12.9-inch (third gen and later). Unfortunately, Apple officially discontinued it, so newer iPads—including the iPad Air (M2, M3, and M4) or iPad Pro (M4 and M5)—don’t support it.

Accessories for the iPad are endless. Below, I’ve highlighted some of my favorites to round out your tablet experience. You can find even more options in our Best iPad Accessories guide.

Twelve South Curve Mini for $50: An iPad can be a great tool to use alongside your MacBook or PC, but it needs a proper stand with plenty of adjustability. The Curve Mini is simple, but it’s stable and attractive, especially in this Dune color that I sampled.

Apple Magic Keyboard for $200: It’s expensive, yes. But it’s worth every dollar. It really does transform the iPad Air into a capable laptop replacement, with a keyboard and trackpad that’s every bit as good as a high-end laptop. It also adds a secondary USB-C port. The magnetic design does make it quite heavy, but I love how easy it is to pick up the iPad right off the case. There’s also a Magic Keyboard available for both versions of the iPad Pro, though they’re more expensive.

Zugu Case for $50+: This is my favorite folio case for the iPad for multiple reasons: It’s durable (with a rigid bumper), has a magnetic cover that stays shut, and has a flap that lets you position the screen at eight angles. The case is magnetic, so you can stick it securely to the fridge. It’s also reasonably priced, comes in an array of colors, and has a spot for your Apple Pencil.

Satechi M1 Wireless Mouse for $30: We’re already big fans of Satechi’s accessories at WIRED, and this mouse didn’t disappoint. It has a comfortable ergonomic design, a sleek aluminum finish, and smooth scrolling. It also has great battery life, thanks to its built-in lithium-ion battery. I’ve been using it for the past four months and have yet to charge it.

Mageasy CoverBuddy Case (iPad Pro) for $70: The CoverBuddy lets you magnetically connect your iPad to Apple’s Magic Keyboard case without removing the case each time. It feels durable and doesn’t add too much bulk to the iPad. There’s also a slot for the Apple Pencil Pro or the USB-C version. The company also offers the CoverBuddy Lite for the iPad Air (M2).

Logitech Combo Touch (10th-gen) for $260: The Combo Touch (8/10, WIRED Recommends) comes with a built-in keyboard, trackpad, and kickstand, making it ideal for getting work done on your iPad. You can also easily remove the detachable keyboard when you don’t need it. The Combo Touch connects via Apple’s Smart Connector, so you never need to tinker with Bluetooth or charge it. It’s also available for the iPad Pro (M4) and M5 (although these add a bit of weight) and the iPad Air (M2).

Casetify Impact Screen Protector for $56: If you’re worried about damaging your iPad screen, I recommend this Casetify protector. It’s super thin, has excellent touch sensitivity, and is mostly fingerprint-resistant (I’ve wiped off some smudges here and there). It’s painless to apply—the company supplies a microfiber cloth, a de-dusting sticker, and wet and dry wipes.

Paperlike Charcoal Folio Case for $70: Paperlike is known for its screen protector, but the company also offers a great case. It’s designed to feel like a sketchbook, complete with a polyester fabric cover that feels lightweight and high-quality. You can also prop up your iPad at two levels. This case doesn’t include an Apple Pencil slot, but a large flap closure keeps it from falling out. I tested the case using the iPad Air, but it’s also available for the iPad Pro (both sizes).

Twelve South HoverBar Duo 2.0 for $80: The HoverBar serves two purposes. You can mount it to the side of your bed, kitchen counter, or shelf (for comfortable, hands-free viewing), or use the included stand at your desk. With the second-gen version, you can now remove the arm from the clamp and attach it directly to the stand, making it easier to swap between the two modes.

#414. Fossil CEO says turnaround is ahead of plan as sales return to growth

Fossil Group posted quarterly results on Tuesday that could point to early signs of a turnaround for the legacy watchmaker.

The company, which has struggled to compete with new gadgets in the years since its sales and stock price peaked in the 2010s, has been quietly undertaking a comeback strategy under CEO Franco Fogliato. The company's fiscal third-quarter earnings indicate the strategy is beginning to bear fruit.

The company said Tuesday that it returned to profitability in the third quarter, posting operating income of $25.3 million, compared with an operating loss of $21.7 million last year. It also returned to sales growth, with net sales up 0.4% to $271.2 million. The company last reported a year-over-year sales increase in the first fiscal quarter of 2022.

"We're excited about improving dramatically the profitability of the company through a very strong and disciplined management of full-price sales as well as cost management," Fogliato told CNBC in an exclusive interview.

For the quarter ended Oct. 3, Fossil reported net income of $11.7 million, or 19 cents per share, compared with a net loss of $39.9 million, or 76 cents per share, the year prior. The company also said its operating expenses fell 11.1% to $137 million. The company last posted quarterly net income in the third fiscal quarter of 2022.

With the signs of growth, the retailer also raised its full-year outlook, guiding for full-year net sales of between $980 million and $990 million, which would mark a slight decline from just over $1 billion in net sales in the prior year. That compares with a previous expectation of net sales falling between 3% and 5%. Fossil also said it's expecting positive free cash flow by the end of the year.

Fogliato said Fossil had previously not expected sales growth for another year and a half.

The stronger quarter comes after the company saw slumping sales and financial losses over the past few years. It struggled with the rise of smartwatches, mounting debt and the decline in mall foot traffic.

Fossil stock has lost the vast majority of its value since the early-to-mid 2010s, and despite a recent surge, was trading under $8 a share on Tuesday.

To rightsize the business, Fogliato, who joined Fossil in 2024, said he focused on closing stores, moving away from promotions to full-price sales, improving marketing and broadening Fossil's product range. That meant shrinking the business to a sustainable baseline before trying to grow it.

The company saw a 5.9% decrease in direct-to-consumer sales for its third quarter, which Fogliato attributed to the company's "aggressive" downsizing.

"We simplified a lot of the business doing this," Fogliato said, adding that customers responded well when the company moved away from markdowns. "People tell me they are excited about what they are seeing in the marketplace now."

Fossil added that its key growth engine for the quarter was Asia, where net sales grew 15.1%. Fogliato said much of that growth was led by the India market.

At the same time, growing internationally is only part of the watchmaker's strategy as it tries to improve its performance in its home country.

"Priority number one is to grow our home market, and we're doing that," he said. "As retail is settling into a smaller, more profitable base, ready to grow again, we're entering 2027 with plenty of opportunities."

Fogliato said one key to growth was not trying to keep up with the smartwatch trend. While trying to make gains in the smartwatch space initially hampered Fossil's performance, it's now investing in analog watches again.

Smartwatch sales came in under $1 million for the quarter, but sales for traditional watches — which make up around 86% of Fossil's sales — grew 6.4%.

"Fossil is literally having a comeback, a strong comeback, a momentum, and we can see this," Fogliato said. "And it comes not only through the numbers — it comes with the excitement we see in the stores, and we think we're just at the beginning. We have so much work to do."

#413. GTA 6 preorders are live: Where to buy for PS5 and Xbox

It's the last call for your "we got X before GTA 6" memes. Preorders for Grand Theft Auto 6 are live ahead of its Nov. 19 release. The highly anticipated game is available in two editions for PlayStation 5 or Xbox Series X/S: an $80 standard edition and a $100 Ultimate Edition. Both come with a slew of preorder bonuses if you purchase them before launch.

#408. Measles is surging in the U.S. — and outbreaks can cost millions

Measles outbreaks aren't just a public health threat. They can also be a financial burden on the U.S. — and the cost could only deepen as the once-eliminated disease spreads more widely.

The U.S. is expected to lose its official measles elimination status following a review by the Pan American Health Organization this week, after cases ratcheted to recent highs this year.

More than 5,100 measles cases have been reported nationwide in 2026 as of early November, marking the country's worst outbreak in more than three decades. Eight deaths associated with the disease have been confirmed by states so far in 2026: six in Pennsylvania, one in New York and one in Wisconsin.

Based on this year's case count, a new tool developed by researchers at Johns Hopkins University estimates an economic cost of nearly $83 million for the outbreaks. That total includes $56 million in public health response costs, almost $25 million in lost productivity and more than $1.8 million in direct medical expenses.

The tool also estimates that 2025 measles outbreaks in the U.S. — which involved 2,289 confirmed cases nationwide – carried an economic cost of more than $37 million.

The costs extend beyond treating people who become sick, accounting for the resources health departments spend investigating and containing outbreaks, as well as wages lost when people have to miss work. The tool uses more recent, state-specific cost and financing data than an initial study by the researchers, which relied on historical outbreak costs.

The estimates from the tool are not a definitive accounting of what the outbreaks actually cost. But it illustrates the hefty bill tied to the spread of a disease that can be prevented through vaccination, and the potential economic consequences of falling childhood immunization rates. CNBC previously reported on how the return of measles in the U.S. exposes weaknesses in the nation's public health system that raise concerns about the return of other eliminated diseases.

"We're starting to see a whole bunch of people who decided not to get vaccinated as kids or have never gotten exposed to measles because they benefited from herd immunity, who now make it into adulthood and risk potentially getting quite seriously ill and requiring very significant medical expenses, or dying at the hospital," Bryan Patenaude, a health economist at the International Vaccine Access Center at the Johns Hopkins Bloomberg School of Public Health, who helped develop the tool, said in an interview.

Measles outbreaks can trigger a costly public health response, even when many people who are exposed never become sick.

When a case is identified, public health officials must investigate the infection, identify and notify people who may have been exposed, coordinate with hospitals and schools, and sometimes organize vaccination campaigns to prevent further spread. Those efforts can divert staff and resources from other public health priorities.

Costs could differ heavily depending on the state.

Johns Hopkins' data includes national averages alongside state-specific inputs, including inpatient hospital costs and wages from government data or trusted health policy sources. In New York, for example, the average inpatient hospital stay is roughly $4,200 a day, while the minimum wage is $16 per hour.

The tool estimates that the 48 cases reported by New York in 2025 carried an estimated $1.1 million in costs, including nearly $749,000 in public health responses, $333,000 in productivity losses and roughly $24,500 in direct medical expenses.

Meanwhile, the 803 cases in Texas, the epicenter of the largest U.S. measles outbreak in 2025, carried estimated costs of nearly $13 million, according to the tool. That includes $9 million in public health costs, $4 million in productivity losses and around $288,000 in direct medical expenses.

The tool builds on a study published by Patenaude and other researchers in August, which examined the costs of measles outbreaks in the U.S. The researchers reviewed published studies and government reports to compile historical estimates of medical expenses and public health response costs across 18 states.

The paper estimated that outbreaks carried a historical average cost of nearly $59,000 per case, just from the public health response. The researchers also estimated how costs scale with outbreak size, separating fixed up-front response expenses from the additional costs associated with each new case.

"A lot of this is incurred up front, and that's because of the human labor that's involved in contact tracing and all of the different actors that sort of need to be mobilized to deal with the fact that there's an outbreak," Patenaude told CNBC. "It's a largely labor-driven cost."

That model estimated roughly $298,000 in fixed costs and about $16,300 for each additional case. But it relied on historical data, including a particularly large 2019 outbreak in New York City, where contact-tracing labor costs were high. The researchers also excluded productivity losses from that model because most of the historical studies they reviewed did not consistently account for them.

The economic burden of measles extends beyond the costs of treating infected patients and organizing a public health response.

People who are exposed to measles may have to quarantine, even if they never develop symptoms. Parents can also be forced to miss work to care for sick or exposed children, while workers who contract the disease may lose wages during their recovery.

A 2025 study by Joey Mattingly, a pharmacist and health economist at the University of Utah, used published epidemiological and cost data to model the potential economic burden of a hypothetical 1,500-case outbreak. His analysis included direct medical expenses, public health spending and lost productivity, and examined how the estimates changed under different assumptions, including hospitalization rates.

The study estimated that the hypothetical outbreak could cost about $90 million, including roughly $26 million in productivity losses.

Those indirect costs can add up quickly because measles is highly contagious and a single infected person can expose dozens of others. Those contacts may need to quarantine or miss work while health officials determine whether they have been infected, creating costs even when they never develop the disease.

Mattingly estimated that each person who is quarantined could amount to roughly $300 in lost productivity alone.

If a single measles case exposes 40 to 50 people who then need to quarantine, that could translate into an estimated $12,000 to $15,000 in lost productivity, even if none of those contacts become sick.

That ripple effect is one reason Mattingly argues that the economic costs of outbreaks can quickly outweigh the investment required to vaccinate people against measles.

"Even if you don't get the infection, the contact costs you," he said. "And so it's just showing that there's this multiplication or exponential growth on the economic side that happens."

The financial burden can also fall unevenly across communities. While the costs of responding to an outbreak may be shared among local, state and federal agencies, Mattingly said the impact is often felt directly by local health departments.

"It's not like your health department is sitting around waiting for measles outbreaks," he said. "They're working, doing all these other things, and imagine having to just drop what you're doing because there's been a huge outbreak in the community."

Patenaude said the findings could help state and local health departments better anticipate the resources needed to contain outbreaks — particularly as measles cases continue to rise.

"It's not super expensive to treat and deal with a single case of measles compared to a single case of cancer," he said. "But if you have these outbreaks popping up and you're having to constantly divert resources that could be spent elsewhere by state and local health departments on measles, then you're going to wind up spending a lot of money very quickly."

#405. A New Benchmark for De-Aging Comes From Slowly Turning Back Cells’ Clocks

Scientists have long known how to make cells younger—at least in the sense that adult cells can be converted into pluripotent stem cells very similar to those found in embryos. While this capability has been foundational for biological research, these methods aren’t viable for anti-aging technologies, for a few reasons.

“You don't want to have a pluripotent cell in your body, because it can become all types of cells,” says Oliver Brüstle, a professor who studies stem cells at Germany’s Bonn University.

Additionally, the few key genes involved in the stem cell conversion process can drive the formation of cancer if left active. That’s why Brüstle and his team have looked for other ways of de-aging cells without going through a pluripotent state. In their recent study published in Aging Cell, the researchers started with blood cells from people of various ages, including an infant and a 101-year-old donor.

They guided these cells into a younger state but not all the way toward becoming embryonic-like stem cells. And as this transformation progressed, the chemical signals in the DNA that help estimate a cell’s age also changed. In less than two months of reprogramming, one biomarker in particular was set back as far as 60 years.

Aging continued to reverse even when the scientists drastically reduced the cells’ ability to multiply. Therefore, the apparent rejuvenation can’t be explained solely by the fact that the cells were dividing and producing new cells over and over again. However, the mechanism that did erase these signs of aging is still unclear.

“The hope is that you actually can do this systemically in the entire animal,” Brüstle says. “But if you think about applying it in a patient, then you want to be sure this is not a lab experiment, so it's about finding the mechanism.”

The researchers also shifted the blood cells specifically into a kind of cell that produces neurons. One caveat is that blood cells and neurons age on different timelines, so the extent of the reversal could be exaggerated by the use of a different measuring stick. The authors acknowledge this limitation in their paper, though this discrepancy couldn’t account for all the age-associated biomarkers that reversed course.

Blood cells getting brainy also would not be a desirable result if you gave a patient a de-aging drug, but it would be useful for other regenerative medicine purposes. However, what Brüstle sees as one of the most exciting outcomes of the study is the information gleaned about the process of de-aging.

Usually, making stem cells is quick. That’s useful for many experiments, but it doesn’t give researchers the chance to study cells as they shed the markers of age. In this recent study, since the scientists were able to slowly turn back the cells’ biological clocks, they could track each step of de-aging. “Now we can actually try to dissect the process much more easily than having an instantaneous event,” Brüstle says.

Scientists began converting adult cells back into stem cells two decades ago, but we’ve known very little about how the subsequent de-aging occurs. This means when clinical researchers test potential medical interventions for age-related conditions, they haven’t always had a reliable measure to compare against.

“Now we have a model” Brüstle says. “You can work along this, and now you can probe a zillion things. What impacts the process? This will lead you to the mechanisms. That’s our hope.”

#404. JetBlue unveils new first-class seats to bring back profits

JetBlue Airways unveiled its new domestic first-class seats Wednesday as more of the country's carriers bet on the premium travel boom to make them profitable again.

JetBlue announced it would offer a domestic first class — BlueFirst — in December 2024 and plans to start flying aircraft with the new seats and full on-board product next month. The carrier showed off the new cabin at its home hub at John F. Kennedy International Airport on Wednesday. Retrofits will continue during 2027, CEO Joanna Geraghty told CNBC late last month.

JetBlue will have 12 of the seats on its Airbus A320 fleet and 30 extra-legroom seats, down from 42 of those options previously. Because the airplanes don't have ovens, JetBlue said it is offering an array of cold food options like sesame noodles, pastrami sandwiches and tortellini salad, though they vary by route.

The rollout comes as the New York airline tries to stabilize after last posting a profit in 2019. Last month, JetBlue executives sought to calm investors after shares in the airline hit a record low following a report that bondholders had contracted restructuring advisors.

"We have no intention, no plans to file for bankruptcy. We are not engaged with any restructuring company," Geraghty told CNBC in an interview on Oct. 27.

Geraghty told CNBC's Phil LeBeau on Wednesday that JetBlue has "met with a few of the creditors over the last couple of weeks as part of ... normal investor engagement." She said the company made it clear to bondholders that JetBlue doesn't need to reorganize.

The airline's shares are up more than 40% this month, more than other passenger carriers, as of midday Wednesday, though they are still off about 11% so far this year.

JetBlue is betting that resilient travel demand, especially for roomier seats will help fuel its turnaround plan, which aims to get the carrier to an operating profit next year and $1 in adjusted earnings per share in 2028.

Airline executives have said they expect fares to stay elevated next year as they grapple with higher costs, including but not limited to fuel, such as labor and airport fees. Geraghty said Wednesday that even if fuel prices moderate next year, "I don't think fares are going to fall as much as people expect."

Airlines large and small are racing to outfit their cabins with roomier, more expensive seating options to help cover ever-higher costs, from jet fuel to labor to airport operation fees.

For example, a ticket in the new recliner first-class seats between Kennedy airport and Fort Lauderdale-Hollywood International Airport in Florida is going for $847 roundtrip in the first week of March while standard economy is $446.

On Tuesday, budget carrier Frontier Airlines unveiled its new first-class seats, which will take up the first two rows, and said it would outfit its Airbus fleet with premium seating that has extra legroom and in-seat power through row eight. The airline will take out a row of seating on its planes to make the pricier ones fit, Chief Marketing Officer Bobby Schroeter told CNBC in an interview Monday.

JetBlue last month reported that it ended the third quarter with $2.2 billion in liquidity and that its unencumbered asset base is now $7 billion, including $2.8 billion in aircraft and engines.

JetBlue had total debt of $8.4 billion as of Sept. 30, the carrier said in its earnings filing.

Geraghty told CNBC last month there is a "fundamental disconnect between the narrative in the bond market and what our financial results actually demonstrate."

When asked whether JetBlue's eye on keeping costs down includes furloughs, layoffs or buyouts, Geraghty last month said "absolutely not."

#403. Moon phase today: What the Moon will look like on November 11, 2026

The Moon is now a little further into its Waxing Crescent phase, with a small amount of its surface illuminated by the Sun. It should be slightly easier to spot tonight, although there's still not much to see in the way of lunar features.

As of Wednesday, Nov. 11, the NASA's Daily Moon Guide tracker tells us the Moon is in the Waxing Crescent phase, with 4% of its surface visible.

The next Full Moon will take place on November 24.

The Moon takes approximately 29.5 days to complete one full cycle. As it orbits Earth, the amount of sunlight reflecting off its surface that we can see changes, creating the different phases. This takes us from the New Moon to the Full Moon and back again, before the cycle begins once more, according to NASA.

New Moon - The Moon is between Earth and the sun, so the side we see is dark (in other words, it's invisible to the eye).

Waxing Crescent - A small sliver of light appears on the right side (Northern Hemisphere).

First Quarter - Half of the Moon is lit on the right side. It looks like a half-Moon.

Waxing Gibbous - More than half is lit up, but it's not quite full yet.

Full Moon - The whole face of the Moon is illuminated and fully visible.

Waning Gibbous - The Moon starts losing light on the right side. (Northern Hemisphere)

Third Quarter (or Last Quarter) - Another half-Moon, but now the left side is lit.

Waning Crescent - A thin sliver of light remains on the left side before going dark again.

Lois Mackenzie is a freelance reporter at Mashable. Over the years she has written for many publications, covering everything from the local news to the best pair of running shoes. You can find bylines in publications including Fit&Well, Metro, and Coach magazine, usually covering deals on everything from earbuds to TVs, or guides on how to beat your half marathon time.

Lois also holds a Master's degree in Digital Journalism from Strathclyde University and obtained a Master of Arts in English Literature at the University of Aberdeen.

#401. Chelsea searches for new manager as Premier League breaks record for most sackings in a single season

It was just six months ago that Graham Potter was deemed the man to usher in a fresh, exciting era at Chelsea.

The club’s new ownership group, led by American businessman Todd Boehly, had enticed Potter away from Brighton with the promise of managing one of the most lucrative teams in world soccer.

More than $600 million was spent on bringing big-name players to the club and Potter, one of the most exciting young managers in the Premier League, was to mold them into a winning machine.

Chelsea has spent over $600 million on new signings. Here's why that's significant

But, on Sunday, the club announced that it had sacked its manager after an underwhelming spell in charge.

Potter managed to win just 10 of the 28 league matches this season and leaves the team 11th in the Premier League table.

In recent weeks, players had looked a shadow of their former selves and many fans had lost confidence that Potter’s plan for the club could ever work.

Eventually, after previously sticking by its manager, Blues ownership agreed.

“On behalf of everyone at the club, we want to thank Graham sincerely for his contribution to Chelsea,” the club’s co-owners Todd Boehly and Behdad Eghbali said in a statement.

“We have the highest degree of respect for Graham as a coach and as a person. He has always conducted himself with professionalism and integrity and we are all disappointed in this outcome.”

In his previous role as Brighton manager, Potter was given time to stamp his identity on the squad.

The club was punching above its weight financially and, as a result, carefully selected new signings which would compliment Potter’s style of play.

The approach paid off as the team became an established Premier League outfit which has gone on to greater things this season – the Seagulls currently sit sixth in the league and in the UEFA Conference League qualification spot.

At Chelsea, it seemed the complete opposite.

As was the case under previous owner Roman Abramovich, the club expected instant results and used its financial weight to sign a number of high-profile names, including the likes of Enzo Fernández and Mykhailo Mudryk.

Despite his impressive CV, Potter had never managed superstars and suddenly found himself in a changing room with multiple.

The scatter gun approach to recruitment also left the team lacking balance and organization with Potter failing to get a tune out of a team busting with talent.

In truth, many had raised an eyebrow when Potter, inexperienced at the very top level, was chosen to replace Thomas Tuchel as manager.

Despite falling out of favor with fans by the end of his tenure, Tuchel had guided Chelsea to the Champions League title in 2021 and is still considered by many as one of the best coaches in the world.

The German has gone on to sign with Bavarian giant Bayern Munich through the 2024/25 season and recently told reporters he needed “quite a bit of time to distance himself from Chelsea” because leaving the club had hurt him.

Bayern Munich sacks manager Julian Nagelsmann, appoints Thomas Tuchel

“Todd [Boehly] said he would be different to Roman [Abramovich],” former Liverpool player and current pundit Jamie Carragher wrote on Twitter.

“I feel for Graham Potter, but it was inevitable. You don’t change Tuchel for Potter. Ridiculous decision to start with.”

With 10 games left in the league and a Champions League quarterfinal against Real Madrid on the horizon, Chelsea still has plenty to play for.

Assistant coach Bruno Saltor will take over on an interim basis, but Boehly and his team must now look for a long-term replacement capable – and willing – to bring order to Chelsea’s chaos.

Potter’s sacking is yet another sign of just how transient managers can be in the top-flight of English football, especially when results aren’t instantaneous.

There has now been a record 12 sackings in the Premier League so far this season, with Potter leaving his position the same day Leicester boss Brendan Rodgers was sacked from his.

The previous record was 10 manager changes in a single season, set in four other campaigns, most recently in 2017/18.

#400. A 13-Year-Old Asked AI About Suicide on Her School Laptop. It Didn’t Get Flagged for Weeks

A 13-year-old girl spent three months using her school-issued laptop to discuss depression, research suicide, and plan her funeral before self-harm-monitoring software finally prompted school officials to alert her parents, who intervened and stopped her, according to a lawsuit filed this month by her parents.

The lawsuit alleges that Google and parent company Alphabet were negligent in their rollout of a Gemini model, claiming that the AI chatbot “coached” the North Carolina teenager to consider taking her own life. The case, which was previously reported by The New York Times, is one of several that allege harm from AI chatbot use, including claims of delusions and suicide. But it also illustrates the limitations of surveillance tools that thousands of schools have adopted in recent years, which are ostensibly designed to protect students from themselves when browsing and chatting online.

Andrew Liddell, a lawyer for the family, tells WIRED that, according to information the school provided to the family, the alert went to the personal phone of a former counselor who had left the school nine months before. The ex-employee was diligent in passing the alert along to current staff, who then notified the girl’s mother, he says.

As classrooms continue to rely on Google’s low-cost offerings, including Gemini, their ability to keep up with the potential risks is front and center. Just last week, parent and teacher groups across the country decried Google’s new requirement that schools must turn on generative AI features if they want to use Search. US senator Ed Markey cited Emma’s lawsuit in a letter to Google demanding the company let schools disable AI Mode and AI Overviews, writing that “Google is recklessly forcing AI on schools and treating kids like collateral damage.”

According to the lawsuit, the North Carolina girl, identified as Emma Doe, descended into “a severe psychological crisis” after Google Search routed her to Gemini on her school-issued Google Chromebook. Though Emma’s school had blocked the stand-alone Gemini website, the lawsuit alleges that she was steered into interacting with the chatbot through AI Mode, which lets people ask follow-up questions about a query or begin an unrelated conversation.

Emma queried AI Mode about mental health issues starting last December. She asked about “severe depression” and “reasons to live,” and by late January she was asking about suicide, per the lawsuit. Over the course of two days around Valentine’s Day, she began writing a 1,500-word eulogy for herself with help from AI Mode in a Google Doc titled “Funeral.” The lawsuit says she worked on the document for more than four weeks while continuing to interact with the Gemini tool thousands of times.

After school on St. Patrick's Day, she deleted most of the file and restarted. But Liddell says the themes and thoughts the girl expressed didn't materially change. Around 7:30 pm, her mother allegedly received a message from the school indicating that her daughter was in “imminent danger,” the lawsuit states. Her mother used her phone to log in to Emma’s school account and “watched her daughter compose a suicide note in real time,” according to the lawsuit.

Google spokesperson José Castañeda says that the company is seriously “reviewing the claims in the lawsuit” and that its systems, including AI Mode, “aim to prevent users from engaging in harmful conversation.”

"We’ve worked extensively with medical professionals and academic experts to build features that help young people find critical support and trusted resources when they need it,” Castañeda says.

Liddell tells WIRED that Emma’s school only learned about her mental health crisis because it used at least one third-party student-activity-monitoring system. Citing information that was allegedly previously provided to the school’s parents, he says these may have included products from the companies GoGuardian and Gaggle. Liddell says that the family lacks a definitive answer because the school’s “window of cooperation” with them was brief.

The screening tools generally work by capturing keystrokes or scanning files and using algorithms and human review to identify potentially concerning content. It’s unclear whether the school’s third-party screening tools monitor AI Mode interactions or, if they do, how well they actually work. GoGuardian wrote in a July 2025 blog post that its flagship product monitors conversations with AI chatbots, including Gemini, but it didn’t mention AI Mode specifically.

Though it’s unclear exactly how effective student surveillance tools are at protecting students, studies show that educators perceive them as helpful. A 2022 white paper from the EdWeek Research Center found that in a survey of 1,000 American K-12 educators, 96 percent of Gaggle users believed that it helped to “keep students safe from suicide and/or self-harm.” While the study was sponsored by Gaggle, the findings were similar for teachers who reported using alternatives.

The allegation that Emma’s escalating interactions with Gemini went unnoticed for almost two months, after she explicitly asked about “hanging, suffocation, and overdosing,” appears to indicate that chatbot conversations embedded in Google Search may be a blind spot.

Benjamin Boudreaux, a policy researcher at Rand, which studies topics like student surveillance, tells WIRED that he fears that some schools have rushed to use tools without considering their limits and proper processes to act on alerts.

He expects Emma’s lawsuit to spark further outcry from parents, teachers, and students for instructional opportunities free of AI and surveillance and full of empathy. “It’s really only through that human connection,” he says, “that we can address the root issues.”

If you or someone you know needs help, call 988 for free, 24-hour support from the National Suicide Prevention Lifeline. You can also text HOME to 741-741 for the Crisis Text Line. Outside the US, visit the International Association for Suicide Prevention for crisis centers around the world.

#399. American Airlines brings fresh food back to NY-Chicago first class for first time since 2019

American Airlines said Wednesday it's bringing small, fresh "bites" to first class on flights between its hubs in New York and Chicago, the carrier's latest effort to win over higher-spending customers.

The changes start this month. Customers will be able to pick between two options, which include prosciutto and melon or apple cinnamon chia pudding for breakfast and a Thai noodle salad or mezze plate for lunch and dinner, American said Wednesday.

American last offered fresh food for first class on these routes in 2019, a spokeswoman said. Currently, customers in first class on these flights get a smaller, packaged snack. Airlines largely have limited food options for shorter flights, even in first class.

American has fallen behind Delta Air Lines and United Airlines in profits and investments in premium options. The airline has been working to step up those premium offerings in recent years with new cabins, lounges and plans for seatback screens on its narrow-body fleets.
